  claims_for_R (12):
    2.1.C1 Shadow price of an LP constraint = objective gain per unit relaxation; zero if non-binding
    2.1.C2 Shadow-price / resource-allocation idea traces to Kantorovich and Koopmans
    2.1.C3 Satisficing (Simon): search for option clearing an aspiration level instead of optimum
    2.2.C1 Shapiro & Varian, Information Rules: high-fixed/low-marginal-cost info goods; competition pushes price toward MC, fixed cost unrecovered, so price to value (corrected v005, T'2)
    2.2.C2 Usage meters on inference/storage pass variable infra cost to the causing buyer (characterization)
    2.3.C1 Kydland & Prescott: optimal announced policy can become non-optimal after agents act (time inconsistency)
    2.3.C2 Coase (1972) conjecture: durable-goods monopolist without commitment drives price toward MC as buyers wait
    2.3.C3 Information Rules analyzes switching costs / lock-in at length
    2.3.C4 Buyers anticipating lock-in demand compensation at entry (standard switching-cost result)
    2.4.C1 Simon's bounded rationality treats heuristics as rational response to scarce attention
    2.4.C2 Cyert & March, A Behavioral Theory of the Firm: SOPs revised mainly when performance falls below aspiration
    2.4.C3 Annual prepay discount bounded by cost of capital and monthly churn (stated as reasoning)

  register_scan_hits_total 0
  diction: subheads<=2w 12/12 ok
  claims_for_R (11):
    3.1.C1 'Revealed preference' term/approach originates with Paul Samuelson (choices over introspective utility)
    3.1.C2 Van Westendorp price-sensitivity meter and conjoint are standard stated-preference WTP instruments
    3.1.C3 Simon: buyers search until an option clears an aspiration level (satisficing) — carried from 2.1.C3
    3.2.C1 Brandenburger & Stuart (value-based business strategy): total value = WTP minus supplier opportunity cost; under conditions they state, firm cannot capture more than its added value (qualifier added v008, T'7)
    3.2.C2 Teece (profiting from innovation): with weak appropriability, value flows to owners of complementary assets
    3.2.C3 Arrow information paradox: buyer cannot value information before disclosure; disclosure partly transfers it
    3.2.C4 Nash bargaining solution divides gains relative to disagreement payoffs
    3.3.C1 Alchian & Demsetz (team production): joint output hard to meter; organization arises to meter/reward, shirking incentive otherwise
    3.3.C2 Per-call meters induce batching/caching; seat prices induce license sharing (characterization)
    3.4.C1 Consumer surplus: concept developed by Jules Dupuit (1844); named and popularized by Alfred Marshall, Principles of Economics (1890) (corrected v008, T'5)
    3.4.C2 Behavioral evidence that buyers punish prices perceived unfair (pointer to Behavior; no specific study cited)

  register_scan_hits_total 0 (banned list incl. vibe, cosplay)
  diction: subheads<=2w 12/12 ok, Title Case
  claims_for_R (15):
    4.1.C1 Coase conjecture (carried from 2.3.C2): durable-goods seller without commitment faces buyers who wait for cuts
    4.1.C2 Reference dependence is a behavioral topic (pointer to Behavior; no study cited)
    4.2.C1 Cournot: firms choose quantities, price follows from total supply
    4.2.C2 Bertrand: identical products, constant MC, price competition drives price to MC with two sellers
    4.2.C3 Kreps & Scheinkman: capacity precommitment then price competition yields Cournot-like outcomes
    4.2.C4 Hotelling spatial competition: differentiated firms price above cost, margin depends on distance/transport cost
    4.2.C5 Price-matching promises can soften competition (standard IO result)
    4.3.C1 Rochet & Tirole: market two-sided when volume depends on price structure (allocation between sides), not only level
    4.3.C2 Armstrong: single-homing side is competed for; multi-homing side can be charged more (competitive bottlenecks)
    4.3.C3 Caillaud & Jullien: chicken-and-egg coordination problem in intermediation, resolved by subsidizing one side so it joins regardless of expectations about the other side, loss recovered on the other side (divide-and-conquer) (corrected v008, T'6)
    4.3.C4 Teece complementary assets (carried from 3.2.C2)
    4.4.C1 Schelling, The Strategy of Conflict: binding commitment can be an advantage
    4.4.C2 Dixit: capacity investment makes entry-deterrence threat credible
    4.4.C3 Milgrom & Roberts: limit pricing as signaling of incumbent cost
    4.4.C4 Folk theorems: wide range of outcomes sustainable in repeated games with sufficiently patient players

    5.1.C1 Savage (Foundations of Statistics): coherent preferences over acts imply acting as if holding subjective probabilities and maximizing expected utility; subjective probability lineage associated with Bayes
    5.1.C2 Knight (Risk, Uncertainty and Profit): risk = measurable/estimable distribution; uncertainty = no such basis
    5.1.C3 Bayes's rule: posterior proportional to prior times likelihood
    5.1.C4 Illustration arithmetic: prior 1/2, likelihood ratio 2 -> posterior 2/3 (invented numbers, labeled)
    5.2.C1 With n=20 trials, 3 vs 6 conversions is within chance range for many plausible rates (statistical characterization)
    5.2.C2 Spence (job-market signaling): costly action separates types when cost differs by type
    5.2.C3 Milgrom & Roberts limit pricing as signal (carried from 4.4.C3)

  6.1 words_body 870 in_band 1 | subs 3: Procedural Reason / Aspiration Levels / Reference Points | register_hits 0
  6.2 words_body 837 in_band 1 | subs 3: Founder Load / Menu Costs / Buyer Search | register_hits 0

  diction: subheads<=2w 12/12 ok, Title Case, unique book-wide (script-verified)
  claims_for_R (14):
    6.1.C1 Simon (1950s, e.g. 'A Behavioral Model of Rational Choice' 1955): limited knowledge/computation; search among few alternatives, stop when good enough; 'satisficing' term introduced by Simon
    6.1.C2 Simon distinguished substantive from procedural rationality
    6.1.C3 Aspiration levels adapt: rise after success, fall after failure (Simon; Cyert & March)
    6.1.C4 Cyert & March, A Behavioral Theory of the Firm: 'problemistic search' near the current problem; routines revised when performance falls below aspiration (latter carried from 2.4.C2)
    6.1.C5 Kahneman & Tversky prospect theory: outcomes evaluated relative to a reference point; losses weigh more than equivalent gains
    6.1.C6 Kahneman, Knetsch & Thaler (1986, 'Fairness as a Constraint on Profit Seeking'): survey evidence; price rise exploiting demand shift judged unfair, passing on cost increase more acceptable; evidence concerns judgments not measured purchases
    6.2.C1 Simon: 'a wealth of information creates a poverty of attention' (c.1971)
    6.2.C2 'Menu costs' in economics = physical/administrative cost of changing prices
    6.2.C3 Illustration: 30 of 50 deals x 1 founder-hour = 30 hours per 50 deals (invented numbers, labeled)
    6.3.C1 March & Simon, Organizations (1958): recurring decisions handled by programs (stored routines/performance programs) that economize on deliberation
    6.3.C2 March & Simon: uncertainty absorption — inferences drawn from evidence are transmitted in place of the evidence
    6.3.C3 Cyert & March: firm as coalition with differing goals; quasi-resolution of conflict, sequential attention to goals
    6.3.C4 Williamson (transaction cost economics): bounded rationality plus opportunism -> incomplete contracts; choice between market and hierarchy (governance form) affects transaction cost; builds on Simon
    6.4.C1 Illustration: startup 50% first-year discount with two triggers (>1/3 qualifying; <1 in 5 references) (invented, labeled; no arithmetic claim)

  claims_for_R (10):
    7.1.C1 Pareto efficiency (after Vilfredo Pareto): no feasible alternative improves one aim without worsening another; efficient set forms a frontier
    7.1.C2 Weighted-sum vs maximize-one-subject-to-floors (epsilon-constraint) formulations are closely related ways to choose on the frontier
    7.1.C3 Illustration: new account valued at 400 -> accept first-year discount up to 400 per account (invented, labeled)
    7.2.C1 Lagrange multipliers (Lagrange); extension to inequality constraints by Kuhn & Tucker (1951); multiplier = objective gain per unit relaxation, zero if non-binding (links 2.1.C1)
    7.2.C2 Illustration: capacity 20 accounts/quarter, 21st adds 15,000 -> shadow price ~15,000; 5 slots x 15,000 = 75,000 hiring threshold (arithmetic check)
    7.3.C1 Oi (1971), 'A Disneyland Dilemma': two-part tariff (entry fee + per-use price) captures surplus a single price leaves
    7.3.C2 Adams & Yellen (1976): bundling can raise profit when component valuations vary across buyers; bundle reduces dispersion of willingness to pay
    7.3.C3 Illustration: meter +10%, large-account volume discount 15%->25% lowers effective large-account price, raises small (invented; check direction: 1.10x0.75=0.825<0.85)
    7.4.C1 Simon's substantive vs procedural rationality (carried from 6.1.C2)
    7.4.C2 Illustration: 40% of enterprise proposals overridden citing implementation cost (invented, labeled)

  claims_for_R (10):
    8.1.C1 Jensen & Meckling (1976): agency costs = principal's monitoring expenditures + agent's bonding expenditures + residual loss
    8.1.C2 Fama & Jensen (1983, 'Separation of Ownership and Control'): decision management (initiation, implementation) vs decision control (ratification, monitoring); separated when decision agents do not bear major share of wealth effects
    8.1.C3 Aghion & Tirole (1997, 'Formal and Real Authority in Organizations'): formal authority = right to decide; real authority = effective control; principal often defers to better-informed agent
    8.1.C4 Illustration: 200 deals; bands 10/20/30%; 150+40+10=200; desk reviews 10/200=5% (invented, arithmetic check)
    8.2.C1 Kerr (1975), 'On the Folly of Rewarding A, While Hoping for B': reward systems that pay for one behavior while the organization hopes for another

# Pricing Tech

## Introduction
### Journey

Pricing Tech is organized as a three-part account of how technology firms price as they grow. Part I, Strategy, reconstructs the economic reasoning behind founder-led pricing under thin data, including familiar instruments such as freemium, early-bird windows, and list tiers, so that those labels can be analyzed as choices under scarcity and interaction rather than as folklore. Part II, Policy, turns that reasoning into stated objectives, constraints, instruments, and governance while a human still owns the decision. Part III, Engine, treats pricing when portfolio size and environmental speed make item-by-item human control infeasible: a mapping from environmental variables to a real-time price vector, together with simulation of alternatives, conjectures about buyers and rivals, firm and regulatory constraints, and pointers toward portfolio evolution.

The sequence matches a common growth path. Early prices are set by hand from judgment, analogy, and sparse market signals. Later, the firm needs rules that can be debated and revised without reinventing the rationale each week. Still later, exception queues and approval latency make a supervised, auditable engine the relevant operating form. The book’s end-state is therefore not a slogan about data-driven pricing; it is a specification of what such an engine must do when pricing by exception has failed, and of what Strategy and Policy must supply as inputs if the engine is not to become an opaque substitute for reasoning.

### Mindset Gap

Academic models of pricing are powerful and, for many founders, difficult to use. They typically presume information sets early firms lack: clean demand representations, well-measured costs, stable rival reaction functions, and samples large enough for identification. Founders ship under thin data, noisy feedback, and deadlines that do not wait for that identification. Distrust of academic economics as impractical, and dismissal of founder heuristics as under-theorized, both discard structure that remains usable when the spreadsheet is empty.

Pricing Tech addresses that gap without denying the data constraint. It outlines the economic reasoning behind pricing decisions at a level above ordinary MBA and Silicon Valley treatments, introduces formal methods when a stage of the journey earns them, and points outward rather than fully deriving those methods. It does not offer a quantitative cookbook. Technical founders are the intended readers: formal modeling in engineering contexts is assumed; economic acquaintance at the level of Information Rules is enough to begin; econometric depth is not required. Where measurement matters later, the text states what evidence would help and what class of model would use it, without becoming a methods manual.

### Information Goods

A second line of argument runs through the journey: the relation between a traditional production economy and markets in digital, information, and attention goods. Classical pricing stories often begin with scarce physical output, rising marginal cost, and inventory that spoils or sits. Many technology products invert those premises. Copies can be reproduced at near-zero marginal cost; information can be nonrival; attention is scarce on the demand side rather than on the factory floor; platforms and multi-sided markets rearrange who pays whom and when.

Those inversions do not abolish economics. They change which constraints bind and which analogies mislead. Marginal cost still matters, often as a boundary case rather than the center of the story. Capacity still matters, appearing as compute, compliance load, or human support rather than warehouse units. Competition still matters, reshaped by entry, switching, and network effects. The book keeps the textbook production economy and the firm’s digital economy in view so that tools transfer without being forced into the wrong shape.

### Method

The prose aims at lucid density: arguments stated, concepts defined when used, formal notation kept light. An equation may appear where words alone obscure a relation; skipping the formalism should still leave the point intact. Constraints on purely selfish optimization are framed as integrity—efficiency in the use of resources and accountability for contributions across a multi-step value chain—rather than as ethics sermons. Bounds on rationality, when they appear, follow the Carnegie tradition of limited attention, search, and organizational decision processes, not a tour of soft behavioralism.

Examples, fictional and real, carry analytical weight. Illustrations, when used, are aids; the text must stand without them. The book is serialized: each section is a self-contained unit of the larger journey, sized for careful reading. Footnotes are avoided; pointers to deeper treatments appear in the open text and in a bookshelf spanning industrial organization, game theory, contract theory, organization, and information economics. Founder judgment, written policy, and an auditable engine are successive forms of the same pricing problem under changing information and scale—not interchangeable slogans.

# Part I — Strategy

## Introduction
### Strategy Stage

Part I of Pricing Tech concerns Strategy: the economic reasoning beneath the pricing choices a technology firm makes while the founder still prices by hand. The book proceeds from Strategy through Policy to Engine. This part stays with the first stage—judgment, analogy, and sparse market signals—and does not anticipate formal policy rules or autonomous engines.

Strategy in this sense means a disciplined account of why a price, a free tier, a discount window, or a bundled offer is a choice under scarcity and interaction, rather than a set of positioning statements. Technical founders already make such choices. What they usually lack is a vocabulary that connects those choices to the structure of costs, willingness to pay, rivalry, and uncertainty, particularly when the product is digital, information-rich, or attention-mediated rather than a physical good with rising marginal cost.

The mindset gap named in the book introduction remains the working constraint. Academic models of pricing are powerful, but they assume information sets that early firms rarely possess. Part I therefore rebuilds reasoning that survives thin data. It does not assume that clean demand curves and identified competitor reaction functions are available; it asks what can still be stated rigorously when they are absent. In the Carnegie sense, the founder is boundedly rational: attention, search, and computation are scarce, and a useful strategy account must be usable under those limits.

### Hidden Choices

Startup pricing arrives with familiar labels: freemium, early bird, list tiers, usage-based meters, seat licenses, "land and expand." Each label packages a bundle of economic decisions—who pays and who does not, when price rises, what is rationed, and what is given away to learn or to secure a network. Treated as folklore, the labels circulate as recipes. Treated as strategy, they become objects of analysis: the scarcity each addresses, the interaction each invites, the option each forecloses.

Freemium, for example, is more than a free product with a paid upgrade. It is a deliberate split of the product or the user base, a conjecture about conversion, a cost structure able to absorb unpaid volume, and frequently a claim about network effects or complementary goods. An early-bird price is intertemporal discrimination under uncertainty about demand and about the firm's own quality, not merely a discount. Tiered plans encode segmentation hypotheses—about value, about cost-to-serve, about willingness to self-select—well before a firm can estimate a demand system.

Part I makes these embedded choices visible. Once visible, they can be compared, revised, and eventually formalized. While they remain invisible, the firm cannot distinguish learning from the repetition of a peer's practice.

### Chapter Map

The Strategy part unfolds in six chapters, each contributing one element of that visibility.

Foundations sets the primitives: what is being priced, which cost concept binds when marginal reproduction is near zero, and how digital, information, and attention goods rearrange the classical production account without abolishing it. Principles organizes durable rules of thumb that survive thin data—opportunity cost, marginal reasoning where it still binds, commitment and credibility. Value addresses willingness to pay, the distinction between value creation and value capture, and the gap between a user's surplus and the firm's ability to extract it under integrity constraints along a multi-step chain. Integrity here means efficiency in the use of resources and accountability for contributions, not a moral standard imposed from outside the economics.

Interaction introduces other agents: buyers who anticipate, rivals who react, platforms that set the terms of who pays whom. Uncertainty treats beliefs, learning from sparse signals, and decisions that cannot wait for identification. Behavior closes the part by locating bounds on rationality in the Carnegie tradition—limited attention, search, and organizational decision processes—rather than surveying behavioral anomalies. Its purpose is operational: to describe how founders and early teams actually decide under load, and how that shapes pricing strategy before a dedicated policy apparatus exists.

Across these chapters, examples carry more of the argument than notation. Formal methods appear only where a stage earns them; Part I stops short of full treatments and refers outward when depth belongs elsewhere.

### Strategy Scope

Strategy ends where the firm requires more than rebuilt reasoning—where choices must become explicit rules that can be stated, debated, and revised without reconstructing the rationale each week. That is Policy, the subject of Part II, and it is not developed here. Further ahead lies the Engine: pricing a broad portfolio in a fast environment where item-by-item human control fails. Naming these later stages keeps the map accurate; developing them here would borrow their material and blur what Strategy owns.

Part I supplies a foundation the later parts can inherit: standard startup pricing models recovered as economic choice, coherent under thin data, attentive to digital goods, and capable of being written as policy and later encoded in an engine. On this account freemium and its relatives are structured conjectures that a firm can defend, revise, and eventually outgrow.

## Chapter 1 — Foundations

### Introduction
#### Primitives

Chapter 1 of Pricing Tech is Foundations: the primitives on which every later pricing argument depends. Part I, Strategy, rebuilds economic reasoning under thin data so that familiar startup labels can be read as choice under scarcity and interaction. That project requires prior agreement on what is being priced, which costs bind when copies are cheap, how digital and attention goods alter the classical production account, and how platforms change who pays whom. Without shared primitives, debates about freemium and tiers proceed at cross purposes. Participants argue about conversion while disagreeing about the unit of sale, invoke zero marginal cost while ignoring cost-to-serve, and apply warehouse analogies to markets in which the scarce input is attention.

Foundations does not derive willingness to pay, analyze rivalry, or specify policy instruments. It fixes the language in which those later arguments are conducted. A reader who completes the chapter should be able to identify the priced object and justify the choice, state which cost concept binds for a product with near-zero reproduction cost, reject an inappropriate production-economy analogy, and describe who pays on a multi-sided offer.

The need for agreement can be stated directly. If two members of the same firm disagree whether the customer is buying software, access, seats, usage, or a guaranteed outcome, they are not yet in a position to evaluate freemium. If they agree on the object but disagree whether the binding scarcity is reproduction, support load, or attention on another side of a platform, they will misread every experiment. Disagreement of this kind is a predictable consequence of bounded rationality: each participant reasons from the cost and object most salient to their own function. Foundations makes the disagreement explicit, briefly and without a methods manual, while acknowledging that later chapters will revisit it with better tools.

#### Object, Cost

The first section asks what the price attaches to. The object of sale may be a product instance, access over time, a seat, a unit of usage, an outcome, or a right to participate on a platform. Technical founders frequently ship one thing and price another. A meter priced as though it were a seat, or a seat priced as though it were an outcome, introduces a strategic error into every cohort analysis. The choice of object is a practical decision, and it determines how every subsequent instrument should be interpreted.

Cost follows. Classical accounts rely on rising marginal cost and scarce physical output. Many technology products reverse the reproduction premise: an additional copy may cost almost nothing. Cost remains relevant nonetheless. Fixed and sunk costs shape entry and commitment, and cost-to-serve—support, compliance, compute, abuse handling—often binds long after reproduction cost has ceased to. Foundations separates the claim that copies are cheap from the claim that serving a given customer is free, and asks when each cost concept should constrain the price.

#### Goods, Platforms

Digital, information, and attention goods alter textbook premises without displacing economics. Nonrival copies change the problem of rationing. Information goods can be given away for strategic reasons and still create capture problems elsewhere in the chain. Attention is scarce on the demand side rather than on the factory floor. The task is to transfer existing tools under the appropriate constraints; industrial organization continues to apply.

Platforms extend the same issue to multi-sided exchange. Who pays whom becomes a design variable: subsidies, access gates, complements, and terms define the strategic setting before any list price appears. A production-economy reflex—charging the side that uses the product—can eliminate the participation the platform requires. Foundations introduces this structure only. Later Strategy chapters on value and interaction develop it, Policy writes it as rules, and the Engine encodes it. Here the reader needs an orientation: multi-sided pricing differs in kind from single-sided pricing, because the price on one side changes the value of participation on the other.

These four topics proceed in order of dependence. Cost cannot be assessed until the object is fixed; the premises of digital goods modify which costs bind; platforms then distribute those costs and values across sides. Each subsequent chapter of Part I assumes these choices have been made, even when it does not restate them.

#### Prior Ground

Foundations stops before the thin-data rules of thumb in Principles, before willingness and capture in Value, and before the strategic analysis in Interaction. It also excludes the governance questions of Policy and the price vectors of the Engine. Its subject is prior to all of these: agreement on object, binding cost, the premises of digital and attention goods, and the payment structure of platforms. Freemium, early bird, and tiered plans return in later chapters, where they can be analyzed precisely because the objects those labels refer to have been fixed here.

## Chapter 2 — Principles

### Introduction
#### Thin-Data Rules

Chapter 2 of Pricing Tech is Principles: durable rules of thumb that survive thin data. Chapter 1, Foundations, fixed the primitives—object, binding cost, the premises of digital and attention goods, and who pays whom on a platform. Those primitives prevent arguments from proceeding at cross purposes, but they do not tell a founder how to choose when the spreadsheet is empty. Principles stands between that groundwork and the analysis of willingness and capture in Value. It introduces opportunity cost, identifies where marginal reasoning still binds, treats commitment and credibility along price paths, and distinguishes economic heuristics from imitation of peers.

The chapter does not estimate willingness to pay, analyze rivalry, or write firm policy. It offers portable discipline: apply opportunity cost when data are sparse; determine when marginal cost or capacity matters and when it is a boundary case; design early commitments with credibility in view; and separate heuristics that encode economic structure from those that merely record fashion.

Technical founders often want a formula before accepting a principle. Principles declines that exchange, because a correct statement of opportunity cost under ignorance is more useful than a precise optimization over the wrong object. This position follows from bounded rationality in the Carnegie sense: when computation and information are scarce, good rules of thumb are an efficient allocation of attention rather than a concession. The treatment sits above management slogans and below full formal derivation, providing enough structure to decide and enough openness to revise when signals arrive.

#### Opportunity, Margins

Opportunity comes first because a price is always chosen against an alternative. Next-best use, forgone options, and approximate shadow prices matter even when demand curves are unavailable. Thin data do not remove the scarcity of alternatives; they require the firm to name the alternative explicitly—another segment, another instrument, waiting, or declining the sale. A discount that appears small is often a large transfer once the forgone list-price path is stated. Early-bird and freemium decisions are opportunity-cost problems before they are questions of positioning.

Margins follow as the question of where marginal reasoning still applies. Reproduction cost may be near zero while support, infrastructure, compliance, and abuse handling are not. Capacity can bind on the human side long before it binds on the server side. Principles asks the founder to locate the binding margin rather than treating zero marginal cost as a general permission. Boundary cases exist—sometimes the relevant marginal comparison is indeed near zero—but the claim must be established for the case at hand. Foundations named the cost concepts; Principles determines which one constrains a given price decision.

#### Commitment, Heuristics

Commitment concerns price paths over time: early-bird offers, lock-in, public list changes, and promises that are reversible or sunk. Credibility is a component of the price. A temporary discount the market reads as permanent functions as a permanent one. An early-bird offer that trains buyers to wait for the next such offer has purchased the wrong option. A seat price announced as stable and then cut repeatedly teaches rivals and customers the same lesson. Principles treats these as time-consistency problems under thin data, with less apparatus than full dynamic programming and more discipline than growth tactics.

Heuristics close the chapter: rules that transfer across cases. Freemium, tiers, and meters return here in outline—what scarcity and interaction each packages—rather than as full treatments or as practices copied from neighboring firms. A heuristic earns its place if it encodes opportunity, margins, and commitment in a form a busy founder can apply; it fails if it only names a fashion. Value and Interaction sharpen these labels further, and Policy later writes them as rules. Principles is where they become accountable shortcuts whose economic content can be stated and checked.

The sequence is analytic. Opportunity defines what a price decision gives up; margins identify which costs constrain it; commitment extends the decision through time; heuristics compress the three into rules that survive limited attention.

#### Rule Limits

Principles stops before the arguments about willingness and surplus in Value, before buyers and rivals in Interaction, and before the written rules of Policy. Foundations remains upstream and is not re-derived. The chapter's subject is the thin-data discipline between primitives and fuller strategy: scarcity of alternatives, binding margins, credible paths, and heuristics that can later be written as policy. Freemium receives a sharper treatment in later chapters, once it has been examined in terms of the opportunity cost it imposes.

### 2.1 Opportunity

#### Alternatives

Every price is chosen against an alternative, whether or not the alternative is named. Opportunity cost is the value of the best option given up by a choice, and in pricing the options are concrete: selling the same capacity to another segment, holding a list price rather than discounting, waiting for better information, or declining the sale. The concept requires no demand curve. It requires only that the decision maker state what else could be done with the scarce resource the price allocates.

The scarce resource is often not the product. A copy of software may cost almost nothing to reproduce, but the founder's sales time, the onboarding slot of a support engineer, and the attention of a design partner are rationed. A discount offered to win a marquee account consumes those resources as well as revenue. When the next-best use of the same sales week is a different account at list price, the cost of the discount is the gap between the two outcomes, not the percentage printed on the order form.

This framing changes the comparison founders usually make. The common question is whether a deal is profitable relative to doing nothing. The relevant question is whether it is better than the next-best deal the firm could have done instead. Under thin data the two answers diverge most often, because a young firm rarely has many deals in view and tends to treat the one in front of it as the only one available.

#### Shadow Prices

Formal optimization gives opportunity cost a precise form. In a linear program, the shadow price of a constraint is the increase in the objective obtained by relaxing that constraint by one unit; the idea is standard in operations research and runs back to the work of Kantorovich and Koopmans on resource allocation. A shadow price is zero when the constraint does not bind and can be positive only when it does. It is the price the resource would carry inside the firm if the firm ran an internal market for it.

Founders seldom write the program, but the concept transfers. If support capacity is fully booked, an additional customer carries an implicit charge equal to the value of whatever support work is displaced. If capacity is idle, the implicit charge is near zero. The same list price can therefore be generous in one quarter and costly in the next, without any change in the product or in the buyer. The useful habit is to ask which constraint currently binds and to price as though its shadow price were visible.

Opportunity cost also disciplines internal argument. When a sales lead proposes a discount and a product lead objects, the disagreement is frequently about different alternatives: one compares the discounted deal with losing the account, the other compares it with the list-price account that the same effort could have closed. Neither is wrong about arithmetic; they are reasoning from the option most salient to their function. Naming the alternative converts a dispute about judgment into a dispute about a fact that can, at least roughly, be checked.

#### Proxies

Under thin data the shadow price cannot be computed, so it is approximated. Serviceable proxies include the price of the most recent comparable sale, the revenue of the marginal account displaced when capacity is full, the cost of hiring the next unit of the binding resource, and the value of waiting one more cycle for information. Each proxy is crude; together they bound the number. This is satisficing in Simon's sense: the search for an exact optimum is replaced by a search for an option that clears an aspiration level set by the best visible alternative.

Forgone options deserve particular attention because they are invisible in accounting records. An early-bird price that fills a cohort also forecloses selling those seats at the later list price; a free tier that absorbs a prospect forecloses the paid conversation that might have occurred. Neither loss appears on a ledger. Stating the forgone option in writing, even roughly, is the cheapest available correction for that blind spot, and it prepares the ground for the cost question taken up next.

### 2.2 Margins

#### Binding Cost

The observation that digital goods have near-zero marginal cost is correct about reproduction and frequently wrong about service. Marginal cost is the change in total cost caused by one more unit of output, and the unit matters. One more download may cost a fraction of a cent; one more enterprise account may bring an integration project, a security review, a dedicated channel to engineering, and a share of on-call load. Foundations separated the claim that copies are cheap from the claim that serving a customer is free. Margins asks which of those costs binds for a particular price decision.

The answer depends on the object of sale. When the object is a self-serve seat, reproduction and hosting dominate and marginal cost is plausibly small. When the object is an outcome, a guaranteed throughput, or a regulated workload, marginal cost includes everything required to deliver that outcome, and it can rise with volume. A pricing argument that cites zero marginal cost without naming the unit is incomplete, because the same product can sit at both ends of this range depending on how it is sold.

#### Capacity

Capacity converts marginal cost from a flat line into a step function. Compute, support headcount, compliance review, and abuse handling each have limits, and near those limits the marginal cost of one more customer jumps from the running cost of existing capacity to the cost of adding a new increment. Usage meters on inference or storage exist partly for this reason: they pass a variable infrastructure cost to the buyer whose usage causes it.

Human capacity usually binds before machine capacity. Servers scale with a purchase order; trained support staff, solutions engineers, and compliance specialists scale with months of hiring and onboarding. A firm that prices as though only servers matter will discover the binding margin in its support queue, often as degraded service to existing customers rather than as an explicit cost. That degradation is a transfer from current customers to new ones, and it belongs in the price decision.

Compliance load behaves similarly. Each new jurisdiction, certification, or regulated customer class adds review work that does not shrink with scale in the way reproduction cost does. For products sold into finance or health care, compliance can be the dominant marginal cost of an additional segment even when the marginal cost of an additional user inside a segment is negligible.

A brief example makes the step structure concrete. Suppose a developer tool sells a team plan at a flat monthly price, and one support engineer can handle roughly two hundred team accounts. The two-hundredth account costs little beyond hosting; the two-hundred-and-first requires either a new hire or slower responses for everyone. Averaging that hire across all accounts understates the cost of growth near the threshold and overstates it just after the hire. The relevant number for a pricing decision is the marginal step, not the average.

#### Boundary Cases

There are cases in which marginal cost really is close to zero and capacity is slack: a mature self-serve product with automated onboarding, low support intensity, and idle infrastructure. In such cases the marginal comparison is not informative about the level of price, and other considerations, developed in Value and Interaction, carry the decision. Shapiro and Varian's Information Rules makes the general argument for information goods with high fixed and low marginal cost: competition tends to push price toward marginal cost, which leaves no margin to recover fixed cost, so cost-based pricing fails and prices are set according to value to the buyer.

The discipline lies in treating that case as a conclusion reached for a specific product, not as a premise assumed for all of them. A useful practice is to state the binding margin explicitly for each tier or meter: reproduction, hosting, support, compliance, or none. Where the answer is none, marginal cost sets only a floor. Where the answer is support or compliance, it sets a floor that moves with volume, and prices that ignore it accumulate obligations that the firm later pays for in service quality.

### 2.3 Commitment

#### Price Paths

A price is rarely a single number. It is a point on a path that buyers, rivals, and the firm's own staff project forward. A buyer evaluating an annual contract considers the renewal price; a buyer evaluating a list price considers whether a discount will appear at quarter end; a rival considering entry considers how the incumbent's prices will respond. Each of these projections is part of the effective price, which means that a pricing decision is also a statement about future decisions.

The economic difficulty is time consistency. Kydland and Prescott showed in macroeconomic policy that a plan which is optimal when announced may cease to be optimal once agents have acted on it, so that a rational planner will be tempted to deviate. Pricing has the same structure. A firm may announce a stable list price to encourage early adoption, then find that once adopters have committed, a lower price would attract the remaining market. Coase conjectured in 1972 that a durable-goods monopolist unable to commit against such price cuts would see buyers anticipate them and wait, driving prices toward marginal cost. Software licenses and lifetime deals are durable goods in the relevant sense.

#### Credibility

Credibility is the property that makes a promised path believable. It comes from costs the firm would bear by deviating: contractual price protection, most-favored-customer clauses, published price histories, and reputation with a buyer base that compares notes. A commitment without such costs is cheap talk, and buyers discount it accordingly.

The asymmetry runs in both directions. A temporary discount that buyers read as permanent functions as a permanent price cut, because they refuse to pay the higher price afterward. A permanent price that has been cut repeatedly is read as temporary, because buyers learn to wait. Either way the firm's realized price path is set by what buyers believe rather than by what the firm announced. Integrity in this context is a matter of efficiency: a firm whose announcements carry information saves its buyers search and negotiation cost, and the saving accrues partly to the firm.

Internal credibility matters as well. Sales staff who expect approval for any reasonable discount request will negotiate from the discounted price, not the list price, and buyers soon learn the same expectation. A list price that is routinely undercut carries little information. Policy later addresses approval rights and audit trails; at the level of principle, the observation is that each granted exception is also a signal about the next one.

#### Early Commitments

Early-bird pricing and lock-in are the commitment problems founders meet first. An early-bird offer exchanges a lower price now for revenue, references, and feedback before the product is mature. It works when the window is visibly finite and the later price is credible. It fails when the firm repeats the offer, because each repetition teaches the market that the window is a recurring event and turns the early-bird price into the expected price.

Lock-in operates from the other side. Switching costs, analyzed at length in Information Rules, let a firm price the installed base above what a new buyer would accept. Buyers who understand this demand compensation at entry, often as a discount or a contractual cap on renewal increases. The firm's gain from lock-in is therefore partly prepaid in the initial concession.

The practical distinction is between reversible and sunk promises. A published introductory price that will end on a fixed date is reversible by design and costs little credibility when it ends on schedule. A lifetime deal, a grandfathered tier, or a renewal cap is sunk: it binds the firm regardless of later information. Sunk promises buy more credibility and cost more flexibility, and the trade between them is the central decision in designing an early commitment under thin data.

### 2.4 Heuristics

#### Portable Rules

A heuristic is a decision rule that economizes on information and computation. Simon's account of bounded rationality treats such rules as a rational response to scarce attention: when the full problem cannot be solved, a rule that reliably yields acceptable outcomes is an efficient use of the decision maker's capacity. The question is which rules carry economic content and which carry only habit.

A rule travels across firms when it encodes a structural feature of the problem rather than a feature of the firm that first used it. Three tests follow from the preceding sections. The rule should identify the alternative it trades against, which is opportunity cost. It should state which cost it protects, which is the binding margin. It should be consistent with a credible price path, which is commitment. A rule that passes all three can be applied by a busy founder and checked by a colleague; a rule that passes none is a description of what someone else did.

Cyert and March, in A Behavioral Theory of the Firm, described organizations as relying on standard operating procedures that are revised mainly when performance falls below aspiration. Pricing heuristics behave the same way inside young firms, which is why a weak rule can persist for years. Stating the economic content of each rule makes it revisable when signals arrive.

#### Freemium, Tiers

Freemium, in outline, is a heuristic about opportunity cost and margins. It gives away a version of the product when the marginal cost of a free user is low, when free users generate value elsewhere, through referral, data, content, or network participation, and when the paid conversation forgone with each free user is worth less than that value. Where support or compliance load attaches to every user, the margin condition fails, and the free tier becomes a subsidy with no corresponding return. Value and Interaction return to freemium in full; here the point is the conditions under which the rule is economic.

Tiers package scarcity and segment buyers by self-selection. A reasonable default is that tier boundaries should follow the binding margin, so that customers who impose higher service, compliance, or capacity cost reach higher tiers, and that a tier should differ from its neighbor in something buyers in that segment observe. Meters follow a parallel default: meter what causes cost or what tracks value, and avoid metering quantities the buyer cannot predict, because unpredictable bills damage the credibility of the whole price path.

Early-bird windows and annual prepayment discounts fit the same scheme. An annual discount exchanges price for cash timing and reduced churn risk, and its size can be bounded by the firm's cost of capital and the observed monthly cancellation rate. A discount far larger than those quantities justify is a transfer, not a trade. A discount within them is a heuristic whose economic content can be written in one sentence.

#### Folklore

Folklore is the residue of heuristics whose economic content has been lost. Examples include the claim that three tiers are always correct, that the middle tier should be marked as most popular, that prices ending in nine always convert better, or that price increases should never be announced. Some of these rules have support in particular settings; none is a principle, because none states the alternative, margin, or commitment it addresses.

The distinction matters because folklore spreads through imitation, and imitation transmits the visible form of a peer's pricing page without the conditions that made it work there. A founder copying a competitor's tier structure inherits its boundaries without its cost structure or customer base. The defensible practice is to keep heuristics, attach to each a short statement of its economic rationale, and drop those for which no rationale can be written. Value, Interaction, and Policy then refine the surviving rules with sharper tools.

## Chapter 3 — Value

### Introduction
#### Exchange

Chapter 3 of Pricing Tech is Value: willingness to pay without a demand curve, the distinction between creating and capturing value, integrity along multi-step chains, and the division of surplus under thin data. Foundations fixed the primitives. Principles introduced thin-data rules—opportunity cost, binding margins, commitment, heuristics. Value asks what a user might pay and what the firm can retain, without assuming that the information set of academic models is available and without converting constraints into moral prescriptions.

The chapter has four practical aims. It shows how to form a hypothesis about willingness to pay without inventing a curve; separates value creation from the instruments of capture; frames limits on extraction as integrity in the value chain, meaning efficiency in resource use and accountability for contributions; and treats the division of surplus as an economic quantity rather than a question of fairness. Interaction develops buyers and rivals next, and Policy addresses governance later. Value remains with the object of exchange.

#### Willingness, Capture

Willingness comes first because a price without a hypothesis about willingness to pay has no basis for evaluation. Under thin data the firm seldom has a clean demand curve, survey evidence that generalizes, or identified experiments. It still needs a disciplined conjecture: who values the object, by how much relative to alternatives, and what sparse evidence would overturn the conjecture. Stated and revealed preference, segment hypotheses, and early conversion data are tools for forming that conjecture; none removes the need to acknowledge how little is known. Bounded rationality applies to the buyer as well: willingness to pay is formed under limited search and attention, and the firm's hypothesis must describe the buyer as that buyer actually decides. Foundations named the object, Principles named opportunity cost, and Willingness asks what the object is worth to someone who might pay.

Capture follows. Creating value does not ensure capturing it. Complements, spillovers, and bargaining position determine how much of the surplus remains with the firm. Instruments—list price, discount, meter, bundle—appear here only by reference; Policy addresses their formal use. One strategic error equates evidence that customers value the product with evidence that the firm can charge for it. The opposite error extracts so aggressively that the chain producing the value breaks down. Value keeps the two errors distinct.

#### Integrity, Surplus

Integrity is the frame for constraints on purely self-interested optimization. Efficiency means that resources—including attention, trust, and complementary effort across a multi-step chain—are directed to productive uses rather than consumed in disputes over transfers. Accountability means that contributions, positive or negative, can be traced: who created value, who free-rode, and who broke a promise on which willingness to pay depended. In collaborative products and on platforms, capture that destroys upstream contribution is internally inconsistent, since it erodes the source of the value being captured. Integrity identifies that inconsistency in economic terms.

Surplus closes the chapter: user surplus, firm surplus, and their division under thin data. An acceptable division is one the chain can sustain over repeated exchange. Thin data make the division harder to measure and easier to misjudge. A price that appears effective in isolation can eliminate the surplus that attracted the next cohort. Value treats surplus as an economic quantity to be tracked.

Language is a recurring source of error. Teams use "value-based pricing" to mean higher prices, or "customer value" to mean the product roadmap. Value in this chapter has a narrower meaning: a disciplined account of willingness, capture, integrity constraints, and surplus, usable when identification is weak and consistent with the instruments Policy later specifies.

The sequence is analytic. Willingness bounds what can be charged; capture determines how much of it the firm retains; integrity limits capture to what the chain can sustain; surplus records the resulting division.

#### Value Edges

Value does not treat strategic delay by buyers, rival reactions, or platform games; those belong to Interaction. It does not assign authority or audit trails; those belong to Policy. It does not re-derive Foundations or Principles. Its subject is the connection between thin-data discipline and exchange: what someone might pay, what the firm can retain, under which integrity constraints, and with what surplus remaining. Freemium and tiered plans look different once capture and integrity have been specified, because claims about value become hypotheses that evidence can refute.

### 3.1 Willingness

#### Without Curves

A demand curve is a schedule: for each price, the quantity buyers would take. Textbook pricing begins with the schedule and derives the optimum. A young technology firm almost never has it. Its price history is short, its customer base is small and heterogeneous, and its prices have moved together with its product, its channels, and its reputation, so that no observed change in quantity can be attributed to price alone. Estimating a curve from such data produces a number with the appearance of identification and none of its content.

The absence of a curve does not remove the need for a statement about willingness to pay. Every price embeds one. A list price of 40 per seat per month asserts that enough buyers value a seat at more than 40, relative to their next-best alternative, to make the price worth posting. Willingness asks the firm to write that assertion down as a hypothesis rather than leave it implicit in the price. A written hypothesis names the buyer, the alternative against which the product is compared, the dimension along which the product is better or worse than that alternative, and a rough range for what the difference is worth. It also names the evidence that would count against it.

Principles supplied the anchor for this exercise. Opportunity cost, read from the buyer's side, is the value of the best alternative the buyer gives up. Willingness to pay for a product is therefore bounded above by the value the buyer places on the product minus the value of that alternative, plus whatever the alternative costs. A hypothesis built on the alternative is more tractable than one built on the product in isolation, because buyers can usually describe what they would otherwise do, while they rarely can state what the product is worth in the abstract.

#### Stated, Revealed

Two families of evidence inform the hypothesis. Stated preference asks buyers what they would pay: surveys, interviews, and structured instruments such as the Van Westendorp price-sensitivity questions or conjoint exercises. Revealed preference infers valuation from choices actually made. The term comes from Paul Samuelson, who proposed that consumer theory be grounded in observed choices rather than in introspective utility. For a pricing team the distinction is practical. Stated answers are cheap to collect and weakly binding on the person who gives them; revealed choices are expensive to collect and binding by construction.

The gap between the two is systematic rather than random. Respondents in a survey spend no money, face no budget constraint, and often answer as the person they intend to be rather than the person who signs the purchase order. In business purchases the respondent is frequently not the buyer at all: the user who praises a tool is not the controller who approves its renewal. Stated willingness therefore tends to be informative about the ranking of features and the identity of alternatives, and less informative about levels.

Revealed evidence under thin data is scarce but not absent. Conversion from trial to paid at a posted price, the plan a buyer selects when several are offered, the point at which usage stops when a meter begins to charge, the discount a procurement team asks for before signing, and the churn that follows a price change all reveal something. Each is confounded. Conversion depends on onboarding quality, plan selection on how the tiers are framed, churn on everything that happened in the same quarter. The appropriate use of such evidence is to bound the hypothesis rather than to estimate a point.

#### Sparse Evidence

Segment guesses organize sparse evidence. A segment, for pricing purposes, is a group of buyers whose willingness to pay is believed to be similar because their alternatives are similar. A small agency choosing between the product and a spreadsheet, and a regulated enterprise choosing between the product and an internal build with a compliance team attached, have different alternatives and therefore different bounds, even if they use identical features. Segmenting by alternative rather than by firm size or industry keeps the hypothesis tied to opportunity cost. The formal construction of segments and the instruments that separate them are treated in Segmentation; here the segment is only a unit of conjecture.

Herbert Simon's account of bounded rationality applies to the buyer as much as to the firm. Buyers do not compute a reservation price by surveying all alternatives. They search until an option clears an aspiration level, and their willingness to pay is formed within that limited search: by the alternatives they happen to know, by the reference prices they have seen, and by the attention their organization allocates to the purchase. A hypothesis that describes an idealized buyer with full information will overstate willingness where search is shallow and understate it where switching away from the product would require effort no one has budgeted.

An illustration with invented numbers shows how sparse evidence bounds a hypothesis. Suppose a firm posts 40 per seat and observes, across sixty trials in one segment, that eighteen convert. Interviews with eight converters indicate that their alternative is a contractor costing roughly 300 per month for comparable work; interviews with six non-converters indicate that their alternative is an existing tool already paid for. The evidence does not identify a curve. It does support two statements: converters' willingness is plausibly well above 40, since their alternative costs several times that, and non-converters' willingness is bounded by the switching effort away from a sunk tool, not by the posted price. The next price decision should test the first statement, for instance by a higher price for new trials in that segment, and should not expect a lower price to move the second group.

The hypothesis is revised as evidence arrives, and the revisions are recorded. Under thin data a record of what was believed, when, and on what evidence is the closest available substitute for an estimated demand system, and it is the input that Capture requires.

### 3.2 Capture

#### Create, Capture

Creating value and capturing it are separate events. Value is created when an exchange makes the buyer better off relative to the alternative by more than it costs the firm to supply. Value is captured when some part of that difference is retained by the firm as price above cost. The first is a property of the product, the buyer, and the alternative; the second is a property of the bargaining position in which the product is sold. A product can create large value and capture little, and the history of technology contains many examples of exactly that outcome.

Adam Brandenburger and Harborne Stuart gave the distinction a compact form in their work on value-based business strategy. Total value created in a set of transactions is the gap between buyers' willingness to pay and suppliers' opportunity cost. A firm's added value is the reduction in that total if the firm were removed from the game, and, under conditions they state, the firm cannot capture more than its added value. The bound is useful because it redirects attention from what the product is worth to buyers toward what would be lost if this particular firm were absent. Where close substitutes exist, added value is small even when the value of the category is large.

The hypothesis formed under Willingness enters here. It estimates what buyers would pay relative to their alternatives. Capture asks what fraction of that gap survives competition, imitation, and the claims of everyone else whose contribution the product requires. The two numbers can differ by an order of magnitude, and a pricing argument that moves from evidence of high valuation to a high price without passing through added value commits the error the Value introduction described.

#### Complements

David Teece's analysis of profiting from innovation identified the role of complementary assets. When imitation is easy, the innovator's ability to capture value depends on who controls the assets required to deliver the innovation to buyers: distribution, manufacturing, service, data, integration. Value tends to flow to the owner of the scarce complement rather than to the originator of the idea. For technology products the complements are often the operating system, the app store, the cloud platform, the system integrator, or the data the buyer already holds. A firm whose product creates value only in combination with a complement controlled by another party should expect that party to claim a share of the surplus, through fees, terms, or the threat of building the product itself.

Spillovers work in the opposite direction. Some of the value a product creates accrues to parties who do not pay for it: users of an open format, developers who learn from a public interface, competitors who adopt a category the firm educated. Spillovers are not failures of capture to be eliminated. They often finance the adoption on which later capture depends. They are, however, value the firm should not count when estimating what it can charge, and a plan that assumes the firm will eventually capture its spillovers is usually a plan to fight its own ecosystem.

Kenneth Arrow pointed to a related difficulty with information as a commodity. A buyer cannot know the value of information before acquiring it, and once the seller discloses enough to establish its value, the buyer has partly acquired it without paying. Trials, demonstrations, and free tiers are partial solutions to this disclosure problem, and each gives away some of the value the firm hopes later to capture. Principles treated free tiers as heuristics; under Capture they are also a cost of revealing value to a buyer who cannot otherwise assess it.

#### Bargaining Room

Between the buyer's willingness to pay and the firm's opportunity cost lies a range within which any price is mutually acceptable. Bargaining room is the width of that range, and the price chosen within it determines the split. The Nash bargaining solution, which divides the gain from agreement according to the parties' disagreement payoffs, is a reference point rather than a prediction: it makes explicit that each side's share depends on what it receives if no agreement is reached. A buyer with a credible alternative has a strong disagreement position; a buyer whose workflows already depend on the product has a weak one.

An invented illustration makes the bounds concrete. Suppose a buyer's hypothesized willingness for an annual contract is 120,000, and the cost to the firm of serving that account, including the support and integration costs Margins identified, is 30,000. Bargaining room is 90,000. If the buyer's alternative is an internal build that would cost 80,000 and take a year, the buyer's effective ceiling is lower than 120,000 once delay is valued, and the firm's added value against that alternative is what bounds the price. A competing vendor at 60,000 compresses the room further. The arithmetic is trivial; the content lies in naming which alternative sets the ceiling.

The instruments that move the split, such as list price, discount, meter, bundle, and contract term, are referenced here only as pointers. Their formal design belongs to Policy and their strategic use against buyers and rivals belongs to Interaction. Capture establishes the quantity those instruments act on: the room between willingness and cost, bounded by added value and by the claims of complement owners. Integrity then asks how much of that room the firm can occupy without degrading the chain that produces it.

### 3.3 Integrity

#### Efficiency

Integrity in this book is an economic property of a value chain, not a moral quality of a pricing team. A chain has integrity when the resources it uses are directed to productive purposes and when contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them. Both conditions can be stated without reference to fairness or virtue, and both bear directly on what a firm can capture over more than one period.

Efficiency in use concerns where effort goes. A price structure induces behavior. A meter that charges per API call induces buyers to batch, cache, and avoid calls whose marginal value is below the meter rate; this can be productive, if it removes waste, or unproductive, if it pushes buyers to fragile workarounds that consume engineering time on both sides. A seat price induces license sharing. A contract with heavy overage penalties induces buyers to over-provision. In each case some resources are consumed not in producing value but in contesting its division: monitoring, negotiating, auditing, and evading. Those resources are a deadweight cost of the price structure, borne by the chain as a whole even when the firm's revenue appears to rise.

Attention and trust are resources in this accounting. A buyer who must model a complicated tariff before each decision spends attention that, under Simon's account of bounded rationality, has a real opportunity cost. A buyer who has learned that list prices are opening bids spends negotiation effort on every renewal. Neither cost appears on the firm's income statement, but both reduce the surplus available to be split and therefore the willingness that the next price decision can draw on.

#### Traceability

Accountability for contribution concerns attribution. Armen Alchian and Harold Demsetz analyzed team production, in which output is joint and individual contributions are hard to measure; their argument was that organization arises partly to meter contributions and reward them, because without metering each member has an incentive to shirk. Value chains in technology are teams in this sense. A platform's value depends on developers who build on it, users who contribute data or content, partners who integrate it, and the firm that maintains it. When contributions are untraceable, prices cannot reward them, and contributors who cannot be rewarded reduce effort or leave.

Traceability is therefore a precondition of sustainable capture. A firm that can show which features, integrations, or partner contributions generate willingness can price in ways that preserve them: revenue shares for complements that bring buyers, lower rates for usage that produces data the firm values, credits for referrals that demonstrably convert. A firm that cannot trace contribution prices on aggregates and will, in time, tax contributors it depends on at the same rate as those who free-ride.

Accountability also runs toward the firm. Willingness to pay often rests on a commitment, such as uptime, data handling, a price path, or a roadmap item, whose breach is traceable to the firm. Principles treated commitment as a constraint on price paths. Under Integrity the same commitment is a contribution the firm makes to the chain, and its breach lowers every participant's willingness to pay for the next exchange. A price increase that breaks an explicit or implied promise draws down that stock of willingness even when the increase itself is modest.

#### Chains

Multi-step chains amplify both conditions. Many technology products reach buyers through several hands: a model provider sells to an application developer, who sells to a reseller, who sells to an end customer; or a payments layer sits beneath a marketplace that sits beneath merchants. Each link prices to the next. Extraction at one link reduces the willingness available at every link downstream, and the loss is not confined to the link that extracted.

An invented illustration shows the mechanism. Suppose an end customer values a finished service at 100 relative to the alternative. An infrastructure provider charges an application developer 30, the developer's own costs are 20, and a reseller takes 15, leaving 35 of surplus across developer and customer. If the infrastructure provider raises its charge to 55, the remaining surplus is 10. At that level the developer may exit, switch providers, or stop investing in the features that generated the customer's valuation of 100. The provider's revenue rose for one period and the chain that produced its revenue contracted. Nothing in this account requires a judgment about whether 55 is fair. It requires only that each link's contribution and each link's alternative be visible.

The integrity frame does not prohibit high prices or large shares. A link with large added value, in the sense defined under Capture, can sustain a large share without degrading the chain, because removing it would cost participants more than its price. The frame identifies capture that is self-defeating: capture that consumes resources in contesting division, that cannot be attributed to contribution, or that removes the margin on which upstream or downstream contributors depend. Governance of these constraints, including who is authorized to change terms and how changes are recorded, belongs to Policy. Surplus takes up the quantity the constraints protect.

### 3.4 Surplus

#### Two Surpluses

Surplus is the difference between what a party receives from an exchange and what it gives up. User surplus is the buyer's willingness to pay minus the price paid. Firm surplus is the price minus the firm's opportunity cost of supplying the product. Their sum is the total value the exchange creates, and price determines how that total is divided. Alfred Marshall named consumer surplus, a concept Jules Dupuit had developed earlier, as a measure of the benefit a buyer derives beyond what is paid; the concept remains the most direct way to state what a buyer keeps.

The two surpluses play different roles over time. Firm surplus funds operations, investment, and the commitments described under Integrity. User surplus is what brings the buyer back, what the buyer reports to peers, and what makes the next cohort willing to try the product. A firm that sets price to maximize firm surplus in each exchange drives user surplus toward zero for marginal buyers, and buyers with no surplus have no reason to renew, recommend, or tolerate the frictions every product imposes. Retention and referral, in this accounting, are returns on user surplus left in the exchange.

The distinction also clarifies what a discount does. A discount transfers surplus from firm to user within the bargaining room identified under Capture. It is productive when the transferred surplus purchases something the firm values, such as commitment, a reference account, data, or earlier adoption, and unproductive when it merely reprices an exchange that would have occurred anyway. Principles noted that annual prepay discounts are bounded by the cost of capital and churn; that bound is a statement about what the transferred surplus buys.

#### Split Bounds

Measuring the split requires both willingness and cost, and thin data degrade both. Willingness is a hypothesis with a range, as Willingness established. Cost depends on which margin binds, as Margins established in Principles. The split is therefore known only within bounds, and the bounds are wide early in a product's life.

An invented illustration shows how wide. Suppose a segment's hypothesized willingness lies between 60 and 150 per seat per month and the binding marginal cost lies between 10 and 25. At a price of 50, user surplus per seat lies between 10 and 100, and firm surplus between 25 and 40. The firm knows its own share far more precisely than the buyer's. This asymmetry is typical and has a consequence: firms under thin data tend to optimize the surplus they can measure. Because firm surplus is visible and user surplus is not, pricing decisions drift toward extraction without any deliberate choice to extract.

Two practices limit the drift. The first is to track proxies for user surplus explicitly: expansion within accounts, renewal without renegotiation, unprompted referrals, and the share of buyers who accept a price change without escalation. None measures surplus directly, but a decline in all of them after a price change is evidence that the change consumed user surplus the firm did not observe. The second is to record, for each price decision, the assumed split as well as the price, so that later evidence can be compared to a stated expectation rather than to a recollection. Both practices extend the record that Willingness recommended.

#### Exchange Terms

Discussions of surplus division are often conducted in the language of fairness, and that language tends to obscure the economics. Fairness judgments vary across buyers, are sensitive to framing, and invite the firm to perform restraint rather than to analyze it. Behavioral evidence that buyers punish prices perceived as unfair is real and is treated in Behavior. The claim here is narrower: a division is sustainable when each party receives enough surplus to prefer continuing the exchange to its alternative, given what the other parties contribute.

Sustainability in this sense is a condition on repeated exchange. A split that leaves the buyer barely above the alternative is stable only while the alternative stays fixed; when a substitute improves or switching becomes cheaper, the buyer leaves with no surplus to lose. A split that leaves the firm barely above cost is stable only while costs stay fixed; when a binding margin tightens, the firm cannot fund the commitments on which the buyer's willingness rests. A division with room on both sides absorbs shocks of either kind.

Seen this way, the fair exchange frame reduces to the integrity conditions already stated. Efficiency requires that resources not be consumed in contesting the split; accountability requires that each party's share bear some relation to its contribution. A price that satisfies both and leaves positive surplus on each side is one the chain can sustain. Value ends with that quantity: what buyers might pay, what the firm can retain, under which integrity constraints, and how much surplus remains for each side. Interaction next removes the assumption that buyers and rivals take the firm's price as given, and asks how the split changes when other agents respond.

## Chapter 4 — Interaction

### Introduction
#### Other Agents

Chapter 4 of Pricing Tech is Interaction. The preceding Strategy chapters can be read as if a single firm faces a passive demand schedule: Foundations defines the priced object and the cost concepts that bind when reproduction is cheap; Principles states opportunity cost, binding margins, commitment along price paths, and heuristics that encode those distinctions; Value separates willingness from capture and locates integrity—efficiency in resource use and accountability for contributions—along multi-step chains. Interaction removes the passive-demand reading. Price formation then depends on how other agents move.

Buyers form expectations about paths, search across instruments, and treat published lists or free tiers as reference points; some withhold willingness in order to alter the firm’s information set. Rivals respond on instruments the firm can observe—matching, undercutting, or shifting competition into differentiation—often before any reaction function is identified. Where exchange is multi-sided, platform terms and cross-subsidies determine who pays whom before a one-sided tariff is even well-defined. Strategic forms therefore appear at founder scale with or without specialized game-theoretic staff. If a proposed price can be justified by a calculation that contains neither a strategic buyer nor a rival, the argument has not yet entered this chapter’s subject matter.

#### Buyers, Rivals

Interaction on the demand side concerns how buyers behave when they know the firm also chooses. Delay after an early-bird window, comparison across seats and meters, and anchors created by a free tier or a public list are not merely measurement error around a Value hypothesis about willingness; they are part of the environment in which that hypothesis must hold. Strategic silence—refusal to reveal willingness—is likewise an action that changes what the firm can learn. The task is to name these behaviors and the instruments that induce them, while remaining honest that thin data rarely identify a demand system.

On the supply side, rivalry arrives as observable moves under the same information constraint. Matching and undercutting are easy to see and hard to interpret without an explicit map of who can respond, on what lag, and with which lever. Escape into differentiation may dominate a price fight for a given cost and product structure; the comparison has to be stated rather than assumed. Competitive policy as a written organizational rule set belongs in Part II. Simulated libraries of rival strategies belong in Part III. Chapter 4 requires only a sketch of rivalry that does not pretend the sketch is an identified model.

#### Sides, Games

Sides prices the platform structure already named in Foundations. Two-sided tariffs, cross-subsidy, chicken-and-egg participation, and contractual platform terms can dominate outcomes before any single-sided list price is set. A mapping that charges the side that “uses” the product because that mapping feels natural can remove the side whose participation creates the market that Value described as an object of capture. Cross-subsidy in that setting is an instrument for creating participation; it is not a claim about virtue.

Games asks which strategic forms deserve explicit names at founder scale—preemption, matching, waiting, limited entry and deterrence—and which should remain unnamed because the firm cannot populate the corresponding extensive form with evidence. Repeated interaction matters when price paths teach returning buyers and rivals; one-shot folklore misleads when the same agents return. Formal trees the firm cannot estimate are deferred. Beliefs and options under empty spreadsheets are taken up in Uncertainty; Carnegie limits on attention and organization are taken up in Behavior. Interaction’s contribution is the multi-agent correction: other players enter the Strategy account with limited formalism and with decisions that must still be made.

The sequence Buyers, Rivals, Sides, Games is analytic. Reference prices and delay on the demand side often determine what a rival can profitably match. Platform terms can dominate both demand-side and rival behavior. Closing with Games forces an inventory of which strategic forms will be treated as named objects and which will be left as residual uncertainty.

#### Interaction Scope

The chapter does not restate Value’s surplus accounting, does not write Part II competitive playbooks, and does not construct Engine simulations. Uncertainty and Behavior remain subsequent. What Interaction owns is the displacement of solitary pricing: buyers, rivals, sides, and games as Strategy objects. Freemium and early bird, already treated as heuristics in Principles and as capture problems in Value, appear here as moves whose payoffs depend on other agents’ responses.

### 4.1 Buyers

#### Anticipation

A buyer who knows that the firm also chooses does not take a posted price as a fixed fact about the world. The posted price is read as one observation of a policy, and the buyer's decision depends on what that policy is expected to do next. Principles treated this problem from the firm's side, as commitment along a price path, and recorded the Coase conjecture: a durable-goods seller unable to commit against later cuts will find buyers waiting for them. Interaction takes the same structure from the buyer's side and asks which buyer behaviors the firm's own instruments induce.

Anticipation attaches to every instrument with a visible schedule.
