An early-bird window teaches buyers that prices rise at a known date, which pulls purchases forward; a quarter-end discount teaches the opposite, which pushes them back. A free tier with a generous allowance teaches buyers that the paid tier is negotiable at the margin, because the firm has already shown that it will give capacity away to gain adoption. Renewal terms teach buyers what the second year will cost, and that expectation enters the first-year decision. In each case the buyer is responding to a forecast of the firm's behavior, and the forecast is built from the firm's past moves rather than from its announcements.

The implication for pricing under thin data is that observed demand is partly a response to the firm's own history. A conversion rate measured during a period of frequent promotions estimates demand conditional on buyers expecting promotions. Value treated willingness as a hypothesis to be bounded with sparse evidence; anticipation adds that the evidence was generated by buyers who were themselves forecasting the firm. Change the pattern of moves and the same list price can face a different demand.

#### Search, Reference

Buyers also search across instruments, and the structure of the menu determines what search costs and what it finds. A tariff with seats, usage meters, and add-ons invites the buyer to compute an effective price for its own usage profile, and buyers who can do that computation cheaply will find the cell of the menu that serves them at least cost. Search therefore erodes any price difference that is not supported by a fence the buyer cannot cross. Segmentation later treats fences as Policy; at the level of Strategy the observation is that every menu is also a search problem posed to the buyer, and the buyer's solution determines which prices are actually paid.

Reference prices are the points against which buyers judge a quote. A published list, a free tier, a rival's visible price, and the buyer's own previous contract all serve as references. The firm chooses some of these and inherits the rest. A list price that is routinely discounted becomes a reference for the discount rather than for the price, and a free tier sets zero as the reference for whatever it includes. The psychology of reference dependence belongs to Behavior. The strategic point here is narrower: references are created by the firm's own visible moves and by rivals' moves, so the choice of what to publish is a choice about the reference against which every later quote will be judged.

An invented illustration shows the interaction. Suppose a firm lists a team plan at 50 per seat and closes most deals at 35 after negotiation. Buyers who compare notes learn that 35 is the operative price, and the 50 list functions mainly as a starting point for bargaining. If the firm then lowers the list to 40 and enforces it, buyers who previously paid 35 read the change as a price increase, while new buyers read it as a cut. One move, two references, two opposite readings.

#### Strategic Silence

Some buyers withhold information about their willingness in order to change what the firm can learn. A buyer who signals high value in a sales conversation invites a higher quote, so experienced buyers understate need, cite alternatives they would not in fact choose, and decline to share usage forecasts. Silence is an action in this sense: it alters the firm's information set and, through it, the price.

Strategic silence limits what interviews, trials, and stated-preference instruments can deliver. Value described those instruments as ways of bounding a willingness hypothesis; silence means that the bound is biased in a predictable direction whenever buyers believe their answers will be used to set their own price. The bias is weaker when answers cannot be traced to a negotiation, as in anonymous research or a posted price that applies to everyone, and stronger in enterprise sales, where the buyer's statements and the buyer's quote are linked.

The firm has partial responses. Posted prices with self-selection let buyers reveal type through choice rather than through statements, at the cost of leaving surplus with high-value buyers who choose a cheaper cell. Usage-based components reveal value after the fact, at the cost of uncertainty the buyer may decline to bear. Neither response removes silence; each moves the information problem to an instrument where it is cheaper to manage. Uncertainty takes up how beliefs about buyers should be updated from such fragments. Interaction requires only that the buyer be modeled as an agent who knows that what it reveals is used.

### 4.2 Rivals

#### Reaction

A rival is an agent whose price depends on the firm's price, and the firm's best price depends in turn on the rival's response. Industrial organization formalizes this dependence in reaction functions. In Cournot's model firms choose quantities and price follows from the total supplied; in Bertrand's model firms choose prices, and with identical products and constant marginal cost the competition drives price to marginal cost even with only two sellers. Kreps and Scheinkman later showed that when firms first commit to capacity and then compete on price, the outcome can resemble Cournot's. The models differ in what each firm is assumed to hold fixed when it contemplates a move, and that assumption, more than the number of rivals, determines how hard competition bites.

For a technology firm the useful question is which variable rivals actually treat as fixed. Compute capacity, sales headcount, and integration roadmaps are slow to change; list prices and discount bands are fast. When the slow variables are committed and the fast ones are flexible, price competition operates within the capacity each rival has already built. When reproduction is nearly free and capacity is slack, the Bertrand logic is closer to the truth, and price competition between undifferentiated offers tends toward the margin Principles identified as rarely recovering fixed cost.

Founders rarely have the data to estimate a reaction function. They can still state one: which rivals observe the firm's price, how quickly they could respond, on which instrument, and with what cost. A stated map is a hypothesis about rivals in the same sense that Value's willingness estimate is a hypothesis about buyers, and it can be revised when rivals move.

#### Undercutting

Matching and undercutting are the most visible rival moves and the hardest to interpret. A rival that matches a price cut may be defending share, signaling that cuts will not pay, or simply following an internal rule that pegs its list to the market. A rival that undercuts may have lower cost, a different objective such as growth funded by investors, or a mistaken estimate of the firm's cost. The same observed move is consistent with several reaction functions, and the firm's response should depend on which one generated it.

The Bertrand result explains why a price cut against a rival that can match at no cost rarely buys durable share: the rival matches, both earn less, and buyers learn to wait for the next round. Undercutting pays when the rival cannot follow, because of cost, contract terms, or a product that does not serve the contested buyers. An invented illustration makes the asymmetry concrete. Suppose two firms sell comparable reporting tools at 30 per seat and one cuts to 20. If the rival's cost to serve is 25, it cannot follow profitably and the cut may move share. If its cost is 5, it follows within a week, and both firms have reduced their price by a third for no change in share.

Capacity choices interact with these moves. A rival that has already provisioned infrastructure and hired a sales team for a target volume has a lower incremental cost of defending that volume than its average cost suggests, and it will match cuts that look unprofitable from the outside. A rival still deciding whether to build has more reason to concede. The firm's map of rivals is more useful when it records these sunk positions alongside list prices.

Matching clauses change the calculation. A public promise to match any lower price reduces the rival's gain from undercutting, because buyers do not need to switch to get the lower price. Such promises can soften competition while appearing aggressive, a reading that Competition will take up as Policy.

#### Escape

Differentiation is the standard escape from price competition between close substitutes. When products differ in ways buyers value differently, each firm faces a downward-sloping demand of its own, and a price cut by one firm attracts only the buyers for whom the difference was small. Hotelling's model of spatial competition gives the classic form: firms located apart in product space can price above cost, and the margin depends on how far apart they sit and how costly it is for buyers to cross the distance.

The strategic content lies in the choice between fighting on price and moving in product space. Escape is preferable when the firm can create a difference rivals cannot cheaply copy and buyers value enough to pay for. Fighting on price is preferable, if at all, when the firm's cost advantage is durable and the rival cannot match. The comparison depends on cost structure and product structure and should be stated rather than assumed. Differentiation develops which offer designs survive imitation; at the level of Strategy the point is that escape is a response to rivalry, chosen because a rival exists.

Observable moves are the evidence available for this choice. Rivals publish lists, announce plans, change free-tier limits, and win or lose visible accounts. Each observation updates the stated reaction map. The firm's own moves are observed in the same way, so every price change is also a message to rivals about how the firm responds, and that message shapes what they do next.

### 4.3 Sides

#### Price Structure

A platform sells access to one group of users partly in order to attract another. A marketplace connects buyers and sellers; a developer platform connects application builders and end users; a payments network connects merchants and cardholders. Rochet and Tirole characterized such markets as two-sided when the volume of transactions depends not only on the total price charged to both sides but on how that total is divided between them. Their distinction between the price level and the price structure is the central idea of this section. Two platforms with the same total charge can achieve very different volumes if one divides the charge in a way that attracts the side whose participation the other side values.

The consequence is that a price on one side cannot be judged by comparison with the cost of serving that side alone. A side priced below its marginal cost may be the condition for the revenue earned on the other side, and a side priced above cost may be paying for participation it does not see. Value treated capture as the share of a created surplus the firm retains; on a platform the surplus is created jointly by the sides, and capture requires choosing which side pays for the joint product. Foundations named platforms as the case in which who pays whom becomes a design variable; Sides makes the allocation explicit.

The side that pays is usually the side whose demand is less sensitive to price and whose value from the other side is larger. Armstrong's analysis of competition between platforms adds that the side which joins only one platform is the side platforms compete hardest for, because access to it is exclusive, while the side that joins several platforms can be charged more.

#### Cross-Subsidy

Cross-subsidy is the deliberate pricing of one side below cost to grow participation that raises value on the other side. Free consumer access financed by advertisers, free developer tools financed by enterprise licenses, and zero listing fees financed by transaction commissions are standard forms. The subsidy is an investment in participation, and it is justified only by the revenue the participation enables on the paying side.

The chicken-and-egg problem is the reason the subsidy is often needed at the start. Neither side wishes to join an empty platform, so a new entrant must attract one side before the other side's participation gives that side a reason to come. Caillaud and Jullien analyzed this problem as a coordination difficulty that pricing can resolve by subsidizing one side enough that it joins whatever it expects the other side to do, and recovering the subsidy from the other side, a strategy they call divide and conquer. In practice the subsidized side is often the one whose participation can be bought most cheaply per unit of value it creates for the other.

An invented illustration shows the arithmetic. Suppose a scheduling platform can attract 1,000 independent practitioners if listing is free, and each practitioner brings an expected 40 bookings a year from clients who pay a booking fee of 2. The subsidy on the practitioner side forgoes whatever listing fee practitioners would have paid; the fee side yields 80,000 a year. If a listing fee of 10 a month would cut participation to 300, the fee side falls to 24,000 while listing fees bring in 36,000, a total of 60,000. The free side earns more in this illustration only because participation on it drives revenue on the other.

#### Platform Terms

Prices on a platform are accompanied by terms that allocate surplus as much as prices do: exclusivity requirements, rules about off-platform transactions, data access, ranking policies, and fees on complements. Teece's point about complementary assets, discussed under Capture, applies in reverse when the firm is the platform: the platform controls the scarce complement and can claim a share of what others create on it. Terms are the instrument through which that claim is made.

Terms are also harder for buyers to compare than prices. A posted fee can be set against a rival's posted fee; an exclusivity rule or a data-access restriction has to be translated into an effective cost before it can be compared, and many participants do not make the translation. Terms therefore carry part of the platform's capture in a form that attracts less scrutiny than a fee of equal value would.

Terms interact with prices because they change participation. A rule against steering transactions off the platform protects the commission but raises the effective price to sellers who could otherwise avoid it; a generous data-access policy lowers the effective price to developers without changing any posted fee. A firm that adjusts its posted prices while ignoring terms has changed only part of the tariff.

Many technology firms are platforms without describing themselves that way. A product with an integration marketplace, an API used by partners, or a community whose content attracts buyers has more than one side. The pricing question for such firms is first which sides exist and which one pays, and only then what the paying side is charged. Competition among platforms, including how terms are used against rivals, belongs to Part II.

### 4.4 Games

#### Founder Scale

Game theory supplies names for strategic situations, and a founder benefits from some of the names without the full apparatus. Commitment is the most useful. Schelling argued in The Strategy of Conflict that the ability to bind oneself can be an advantage, because a party that visibly cannot retreat changes what its opponent finds worth doing. Principles applied this idea to price paths and buyer expectations; here it applies to rivals. A firm that has signed multi-year price protection with its installed base has committed not to cut deeply without cost, and a rival can read that commitment.

Entry and deterrence are the next names. An incumbent can make entry less attractive by committing in advance to fight it, but threats to cut price after entry are credible only if fighting would be in the incumbent's interest once entry has occurred. Dixit showed how investment in capacity can make such a threat credible, because capacity already built lowers the incremental cost of responding. Milgrom and Roberts analyzed limit pricing as signaling: an incumbent prices low to persuade an entrant that its cost is low. At founder scale the entrant is often the firm itself, and the useful exercise is to ask which incumbent responses are credible given the incumbent's sunk commitments and which are announcements.

An invented illustration: an incumbent with 2,000 enterprise contracts on fixed renewals cannot cut its list price for new buyers without inviting renegotiation across the base. A newcomer that understands this constraint can price below the incumbent in the new-buyer segment with less risk of a matching response than the incumbent's public statements suggest.

#### Repeated Play

Most pricing interactions are repeated. The same buyers renew, the same rivals observe each quarter's prices, and the same sales staff negotiate with the same procurement teams. Repetition changes which outcomes can be sustained. In a single interaction, a rival gains from undercutting; in repeated interaction, the prospect of retaliation can make restraint the better choice, and results in repeated-game theory, often grouped as folk theorems, show that a wide range of outcomes can be sustained when players value the future enough. The same logic applies to buyers: a firm that exploits a locked-in buyer at renewal gains once and loses the reputation that made later sales cheaper.

The founder-scale implication is that one-shot reasoning misleads whenever the same agents return. A discount granted to close a quarter teaches the buyer and, through the buyer's peers, the market what to expect next quarter. A price war that a rival interprets as permanent invites a permanent response. Repetition also creates the conditions for tacit coordination between rivals, which is a matter of law as much as of economics, and the book leaves that subject to Competition and Enterprise.

Repetition makes reputation an asset with a price. Consistency in past moves is what allows buyers and rivals to forecast future ones, and the forecasts in turn shape demand and rival response. The firm's price history is therefore part of its strategic position.

#### Deferred Forms

Not every strategic situation deserves a model. Extensive-form games with private information, sequential bargaining with outside options, and auctions with correlated values all have precise treatments, and each requires parameters that an early firm cannot estimate. A model populated with guesses produces precise conclusions from imprecise premises and can mislead more than an informal argument that states its uncertainty.

The practical choice is which strategic forms to name and track and which to leave as residual uncertainty. A reasonable rule is to name a form when the firm can identify the players, their main instruments, and at least the sign of their likely response, and to defer it when any of the three is unknown. Commitment, matching, entry response, and repetition usually meet that condition at founder scale. Detailed signaling equilibria and multi-stage bargaining models usually do not, and they belong in later work once data accumulate.

The rule cuts both ways. A firm that refuses to name any strategic form treats rivals and buyers as passive and repeats the error this chapter was written to remove. A firm that formalizes every form spends scarce analytic attention on models it cannot populate. The inventory sits between these errors, and it is revised as evidence about players and instruments accumulates.

Deferral is a decision, recorded as such. A deferred form remains in the inventory as an acknowledged gap, and the evidence that would justify modeling it is stated. Uncertainty takes up how beliefs about deferred forms are held and revised under thin data; Behavior takes up the limits that bounded attention places on how many forms any team can track. Part II turns the named forms into competitive Policy, and Part III gives the Engine conjectures about buyers and rivals that can be simulated. Interaction's contribution is the inventory itself: the other agents, the strategic forms that govern their responses, and the line between what is modeled and what is left open.

## Chapter 5 — Uncertainty

### Introduction
#### Beliefs First

Chapter 5 of Pricing Tech is Uncertainty. Interaction placed other agents in the Strategy account—buyers who anticipate, rivals who respond, sides that set who pays whom, and games named only where evidence can support them. Those agents act under incomplete information, and so does the firm. Uncertainty makes the book’s opening mindset gap operational: academic pricing models often assume information sets that early firms do not have, while founders still must choose prices. The chapter’s subject is beliefs before identification, learning from sparse signals, options embedded in irreversible price moves, and decisions when the spreadsheet is empty.

The arc runs Beliefs, Signals, Options, Thin Data. Priors are stated first because an update rule without an explicit prior has nothing to revise against. Evidence is treated next because conversion fragments and cohort scraps are easy to over-interpret when samples are small and selection is severe. Options follow because list cuts, early-bird windows, and public commitments change the feasible set in ways that are costly to reverse. Thin Data closes by naming empty-spreadsheet practice without substituting false precision for ignorance. Policy-scale experiments and Engine simulation stacks are out of scope; Behavior will later locate Carnegie bounds on attention and organization that further restrict what rational updating can mean inside an early team.

#### Beliefs, Signals

Beliefs under thin data concern quality the firm has not yet demonstrated, demand it has not yet observed at scale, and rival responses it has not yet seen on the relevant instrument. A usable prior is an explicit stance about what is unknown, what would count as an update, and which decisions cannot wait for that update. It is not a costume of certainty. Interaction’s strategic silence and rival opacity enter here as reasons the information set stays poor; Uncertainty asks how pricing proceeds under that constraint rather than how to pretend the constraint is gone.

Signals are the scraps that arrive: noisy feedback, conversion fragments, early cohort paths, and support tickets that look like willingness or like cost-to-serve. The analytical problem is separation of signal from noise when samples are small and selection is severe. Fitting a week of freemium conversion into a demand curve imports the academic-information error into founder practice. Ignoring every scrap freezes the firm. The required middle is disciplinary rather than motivational: name what the signal could mean, what else could produce it, and what price move—if any—it justifies before identification.

#### Costly Reversal

Many pricing actions are options in the ordinary sense of irreversible or costly-to-reverse commitments. Waiting versus pricing now, cutting a public list, opening a discount window that teaches buyers to wait, and locking an early-bird path all change future feasible sets. Treating those moves only as growth tactics hides the option structure. The chapter outlines that structure without a full real-options derivation: which moves consume flexibility, which preserve it, and how thin data changes the value of waiting relative to learning by pricing.

Thin Data is the residual condition rather than a separate epistemology. Analogy and transfer from neighboring markets, engineering judgment about object and cost from Foundations, and heuristics from Principles remain available when cells are blank. What is forbidden is presenting that transfer as if it were identified econometrics. The book’s goal—rigorous reasoning without assuming academic data richness—is most concrete here. Decisions under ignorance remain decisions; they do not license invented precision.

The sequence matters analytically. Beliefs without a signal rule invite arbitrary updating. Signals without an option vocabulary invite treating every list change as costless. Options without an explicit thin-data stance invite importing textbook identification assumptions through the back door. Closing with Thin Data returns the reader to the constraint that links this chapter to the book introduction: rigor that survives empty cells.

#### Information Scope

Uncertainty does not reopen Interaction’s map of agents, does not write Policy learning loops or experimental governance, and does not build Engine scenario libraries. Behavior follows with bounds on cognition and organization. What this chapter owns is the information problem of Strategy pricing: beliefs, sparse evidence, irreversible moves as options, and honest practice when the spreadsheet does not supply a curve.

### 5.1 Beliefs

#### Explicit Priors

A price set before demand has been measured still rests on a belief about demand. The belief may be unexamined, borrowed from a rival's page, or inherited from a founder's previous company, but it determines the number as surely as an estimated curve would. The subjective view of probability associated with Bayes, and given its decision-theoretic form by Savage, treats such beliefs as legitimate inputs to choice: a decision maker with coherent preferences over acts behaves as if holding probabilities over states and weighing outcomes by them. The practical reading for pricing is modest. A prior is a written statement of what the firm currently thinks is true about buyers, rivals, and cost, with enough structure that later evidence can move it.

Knight's distinction between risk and uncertainty marks the limit of that reading. Risk describes situations where outcomes follow a distribution that can be known or estimated from repeated cases; uncertainty describes situations without such a basis. An early software firm pricing a new category sits closer to the second condition. The Savage framework still permits a decision, because it asks only for coherence, yet the probabilities it yields carry no claim of frequency behind them. Stating a prior therefore commits the firm to consistency, not to accuracy. The value of writing it down lies in what it exposes: the range of willingness the firm is assuming, the share of buyers it expects in each tier, the rival response it is implicitly ruling out.

A usable prior has three parts. The first is a central belief, such as an expectation that a mid-sized team values the product near the cost of one part-time analyst. The second is a spread, stated as the range within which the firm would not be surprised. The third is an update list: the observations that would move the belief up, down, or outward. Without the third part the prior functions as a fixed assumption, and fixed assumptions are what Principles warned against when it treated heuristics as rules with stated revision conditions.

#### Unknown Quality

Two unknowns dominate early pricing, and they differ in kind. The first concerns quality as the buyer perceives it. The firm may know its product well and still not know how buyers will rate it against alternatives they already use. Value described willingness as a hypothesis to be bounded; quality uncertainty adds that the bound depends on a perception the firm has not yet observed forming. A buyer's first months with the product generate that perception, so early prices are set before the main input to willingness exists.

The second unknown concerns demand at scale: how many buyers share the profile of the first few, and whether their willingness resembles that of early adopters. Early buyers are rarely a random draw. They are found through the founders' networks, they tolerate missing features, and they often value novelty for its own sake. A prior built on them overstates both willingness and homogeneity unless the firm writes the selection into the belief. The correction is a matter of stated structure: a separate belief about later segments, with a wider spread, rather than an extrapolation of the first cohort.

The two unknowns interact through price itself. A high price can be read by buyers as a statement about quality, and a low price can be read as a statement about doubt. That reading belongs to Signals, where the direction of inference runs from firm to buyer as well as from buyer to firm. For beliefs, the relevant point is that the firm's uncertainty about quality and the buyer's uncertainty about quality are different quantities, and a prior that conflates them will misread early evidence.

#### Revision Rule

An updating stance specifies how much a given observation should move the belief. Bayes's rule gives the form: the revised belief is proportional to the prior times the likelihood of the observation under each hypothesis. The form is useful even without computation, because it makes two errors visible. One is moving too far on an observation that would be likely under every hypothesis the firm entertains; such an observation carries little information however vivid it feels. The other is refusing to move on an observation that would be unlikely under the current central belief.

An invented illustration shows the arithmetic. Suppose a firm holds two hypotheses about a new tier priced at 80 per month: that a typical prospect values it above that price, or below it. It gives the first hypothesis a weight of one half. Ten trial accounts reach the paywall and three convert. If the firm judges that a conversion rate near 30 percent is about twice as likely under the first hypothesis as under the second, the revised weight moves to roughly two thirds. The numbers are made up, and the judgment of likelihoods is itself a belief, yet the exercise forces the firm to say how diagnostic the evidence is before reacting to it.

Interaction recorded that buyers withhold information strategically, so statements from sales conversations deserve low likelihood ratios. Interaction also showed that demand measured under a pattern of promotions reflects buyers who expect promotions. Both observations feed the updating stance directly: they lower the weight of certain evidence before it arrives. Writing those discounts down in advance keeps the firm from adjusting them after the fact to suit a preferred conclusion.

### 5.2 Signals

#### Noisy Feedback

Early pricing evidence arrives as fragments: a handful of conversions, a sales call in which a buyer balked, a churn notice with a one-line reason, a support ticket that suggests heavy usage. Each fragment is consistent with several explanations. A cancelled subscription may reflect price, a missing integration, a budget cycle, or a champion who left the buyer's organization. Noise in this setting means variation that the firm cannot attribute to the cause it cares about, and with small samples the noise is often larger than the effect of any plausible price change.

The first discipline is to list rival explanations before reading a fragment as price evidence. A buyer who says the product is too expensive may be stating willingness, may be negotiating, or may be reporting that the product has not yet delivered enough value to justify any price. The words are identical; the pricing implication differs. Interaction treated strategic silence as an action buyers take; noisy feedback includes the related case of strategic speech, in which buyers report a lower willingness because they expect the report to shape their quote.

The second discipline concerns sample size. With twenty trials, the difference between three and six conversions sits within the range that chance alone produces for many plausible underlying rates. A firm that reprices on that difference is responding to noise. The rule of thumb that follows needs no statistics course: before reading a change in conversion as a response to price, ask how many additional conversions or losses would be needed to make the change hard to attribute to chance, and compare that figure with the traffic the firm can expect in the next month.

#### Cohort Scraps

Cohorts offer a richer kind of evidence because they follow buyers over time rather than counting decisions at one moment. A cohort that started in a given month can show how many accounts remain after three months, how many upgraded, and how revenue per account moved. Even scraps of such data distinguish between pricing problems and value problems more cleanly than conversion counts do. Accounts that convert and then churn quickly suggest that the price was accepted on an expectation the product did not meet; accounts that never convert but use the free tier heavily suggest that the paid cell is mispositioned or that the free allowance is too generous.

Cohort evidence carries its own confound: each cohort was acquired under different conditions. A cohort recruited during a launch announcement differs from one recruited through paid search, and both differ from buyers referred by existing customers. Price changes made between cohorts are therefore mixed with changes in acquisition channel, product version, and season. Treating the comparison as a controlled test imports the identification the firm does not have. Structured experiments with randomized assignment belong to Policy learning; at the level of Strategy the task is to recognize which comparisons are confounded and to weight them accordingly.

An invented illustration makes the risk concrete. Suppose the March cohort, priced at 40 per seat, retains 70 percent of accounts at three months, while the May cohort, priced at 50, retains 60 percent. The tempting reading is that the increase cost ten points of retention. If the May cohort also came mainly from a single conference and included a large share of students, the difference may owe more to who arrived than to what they paid.

#### Selection

Selection is the most persistent source of misread signals because the firm sees only the buyers who reached a given stage. Conversion rates measure the willingness of prospects who found the product, read the pricing page, and started a trial. A price that looks well accepted may be accepted only by the narrow group that the firm's current channels reach. Raising the price and observing stable conversion can mean that willingness is high, or that the channel delivers buyers with unusually high willingness, or that the buyers most sensitive to price never arrive at the page to be counted.

Signals also travel from firm to buyer. Spence's analysis of job-market signaling showed how an action can convey private information when it is costlier for some types than for others, so that the action separates them. Applied cautiously to pricing, the idea explains why a firm with an untested product may hesitate to price low: buyers uncertain about quality may read a low price as an admission. The analogy has limits. Price is cheap to change, so its signaling value depends on the firm's history and commitments, which Principles treated as credibility; Interaction already covered limit pricing as a signal aimed at rivals.

The working conclusion for Signals is a ranking of evidence by how much it can move a stated prior. Unprompted behavior by buyers who did not expect it to affect their own price ranks highest; cohort comparisons with known confounds rank in the middle; statements in negotiations rank lowest. The ranking does not replace judgment. It records, before the evidence arrives, how much weight each kind will receive.

### 5.3 Options

#### Waiting

A pricing decision taken under uncertainty has a feature that standard demand analysis omits: some choices can be postponed, and postponement has value when the information available later would change the choice. Dixit and Pindyck developed this point for capital investment in Investment under Uncertainty. When an investment is at least partly irreversible and the firm can delay it, the right to wait behaves like a financial option, and exercising it early gives up the value of learning first. The conventional rule of investing whenever expected present value exceeds cost then becomes too permissive, because it ignores the option that the investment extinguishes.

The same structure applies to many price moves, though with an important reversal. In capital investment, waiting usually produces information at little cost because the market evolves independently of the firm. In early pricing, the firm often learns only by pricing: no transactions means no evidence about willingness. Waiting therefore buys less information than it does in the investment case, and sometimes none. The value of delay depends on whether the uncertainty resolves on its own, through rivals' moves, regulatory clarity, or the arrival of a cost figure, or whether it resolves only through the firm's own sales.

That distinction sorts pricing questions usefully. Uncertainty about a supplier's future rates or about a rival's launch may resolve independently, so postponing a public commitment can be worth its cost. Uncertainty about willingness in a new segment resolves through selling, so postponing the price simply postpones the evidence. A firm that waits in the second case is paying for an option that delivers nothing. Many early decisions mix both kinds of uncertainty, and separating them is the first step in judging whether delay has any value at all.

#### Irreversibility

Price moves differ in how costly they are to reverse, and the difference determines how much option value each consumes. A private quote to one buyer is nearly reversible; the next quote can differ. A published list price is less so, because it becomes a reference for buyers and rivals, as Interaction described. A public cut is harder still to undo, since raising the list afterward reads as an increase to everyone who saw the lower figure. Grandfathered contracts, annual prepayments, and promises such as a lifetime price for early adopters are the least reversible of all, because they bind the firm to specific buyers for specific periods.

Principles treated these moves as commitments and asked when commitment helps. Options asks a complementary question: what flexibility does each move consume, and what is that flexibility worth given current uncertainty. When beliefs about willingness are wide, flexibility is valuable, because the probability of wanting to revise is high. When beliefs are narrow, flexibility is worth less and commitment benefits can dominate. The same move can be sound at one stage of evidence and premature at another.

An invented illustration makes the asymmetry concrete. A firm unsure whether its tier is worth 30 or 60 per seat can start at 60 with private discounts, preserving the ability to settle at either figure, or publish 30 and later attempt to raise it. The first path keeps the option open at the cost of some lost deals; the second gains early volume and closes the upper branch for every buyer who saw the lower price.

#### Discount Windows

Discount windows deserve separate treatment because they combine irreversibility with teaching. A launch discount, a quarter-end offer, or an early-bird rate creates a dated opportunity, and buyers who observe it learn something about the firm's future behavior. The Coase problem recorded in Principles is the extreme case: buyers who expect later cuts wait for them. Each window is therefore both a sale and a lesson, and the lesson persists after the window closes.

Viewed as options, discount windows have identifiable costs. They spend the firm's ability to hold a list price credibly, they move purchases in time rather than adding new ones when buyers anticipate them, and they make later observations harder to read, since demand now reflects expected promotions. Their benefits are also identifiable: earlier evidence on willingness, faster accumulation of references, and an exit path when the firm's prior turns out too high. A window with a fixed end date and no repetition consumes less option value than a recurring one, because buyers cannot build a schedule from a single instance.

The practical framing treats each window as a purchase of information and volume paid for with flexibility. Before opening one, the firm can state what it expects to learn, what result would justify a second window, and what result would end the practice. Without that statement, discounting tends to repeat because each window appears cheap in isolation while the cumulative cost lands on the list price. The real-options vocabulary here is qualitative. Formal valuation requires distributions the firm rarely has, a point Thin Data takes up directly.

### 5.4 Thin Data

#### Analogy

When the spreadsheet is empty, the firm still has evidence of a different kind: prices in neighboring markets, its own cost structure, the budgets buyers already spend on the problem, and the experience of firms that sold to similar buyers. Reasoning by analogy transfers that evidence to the new case. The transfer is legitimate when the analogy is explicit about what is assumed to carry over and what is not. A firm pricing a data-labeling service can look at what buyers pay contractors for the same work, at the price of adjacent software, and at the seat costs of tools in the same budget line, while stating which differences in risk, speed, or quality would shift willingness away from those anchors.

Analogy fails silently when its assumptions are hidden. A founder who prices like a well-known rival has imported the rival's cost structure, customer base, and strategic aims along with the number. Interaction noted that a rival's visible price reflects its own reaction pattern and position; copying it treats a strategic outcome as a market fact. The better practice uses several analogies at once and records where they disagree. Disagreement among anchors is itself information: it marks the dimensions along which the firm's prior is weakest.

Foundations supplies a further anchor through cost and object. Marginal cost bounds the price from below for usage that scales with compute or support, and the definition of the priced object determines which analogies apply at all. A product sold per seat should borrow from seat-priced neighbors; one sold per outcome should borrow from service contracts. Choosing the analogy is part of choosing the object.

#### Ranges

Thin data argues for stating beliefs as ranges and decisions as positions within them. A firm that writes that willingness for its core tier probably lies between 40 and 120 per month has recorded both a belief and its ignorance. The width of the range is informative: a wide range indicates that the next evidence will be valuable and that flexible instruments, discussed in Options, deserve preference. A narrow range indicates that the firm can commit with less risk of regret.

Ranges also discipline communication inside the firm. A single number presented in a planning document invites false confidence; downstream forecasts inherit its precision and compound it. An invented illustration shows the compounding. If a revenue plan multiplies a price point of 80 by a conversion rate of 5 percent and an expected funnel of 2,000 trials, it reports 8,000 in new monthly revenue. If each input is honestly uncertain within a factor of two, the product of the three spans roughly 1,000 to 64,000, and the original figure conveys almost nothing. Presenting the range keeps the plan from serving as evidence for the assumptions that produced it.

The opposite failure also occurs. A firm aware of its ignorance may decline to commit to any figure, holding pricing as permanently provisional. Buyers then face unstable quotes, sales teams negotiate from no reference, and the firm learns little because each data point was generated under a different regime. A range is a basis for choosing, and the choice still has to be made. Holding one regime long enough to observe it is often worth more than the marginal precision a further delay would buy.

#### Decision Rules

Choosing within a range calls for rules that work without identified curves. Three are standard enough to state. The first compares the costs of error in each direction: if pricing too low is hard to reverse and pricing too high mainly costs some deals that can later be recovered, the firm should lean toward the upper part of its range. The second favors instruments whose results are informative, such as prices that let buyers self-select across tiers, so that each sale also refines the belief. The third sets a review date and an update list in advance, which turns the price into a stated hypothesis rather than a settled fact.

These rules extend Principles rather than replace it. Heuristics there were portable rules with revision conditions; here they become rules for choosing under ranges, tied to the asymmetries Options identified. They also respect the limit Knight drew. Under uncertainty in his sense no rule guarantees the right price, and a firm that frames its rule as optimal has claimed more than its evidence supports.

Thin Data also marks where this chapter stops. The bounds on what a small team can attend to and how organizations revise routines belong to Behavior, which follows. Systematic learning through designed experiments belongs to Policy, and model-based simulation belongs to Engine. What remains here is the minimum standard for a founder's price under ignorance: a stated prior, an evidence ranking, an account of what flexibility each move spends, and a range from which a position was chosen for reasons that can be written down and later checked.

## Chapter 6 — Behavior

### Introduction
#### Bounds First

Chapter 6 of Pricing Tech is Behavior. Uncertainty treated the information problem of Strategy pricing: beliefs before identification, sparse signals, irreversible moves as options, and practice when the spreadsheet is empty. Behavior asks what limited cognition and early organization do to that problem. The firm does not only lack data; it also lacks unbounded attention, perfect recall, and a decision process that can re-optimize the entire menu each time a scrap arrives. The chapter’s subject is Carnegie bounds on rationality, attention scarcity in founder and early-team pricing, how those teams actually decide before a policy apparatus exists, and the discipline that turns heuristics into defensible, revisable rationale ready for Part II.

The arc runs Bounds, Attention, Organizations, Discipline. Bounds state the cognitive frame first so that later sections do not smuggle as-if optimization back in under softer labels. Attention treats search costs and menu complexity as binding constraints on what can be considered. Organizations describes early pricing by exception—who decides, under what load, with which informal rules—before governance matrices are written. Discipline closes Part I by requiring that heuristics survive challenge and revision, so that Policy can inherit stated objectives and instruments rather than folklore. Soft behavioralism as a tour of laboratory anomalies is out of scope; the Carnegie tradition—Simon, Cyert, March, Williamson and neighbors—supplies the level of sophistication the book requires.

#### Bounds, Attention

Bounds replace as-if optimization where it does not bind. Satisficing, limited search, and organizational decision routines are not excuses for vagueness; they are positive claims about how pricing choices are made under cognitive load. The chapter uses them to explain why a firm can hold a coherent Strategy account—object, cost, value, interaction, uncertainty—and still fail to apply that account consistently when every price is an exception negotiated under time pressure. Integrity remains the frame for constraints on purely selfish optimization: efficiency in the use of scarce attention and accountability for contributions across the value chain, not an ethics sermon.

Attention is the scarce resource that often binds before capital does. Founder and early-team capacity to compare instruments, read signals, and revise lists is finite. Search costs rise with menu complexity; freemium paths, seat tiers, meters, and discount windows multiply the comparisons buyers and the firm have to make. A menu that is analytically elegant can still be operationally unusable if it exhausts the attention available to set, explain, and defend it. Behavior therefore treats menu design and process design as joint problems: what the firm can price is constrained by what it can attend to.

#### Organizations, Discipline

Organizations concerns early teams that price by exception. Decisions concentrate on a founder or a small circle; rationale lives in chat threads and memory; revisions arrive as one-off concessions. That pattern is not yet Policy. It is the organizational substrate Policy must either formalize or replace. Naming who decides, what counts as an exception, and how often exception queues clog is part of Strategy because it determines whether Interaction and Uncertainty analysis can be executed at all. Authority matrices, approval workflows, and written competitive playbooks belong in Part II; Behavior only sketches the pre-policy reality those instruments will later discipline.

Discipline is the Part I hand-off. Heuristics from Principles and practice under Uncertainty become defensible when the firm can state the rationale, the evidence threshold for revision, and the boundaries the heuristic must not cross. Defensible does not mean identified. It means revisable without reinventing the Strategy account each week. That is the input Policy needs: objectives, constraints, and instruments that can be debated as policy rather than as personalities. Engine automation of decisions is later; Behavior stops at the human and organizational bounds that make written policy necessary.

The sequence Bounds, Attention, Organizations, Discipline is analytic. Cognitive limits without attention scarcity understate why menus fail in practice. Attention without organizational description understates who holds the scarce capacity. Organizations without discipline leave Part I as a diagnosis with no bridge to Policy. Closing with Discipline returns the reader to the book’s journey: Strategy reasoning that can survive thin data and limited minds must still become stated rules before an engine can inherit them.

#### Carnegie Scope

Behavior does not rewrite Uncertainty’s information account, does not write Part II governance or authority matrices, and does not automate pricing decisions. Optimization opens Policy with the grammar of human-controlled instruments. What this chapter owns is the Carnegie correction inside Strategy: bounds, attention, early organizations, and the disciplined hand-off from heuristic to revisable rationale.

### 6.1 Bounds

#### Procedural Reason

Bounds on rationality enter Strategy as a positive account of how pricing decisions are made, and the account comes from a specific tradition. Herbert Simon argued in the 1950s that a decision maker with limited knowledge and limited capacity to compute does not maximize over all alternatives. The decision maker searches among a few, evaluates them roughly, and stops when one is good enough. Simon later distinguished substantive rationality, judged by whether the chosen action is optimal, from procedural rationality, judged by whether the process that produced the action was reasonable given the information and computation available. Behavior adopts the second standard for founder pricing.

The standard changes what counts as an error. Under substantive rationality, a founder who sets a seat price without estimating the demand curve has failed. Under procedural rationality, the question is whether the process that produced the seat price used the evidence available, stated its assumptions, and specified when it would be revisited. The earlier chapters of Part I were written to this standard without naming it: Principles offered rules that survive thin data, Value asked for a conjecture rather than a curve, and Uncertainty asked for a stated prior and an evidence ranking. Each is a procedure a bounded team can carry out.

The tradition is narrower than the general label of behavioral economics. It concerns limits on search, attention, and computation, and the organizational routines that economize on them. It does not proceed by cataloguing laboratory anomalies and asking the firm to exploit them. Behavior sets that second program aside for an analytical reason: a catalogue of biases says little about how a pricing decision is made inside a firm, while the Carnegie account says a good deal.

#### Aspiration Levels

Satisficing, the term Simon introduced for search that stops at an acceptable option, depends on an aspiration level: the threshold an option must clear. Aspirations are not fixed. They rise after success and fall after failure, so the same founder may accept a discount in a slow quarter that would have been refused after a strong one. Principles recorded the organizational version from Cyert and March: routines are revised mainly when performance falls below aspiration. The implication for pricing is that the price a young firm charges reflects its recent experience as much as any estimate of willingness to pay.

Aspiration dependence explains a pattern that a pure optimization account treats as noise. A list price set at launch persists while revenue meets the founders' target, even when evidence accumulates that the price is low, because nothing triggers search. When a target is missed, search begins, but it begins in the neighborhood of the current problem, typically a discount or a new tier for the account that was lost, rather than in a review of the whole menu. Cyert and March called this problemistic search. The menu that results is a record of past shortfalls, each addressed locally.

The correction available to a bounded team is to make aspirations explicit and to attach search to them deliberately. A founder who writes down the conversion rate, contract size, or margin below which the price will be reconsidered has converted an implicit trigger into a stated one. The trigger remains a heuristic, and its adequacy can still be argued about. What changes is that the argument can occur before the shortfall rather than after it.

#### Reference Points

Two earlier sections sent questions here. Value noted that buyers punish prices they perceive as unfair, and Interaction noted that buyers judge quotes against reference prices. Both belong to the behavioral literature, and both deserve brief and cautious treatment. Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky's prospect theory describes choices as evaluated relative to a reference point, with losses weighing more than equivalent gains. Kahneman, Jack Knetsch, and Richard Thaler reported survey evidence in 1986 that people judge a price increase unfair when it exploits a shift in demand, and more acceptable when it passes on an increase in cost.

The findings bear on pricing in a limited way. They suggest that a reference price, once set by a list, a free tier, or a previous contract, constrains later moves, and that the stated reason for a change affects how it is received. Those are already consequences of the Strategy account: Interaction treated references as part of the environment buyers bring to a quote, and Value framed fairness as a problem of terms the firm can defend. The behavioral evidence adds a direction of effect, not a design rule. Its magnitudes vary across settings, and the survey evidence concerns judgments rather than measured purchasing.

The working conclusion is to treat reference dependence as a constraint on paths rather than as a lever. A firm that raises a price should expect the old price to remain the reference for some time, and should be able to state a reason the buyer can verify. A firm that sets a launch price should expect that price to become a reference it may later have to move against. Neither conclusion requires the firm to model buyer psychology in detail. Both require it to remember that every posted price teaches buyers what to expect next, a point the rest of the chapter extends to the firm's own decision process.

### 6.2 Attention

#### Founder Load

Attention is the input that binds first in a young technology firm. Simon put the point in a sentence from 1971 often quoted in discussions of information systems: "a wealth of information creates a poverty of attention." A founder who prices by hand is also hiring, selling, shipping, and raising capital, and the time available to compare instruments, read signals, and revise a list is a small residual. Pricing decisions are therefore made under load, in short intervals, often in response to a specific buyer rather than as part of a review.

Load changes what a reasonable process looks like. A procedure that requires the founder to re-estimate willingness to pay for every segment before each quote will not be followed; it will be replaced, without announcement, by whatever rule is quickest to apply. The relevant design question is which procedure will actually be executed by the people available in the time available. A rule of the form "quote list, discount at most one tier, record the reason" is coarse, but it may be the best procedure a team of four can sustain, and a coarse procedure followed consistently produces evidence that an elaborate procedure followed occasionally does not.

The attention budget also explains why pricing is revisited rarely in young firms even when the earlier chapters would recommend frequent revision. Revision competes with every other claim on the founders' time, and the cost of revisiting a price is concentrated and immediate while the benefit is diffuse and delayed. Integrity, in the sense the book uses, applies to this allocation as well: attention spent defending an indefensible price, or negotiating each exception from first principles, is attention withdrawn from the product and the chain that produces it.

#### Menu Costs

Every instrument on a price menu has an attention cost on both sides of the transaction. On the firm's side, each tier, meter, add-on, and discount window must be explained, implemented in billing, supported when it fails, and revisited when conditions change. On the buyer's side, each option adds a comparison. A menu of three seat tiers, two usage meters, an annual prepay discount, and a startup program presents the buyer with a problem that the buyer, also bounded, will solve by heuristic, typically by anchoring on the middle option or by asking a salesperson.

Economists use the term menu costs for the physical and administrative cost of changing prices. The cost that matters here is broader: the cognitive cost of a menu's complexity, borne whenever anyone has to use it. A menu that is analytically elegant, in the sense that each option separates a segment Value identified, can still fail if neither the sales team nor the buyer can hold its structure in mind. Separation that buyers cannot perceive does not separate.

An invented illustration shows the trade. Suppose a firm offers five plans, and a review of fifty recent deals finds that thirty were closed on a plan other than the one the buyer first asked about, after a call in which the founder explained the differences. If each such call takes an hour, the menu consumes thirty founder-hours per fifty deals. Collapsing the menu to three plans may lose some separation between segments; it also returns most of those hours. Whether the trade is worthwhile depends on the founder's alternative use of the time, which is an opportunity cost of exactly the kind Principles placed at the center of pricing under thin data.

#### Buyer Search

The buyer's attention is limited in the same way, and the limits shape demand. Buyers search until an option clears their aspiration level, as Value recorded, and the cost of search determines how many options they examine. A buyer for whom evaluating a new vendor takes weeks of engineering time compares few vendors and stays with an adequate incumbent; a buyer for whom a trial takes minutes compares many. Search cost is therefore part of the competitive environment Interaction described, and it can be shaped by the firm's own choices.

Free tiers, trials, and public price pages lower search cost for buyers considering the firm. Complex quotes, gated pricing, and bespoke contracts raise it. Neither direction is correct in general. Lower search cost attracts buyers who would not otherwise have considered the product, and it also makes comparison with rivals easier. Higher search cost protects margins with buyers already engaged, and it also deters those who will not invest the time. The choice is a Strategy decision about which buyers the firm wants to reach and what it is willing to concede to reach them.

The connection to Uncertainty is direct. Signals gathered from buyers are shaped by how those buyers searched. A conversion rate from a public page with a free tier reflects buyers who arrived cheaply and decided quickly; a conversion rate from enterprise quotes reflects buyers who invested heavily before the price was revealed. Treating the two as evidence about the same willingness to pay repeats the selection error Signals warned against, now produced by differences in attention rather than by sampling alone.

### 6.3 Organizations

#### Exception Culture

Early teams price by exception. A list exists, but most revenue arrives through deals that depart from it: a discount for a reference customer, an extended trial for a large prospect, a custom meter for a buyer whose usage does not fit the plans. Each departure is reasonable when made. Collectively they form the actual pricing of the firm, and that pricing is recorded, if at all, in messages, contract files, and the memory of whoever negotiated each deal.

James March and Herbert Simon, in Organizations, described how organizations handle recurring decisions through programs: stored routines that specify what to do when a familiar stimulus appears. Programs economize on attention by removing the need to deliberate each time. An early firm has few programs for pricing. Every deal that departs from the list is handled as a new problem, which is expensive in attention and produces outcomes that depend on who handled the deal and when. The exception culture is the absence of programs in a domain where decisions recur.

The culture has a rationale. Programs written too early encode guesses as rules, and Uncertainty argued that early beliefs should be held loosely. Exceptions preserve flexibility while the firm learns which situations recur. The cost is that learning from exceptions requires a record of them, and an exception culture typically keeps no such record. The firm accumulates experience without accumulating evidence, and the same departure is negotiated afresh by different people who do not know it has been granted before.

#### Who Decides

In a firm of a few people, pricing authority usually rests with a founder, often the one closest to sales. As the firm grows, authority diffuses without being assigned. A salesperson grants a discount because a previous one was approved; an engineer agrees to a usage allowance during a technical call; a support lead extends a trial to resolve a complaint. Each action is a pricing decision. None was made by the person who set the list, and none appears in the account the founders give of how the firm prices.

March and Simon also described uncertainty absorption: as information passes through an organization, inferences are drawn from evidence and then transmitted in place of the evidence. In pricing, the salesperson who reports that a prospect will not pay above a figure has absorbed the uncertainty of a negotiation into a single number, and the founder who approves a discount on that basis is deciding on an inference rather than on what the buyer said. The process is unavoidable in any organization that divides labor. It becomes a problem when nobody knows which figures are evidence and which are inferences.

Cyert and March added that firms are coalitions whose members hold different goals, and that conflicts among them are often resolved only partially, by attending to goals one at a time. Sales may treat volume as the goal, finance margin, and product adoption. Each group sets prices that serve its goal when the decision falls to it. The resulting menu reflects which group decided each case rather than any stated objective. Behavior records this pattern. Assigning authority to resolve it is the work of Policy, and the chapter does not undertake it.

#### Firm Boundaries

Oliver Williamson built on Simon's bounded rationality to explain why some transactions are organized inside firms and others through markets. When parties cannot foresee every contingency and may act opportunistically, contracts are incomplete, and the form of organization chosen to govern a transaction affects its cost. Pricing decisions in a young technology firm are shaped by this logic in two places: in the contracts the firm writes with buyers, and in the decisions it keeps inside rather than delegates to partners.

Contracts with buyers are incomplete for the reasons Williamson identified. A usage-based contract cannot anticipate every pattern of usage; an enterprise agreement cannot specify every future feature or support need. The firm responds by leaving terms open to renegotiation, which shifts pricing from the moment of sale to a series of later exceptions. Incompleteness is therefore one of the sources of the exception culture, and it cannot be eliminated by more careful drafting alone, because the contingencies that matter are often the ones nobody foresaw.

Decisions delegated outside the firm raise the same issue in reverse. A reseller, marketplace, or channel partner that sets the final price to the buyer acquires pricing authority the firm does not monitor directly. Interaction treated such arrangements as strategic choices among sides and rivals; Behavior adds that each delegation also moves pricing decisions to people with different information, attention, and goals. The firm's account of its own pricing has to include those decisions if it is to describe what buyers actually pay.

### 6.4 Discipline

#### Stated Rationale

Discipline is the step that turns a heuristic into something an organization can inherit. A heuristic used by one founder is a habit; the same heuristic written down with its reason, its evidence, and its limits is a rationale that others can apply, question, and revise. Principles described heuristics as portable rules with economic content. Discipline asks that the content be made explicit, so that the rule can survive the departure of the person who invented it and the arrival of evidence that contradicts it.

A stated rationale has a small number of parts. It names the instrument and the rule, for instance an annual prepay discount of a given size. It states the economic reason, here the cost of capital and the reduction in churn the firm expects. It records the evidence on which the rule rests and how strong that evidence is, using the ranking Uncertainty proposed. And it states the boundary: the situations in which the rule does not apply and a different decision is required. None of these parts requires data the firm lacks. Each requires only that the firm say what it already believes.

Writing the rationale also exposes rules that cannot be defended. A discount granted because a competitor offers one, without any view of whether the competitor's buyers are the firm's buyers, has no rationale beyond imitation. A free tier sized because a well-known company uses the same allowance has no rationale beyond analogy. Neither rule is necessarily wrong. Both are undefended, and the act of stating rationales makes the undefended rules visible, which is the first condition for deciding whether to keep them.

#### Revision Triggers

A defensible heuristic is a rule the firm can revise without reopening the whole Strategy account each time evidence arrives; it need not be an identified estimate. Revision therefore needs triggers, and the triggers should be set when the rule is adopted rather than after it fails. The aspiration levels described under Bounds are the natural candidates: a conversion rate, a contract size, a margin, or a share of deals closed off-list below which the rule is reviewed.

Triggers serve the bounded organization in two ways. They economize on attention, because a rule with a trigger need not be reviewed until the trigger is reached. And they counter the asymmetry Cyert and March described, in which routines are revised mainly after failure. A trigger can be set on the upside as well: a conversion rate well above expectation is evidence that the price may be low, and a firm that reviews prices only after shortfalls will never notice.

An invented illustration shows the form. A firm adopts the rule that startup accounts receive half off list for the first year, on the rationale that early adopters provide references and feedback worth more than the forgone revenue. It records two triggers: review the rule if more than a third of new accounts qualify, because the discount has then become the effective price, and review it if fewer than one in five discounted accounts provide a reference within the year, because the stated benefit has then failed to appear. Neither trigger requires a demand curve. Both convert the rationale into a claim that evidence can contradict.

#### Policy Handoff

Part I has treated pricing as a problem of reasoning. Foundations fixed the object and the binding cost; Principles supplied rules that survive thin data; Value stated what can be captured and on what terms; Interaction placed the firm among buyers, rivals, and sides; Uncertainty set standards for beliefs and irreversible moves; and Behavior has located the limits of the minds and organizations that carry out the reasoning. The result is an account a founder can use, and also an account of why its use breaks down as the firm grows.

The breakdown has a definite form. Exceptions multiply, authority diffuses, rationales stay unwritten, and the menu comes to record the history of the firm's negotiations rather than any stated objective. The disciplined heuristics described in this section are the material that resists that drift. They are rules with reasons, evidence, boundaries, and triggers, and they can be collected, compared, and made consistent with one another.

Policy begins with that collection. Its first task, taken up in Optimization, is to state the objectives the rules serve, the constraints that bind them, and the instruments they govern, so that the rules can be judged against a common grammar rather than one at a time. Assigning authority, auditing decisions, and bounding exceptions follow in later Policy chapters, and Behavior does not anticipate their form. What Strategy hands over is the reasoning those chapters require: a set of defensible, revisable rules and an account of the bounded people who will apply them.

# Part II — Policy

## Introduction
### Explicit Rules

Part II of Pricing Tech concerns Policy: making pricing decisions explicit while a human still owns them. Part I rebuilt Strategy—the economic reasoning behind founder-led choices under thin data—so that freemium, early bird, and related instruments can be read as structured conjectures rather than folklore. That recovery is necessary, but it does not by itself constitute an operating system for a growing firm.

As the firm grows, product lines multiply, segments appear, rivals become identifiable, and experiments become cheaper. Item-by-item improvisation by the founder ceases to work as a way of running the business. Sales requires a discount rule it can follow; finance requires a rationale it can audit; product requires version fences that hold across deals. Policy occupies the middle of the book's sequence from Strategy through Policy to Engine: still under human control, more formal than founding judgment, and short of an autonomous map from environment to a real-time price vector.

The purpose of Policy is revisability rather than procedure for its own sake. A price choice that cannot be stated, debated, and revised without reconstructing its rationale each week will not survive a larger organization. Policy converts Strategy's recovered reasoning into something the firm can teach, contest, and change deliberately. In Carnegie terms, written rules economize on scarce attention: they let many boundedly rational participants coordinate without each one re-deriving the founder's reasoning.

### Policy Content

Policy begins by stating what the firm is trying to achieve with price—an objective—and what constrains that aim: margin floors, the integrity of promises along the value chain, capacity and support limits, regulatory envelopes. It names instruments: list price, discount, meter, bundle. It keeps a human in the loop. Optimization without governance leaves every decision open to renegotiation; governance without a stated objective has nothing to enforce.

From there, who pays which price becomes policy rather than habit. Segmentation encodes the choice between observable characteristics and self-selection, and weighs cost-to-serve jointly with willingness to pay. Differentiation builds the lines, versions, and bundles that make segments hold, particularly for information goods, where versioning invites adverse selection if fences are weak. Competition is written as a stance on when to match, soften, or contest a rival's move, without appeals to financial endurance as strategy. Learning closes the loop: experiments the firm can afford, updating that resists overfitting, memory that survives turnover, and revision schedules that improve policy without assuming the data have suddenly become rich.

Integrity remains the frame for constraints on purely self-interested optimization: efficiency in the use of resources and accountability for contributions across a multi-step chain. Notation stays light; the analysis sits above management slogans and below full formal derivation, and full treatments are left to sources suited to them.

Policy is also where growth appears as friction. A founder can hold an entire pricing rationale in one head; a twenty-person go-to-market team cannot. Without written objectives and owners, each discount reopens past negotiations. Without segmentation and differentiation stated as policy, each customer becomes a special case. Without a competitive stance, each rival move is handled as an emergency. Without learning rules, each test is an isolated event that leaves no record. The chapters ahead treat these failures as problems of organizational design rather than of individual competence.

### Part Sequence

Optimization supplies the grammar: objectives, constraints, instruments, control. Governance assigns authority, audit, exceptions, and integrity as an operating constraint, so that the grammar has owners. Segmentation turns the question of who pays what into durable rules. Differentiation shapes what is sold to different buyers so those rules can hold under imitation. Competition positions the firm against rivals as revisable policy. Learning makes the apparatus improve over time, still under human control and still short of an unsupervised engine.

The order follows logical dependence. The firm cannot govern what it has not stated. It cannot segment or differentiate coherently without governance. Competition without learning repeats the same contest. Learning without the earlier grammar optimizes the wrong objective with better measurement.

Typical problems illustrate the scope: replacing founder discretion with a discount policy sales will follow; choosing how to discriminate when observables are noisy; versioning an information good without inviting adverse selection; responding to a rival's price cut on the basis of stated policy; and running a test the organization can interpret and then retire.

### Policy Limits

Policy inherits Strategy's thin-data foundations and does not re-derive them. It stops before the Engine: it does not bootstrap machine-readable loops at portfolio scale, build a simulation stack that replaces judgment, or compute real-time price vectors across hundreds of SKUs. Autonomy is excluded by design. When item-by-item human control fails, Part III takes the rules written here and asks what must be encoded, supervised, and held accountable. Until then, Policy's claim is limited and specific: prices the firm can explain, assign to an owner, and revise, without waiting for complete information and without reverting to folklore.

## Chapter 7 — Optimization

### Introduction
#### Grammar

Chapter 7 of Pricing Tech is Optimization. Behavior closed Part I by locating Carnegie bounds on cognition and early organization, and by requiring that heuristics become defensible, revisable rationale. Policy begins where that hand-off lands: the firm now has to state what it is trying to do with price, what binds that ambition, which instruments it will use, and how a human remains in control. Optimization is the grammar of that statement. Governance of who may change which field comes later, as does an engine that maps environment to a real-time price vector.

The arc runs Objectives, Constraints, Instruments, Control. Objectives come first because instruments without a stated aim cannot be debated as policy. Constraints follow because an objective that ignores margin floors, promise integrity, capacity, or regulatory envelopes is not operational. Instruments map the levers—list, discount, meter, bundle—onto those aims and binds. Control keeps the model as an aid under human override rather than as a substitute for ownership. Governance will later assign authority and audit; Engine will later automate under inherited rules. This chapter owns only the grammar those later chapters presuppose.

#### Stated Aims

An objective is a stated claim about what success means for price over a horizon the firm can argue about: revenue, margin mix, adoption, contribution along a chain, or an explicit multi-goal tension among them. Strategy recovered reasons why freemium, early bird, and related instruments can be rational under thin data. Policy requires those reasons to be written as aims that sales, finance, and product can challenge without reinventing the Strategy account each week. Multi-goal tension is normal; leaving it unnamed is how exception culture returns under a new label.

Constraints are the binds that belong in the model rather than in informal understanding. Margin floors, integrity of promises along multi-step value chains, support and capacity limits, and regulatory envelopes restrict the feasible set. Integrity here remains efficiency in the use of resources and accountability for contributions—not an ethics sermon. A constraint that cannot be stated cannot be audited; a constraint that is only felt appears later as an unexplained veto. Optimization treats constraints as first-class objects beside the objective.

#### Levers

Instruments are the levers Policy will govern: list price, discounts, meters, and bundles, with freemium and early-bird windows treated as structured uses of those levers rather than as folklore. Mapping instruments to objectives and constraints is the chapter’s constructive core.
