A discount that maximizes short-run close rate while violating a margin floor or teaching buyers to wait is an instrument used against the stated grammar rather than an aligned one. Thin data from Uncertainty still limit identification; Optimization does not pretend the spreadsheet is suddenly rich. It requires that instrument choice be justifiable against the written objective and binds even when evidence is sparse.

Control keeps a human in the loop. Models may rank options, score exceptions, or propose list moves; override rights and the obligation to record why remain with people who own the decision. Black-box substitution—accepting a score because the dashboard said so—reopens the as-if optimization Behavior rejected, only now with better charts. Control is therefore part of the grammar: the firm states how recommendations become decisions, and what counts as a legitimate override, before Governance assigns seats and Engine inherits the loop.

The sequence Objectives, Constraints, Instruments, Control is analytic. Instruments without objectives invite tool worship. Objectives without constraints invite unconstrained slogans. Both without control invite automation that Policy cannot yet supervise. Closing with Control returns the reader to Part II’s premise: human-owned pricing that can be debated and revised, short of unsupervised autonomy.

#### Grammar Limits

Optimization does not assign organizational authority or write exception matrices—that is Governance. It does not engineer autonomy or real-time portfolio vectors—that is Engine. It does not redo Behavior’s Carnegie account. What this chapter owns is the Policy grammar: stated aims, binding constraints, mapped instruments, and human control of the model as aid.

### 7.1 Objectives

#### Success Measures

Behavior ended with a collection of rules, each carrying a reason, a boundary, and a trigger for revision. A collection is not yet a policy. Two rules can each be defensible and still pull against each other: an annual prepay discount justified by the cost of capital and a startup discount justified by references may, applied to the same account, stack into a price no one intended. Rules become comparable only when they answer to a common statement of what price is meant to achieve. That statement is the objective, and writing it down is the first act of Policy.

An objective names a quantity and a direction. Revenue over the next four quarters, contribution margin per account, the number of paying accounts in a target segment, or contribution earned across a multi-step chain are all candidates. Each implies a different best price. Revenue rewards volume at prices that may leave little margin; contribution rewards price at the expense of reach; account counts reward adoption whether or not the accounts ever pay well. Principles already argued that a pricing decision is judged at the margin, against the next-best alternative and the binding cost. Optimization takes that argument as given and asks the firm to state which version of it governs, and over what population of decisions.

A useful objective has three properties. It is measurable with data the firm already collects, so that success can be checked rather than asserted. It is attributable, in that pricing decisions plausibly move it more than unrelated forces do. And it is contestable: sales, finance, and product can each read it and say where it would lead them to act differently. An objective phrased as "capture fair value" fails all three. An objective phrased as "maximize gross contribution from new annual contracts, subject to the floors in 7.2" passes them, and it can be argued about, which is the purpose of writing it.

#### Time Horizons

Every objective carries a horizon, stated or not, and the unstated horizon is usually the current quarter. Uncertainty showed why that default is costly. A price that raises this quarter's contribution may teach buyers to wait for the next promotion, reveal a willingness to concede that rivals then exploit, or consume the reference value of a list price that took years to establish. Each of these effects arrives later than the revenue and is invisible in a short-horizon measure.

Stating the horizon converts these effects from background worries into terms of the objective. A firm that writes "contribution over the expected life of the account" has committed to valuing retention and expansion alongside the first invoice, and to estimating them, however roughly. A firm that writes "contribution this fiscal year" has made the opposite choice and can at least defend it, for instance when cash is short and the option value of survival dominates. Both are legitimate. What fails is an objective whose horizon shifts with the person reading it, because then every disagreement about a discount is in fact a disagreement about time that no one has named.

Thin data limit how far forward the estimate can reach. The response Uncertainty proposed applies here: state the belief, rank the evidence behind it, and record what observation would change it. A lifetime-value term built on six months of churn data is weak evidence, and the objective should say so, but leaving the term out does not make the estimate better. It sets the value of the future to zero, which is the one estimate the firm can be sure is wrong.

#### Pareto Tension

Most firms hold more than one aim for price. Adoption and margin conflict at low prices; margin and retention conflict at high ones; a platform's two sides, as Interaction described, may each require a different level. The tension is normal. The failure lies in leaving it unnamed, so that each decision settles the tradeoff implicitly and differently, and the pattern of exceptions becomes the firm's true objective without anyone having chosen it.

Light formalism clarifies the choice. Suppose two aims, margin and adoption, and a set of feasible pricing policies. A policy is Pareto-efficient, in the sense that takes its name from Vilfredo Pareto, if no other feasible policy improves one aim without worsening the other. The efficient policies form a frontier. Any policy inside the frontier is simply wasteful, since some alternative is better on both counts. Choosing among policies on the frontier, by contrast, requires a judgment about how much of one aim is worth how much of the other. Analysis can locate the frontier; it cannot choose a point on it.

The firm has two common ways to make that judgment explicit. One assigns weights and maximizes a combined score, for example contribution plus a stated value per new account. The other maximizes one aim and imposes a floor on the rest, for example contribution subject to at least a stated number of new accounts per quarter. The two are closely related, and the second often proves easier to discuss because the floor is a concrete commitment rather than an abstract exchange rate. As an invented illustration, a firm that values a new startup account at 400 in future contribution can accept a first-year discount costing up to 400 per account, and no more. Either form turns a recurring argument into a parameter that can be reviewed when evidence about account value improves, and the floors of the second form lead directly into constraints.

### 7.2 Constraints

#### Margin Floors

An objective says what to pursue; constraints say what may not be traded away in the pursuit. The distinction matters because a constraint is honored in full or broken, while an objective is traded off at the margin. A firm that writes a margin requirement as a weight in its objective accepts that a sufficiently large gain elsewhere can justify breaching it. A firm that writes it as a floor does not. Choosing which form a requirement takes is itself a policy decision, and Optimization asks that it be made deliberately.

Margin floors are the most familiar constraint and the most often misstated. Principles located the binding cost as the opportunity cost of the scarce resource, not the accounting average. A floor set at average cost plus a markup will be too high for an information good whose marginal cost is close to zero and too low for a service whose delivery consumes a scarce engineer. A defensible floor states which cost it protects, on which unit, and why. For a seat-based product it may be the cost of support per seat; for a usage-based product, the infrastructure cost per unit of the meter; for a services-heavy contract, the value of the delivery capacity the contract will consume.

Floors also need a scope. A floor that applies to every line item will block bundles in which one component is priced low to carry another, a structure Interaction showed can be efficient when one component or side draws participation for another. A floor on the contract as a whole permits such cross-subsidy while still preventing the deal from destroying value. The scope belongs in the written constraint, alongside the number.

#### Promise Integrity

Some constraints protect the price system rather than any single deal. Value described integrity as efficiency in the use of resources and accountability for contributions along a multi-step chain. In Optimization terms, integrity enters as constraints on what one decision may do to promises made in others. A grandfathered rate promised to early customers, a most-favored-customer clause in an enterprise contract, a published list price that channel partners rely on, and a revenue share owed to a marketplace are all commitments. A price chosen to maximize the objective in one account while violating them is infeasible, however attractive it appears in isolation.

Regulatory envelopes belong to the same family. Rules on price discrimination, disclosure, auto-renewal, and sector-specific tariffs bound the feasible set from outside. Regulation receives its own treatment in the Engine part; here it is enough that applicable rules be listed as constraints rather than left to legal review after the decision. Capacity and support limits complete the list. A launch price that would attract more accounts than onboarding can absorb, or a meter that would draw usage beyond infrastructure headroom, is constrained by the firm's own ability to deliver. Writing these limits down prevents the familiar pattern in which a constraint is felt but unstated, and surfaces only as an unexplained veto from the team that bears it.

#### Lagrange Multipliers

Light formalism shows what constraints cost. Write the pricing problem as a constrained program: choose prices p to maximize an objective f(p) subject to constraints g_i(p) ≤ c_i, one for each floor, promise, envelope, and capacity limit. Readers who prefer prose may skip to the next paragraph without loss. The method associated with Joseph-Louis Lagrange, extended to inequality constraints in the conditions now credited to Karush (1939) and Kuhn and Tucker (1951), attaches a multiplier λ_i to each constraint. At an optimum, each multiplier measures how much the objective would rise if its constraint were relaxed by one unit. A constraint that does not bind has a multiplier of zero.

These multipliers are shadow prices, the same object Principles introduced in 2.1 as the value of one more unit of a scarce resource. The connection gives the formalism its practical use. A firm rarely solves the program exactly, because Uncertainty has shown that the demand terms in f are poorly identified. It can still ask, for each constraint, roughly how much contribution it is giving up to honor it. A margin floor whose shadow price is large is a candidate for review: either the cost it protects has been overstated, or the floor is doing important work and its cost should be acknowledged openly.

An invented illustration makes the reading concrete. Suppose a support-capacity constraint limits new enterprise accounts to 20 per quarter, and the best estimate is that a 21st account would add 15,000 in contribution. The shadow price of support capacity is then about 15,000 per account-slot per quarter, and, if each added slot is worth roughly as much as the 21st, hiring that raises capacity by five accounts at a quarterly cost below 75,000 passes on contribution grounds. The numbers are invented; the structure is general. Constraints are part of the statement of an optimization problem rather than obstacles to it, and their shadow prices tell the firm which ones are expensive. That information is what the next step, the choice of instruments, has to work within.

### 7.3 Instruments

#### List Anchors

Instruments are the levers through which an objective is pursued within its constraints. Optimization names four that most technology firms use: list price, discounts, meters, and bundles. Freemium, early-bird windows, and annual prepay are configurations of these four rather than separate instruments, which is why Strategy could read them as structured conjectures and why Policy can now place them in a common grammar. Each lever moves a different part of the problem, and the useful question for each is which term of the objective it acts on and which constraint it is most likely to strain.

List price is the anchor. It sets the reference against which buyers judge every other offer, as Behavior described in its account of reference points, and it is the price the firm has publicly committed to. Its main effect is on the level of revenue across all buyers who pay it, and its main risk lies in the promise constraints of 7.2: a list change touches grandfathered rates, channel margins, and published comparisons at once. For that reason list price is the lever moved least often and with the most deliberation. A firm that changes list every quarter has turned its anchor into a discount, and loses the reference value the anchor provided.

A list price also carries information about positioning that no other lever conveys as cheaply. Interaction showed that rivals read published prices as signals. A list set well below a rival's announces a contest; one set at parity with discounts beneath announces that competition will happen in negotiation. Optimization does not decide which stance to take, which is the work of Competition, but it requires that the list be chosen with its signaling effect counted among its consequences.

#### Discount Rules

Discounts are the most flexible lever and the one most prone to drift. Behavior traced how exceptions accumulate when discounts are granted case by case. In the grammar of Optimization, a discount is legitimate when it serves a term of the stated objective within the constraints, and the rule granting it can say which term and which constraint. A volume discount that reflects lower cost-to-serve per seat serves contribution. A first-year discount for accounts with high reference value serves the adoption term weighted in 7.1. A discount granted because a deal is late in the quarter serves neither, and at best serves an unstated short horizon.

Writing discounts as rules rather than as allowances changes what can be checked. A rule states the condition that triggers the discount, its size or range, its duration, and the term of the objective it serves. Each element can then be compared against the outcome. Uncertainty warned that buyers who learn the conditions will adapt to them, and a discount that can be predicted will be waited for. Rules therefore carry the same revision triggers Behavior required of any heuristic: a threshold share of qualifying deals above which the discount is treated as the effective price. Who may grant a discount, and how exceptions are recorded, is left to Governance.

#### Meters, Bundles

A meter defines the unit on which a buyer pays: seats, transactions, compute, storage, outcomes. Choosing the meter is often a larger pricing decision than choosing the price per unit, because it determines how payment scales with value. Principles argued that a good meter tracks either the buyer's benefit or the firm's binding cost. A meter that tracks only benefit can leave margin exposed as usage grows, and one that tracks only cost may bear little relation to value; the stated objective decides which governs. Two-part structures, a fixed fee plus a usage charge, let a firm separate the two. Walter Oi's 1971 analysis of the "Disneyland dilemma" set out how an entry fee and a per-use price can be combined to capture surplus that a single price leaves behind.

Bundles combine products under one price. Adams and Yellen (1976) showed that bundling can raise a seller's profit when buyers' valuations for the components differ, because the bundle reduces dispersion in what buyers will pay for the whole. Bundles act on the revenue term, but they interact with constraints: a component priced low within a bundle may fall beneath its line-item floor, which is why 7.2 asked that floors carry a stated scope. How bundles are designed to separate buyers is the work of Differentiation.

The four levers are not independent. An invented illustration shows the interaction: a firm that raises its per-seat meter by 10 percent while holding list constant, and simultaneously widens its volume discount from 15 to 25 percent for large accounts, has lowered the effective price for those accounts while raising it for small ones. Mapping each lever to the objective and constraints one at a time misses such combinations. The mapping becomes a policy only when it states how the levers move together, and that statement is what a human must still be able to read, check, and override.

### 7.4 Control

#### Model Aid

Objectives, constraints, and instruments together form a model of the pricing problem, whether or not anyone writes it as an equation. Control concerns how that model relates to the people who own the decisions it informs. Policy, as Part II defines it, keeps a human in the loop. Optimization makes that commitment specific: the model ranks, estimates, and proposes; a named person decides, and the decision record states whether the proposal was followed.

The case for the model as aid rather than authority rests on what earlier chapters established. Uncertainty showed that the demand terms of the objective are poorly identified with thin data, so a model's recommendation is only as good as estimates that may be off by a large factor. Behavior showed that the people who run a firm work within bounded attention, and that this bound is the reason to write rules at all. A model relieves attention by narrowing the options and flagging the deals that depart from policy. It cannot also be trusted to know what it does not know, because the information it lacks is often exactly what a salesperson learned on the call.

Herbert Simon's distinction between substantive and procedural rationality, introduced in Behavior, applies to models as much as to people. A model's output is substantively optimal only within its assumptions. Procedural rationality asks whether the process that produced and accepted the output was reasonable given what was known. Control is the part of the grammar that makes the process inspectable: which inputs were used, which constraints were active, which proposal was made, and what the human did with it.

#### Override Rights

An override is a decision to depart from what the model proposes. Overrides are necessary, because the model omits information, and dangerous, because each one reopens the exception culture Behavior described. Control resolves the tension by treating overrides as data. Each override is recorded with its reason, stated in the terms of the grammar: which term of the objective the model misjudged, which constraint it failed to represent, or which instrument it used inappropriately. A reason given in those terms can be checked later; a reason given as general judgment cannot.

Recorded overrides improve the model over time. If a class of overrides consistently cites the same omitted constraint, for example a capacity limit the model does not represent, the constraint belongs in the model. If overrides in a class consistently lose contribution relative to the proposal, the reason they cite is weaker than it seemed. The pattern of overrides thus becomes evidence about the model, in the sense of the evidence ranking Uncertainty proposed, and the model's revision becomes a routine rather than an argument. As an invented illustration, a firm that finds 40 percent of its enterprise proposals overridden, mostly citing implementation cost, has learned that its objective omits a cost term, and the override rate is the measure that shows it.

The authority to override, the thresholds above which an override needs approval, and the audit of overrides after the fact are questions of Governance. Optimization requires only that the right exist, that its use be recorded, and that the record be stated in the model's own terms.

#### Open Models

A model that cannot be explained cannot be overridden on reasoned grounds. Black-box substitution, accepting a recommendation because a dashboard produced it, returns the firm to the as-if optimization that Behavior rejected, with better presentation. The person who accepts an unexplained recommendation has delegated the decision while keeping responsibility for it, a combination that fails the accountability standard Value set for integrity.

Openness here means something modest. The objective, with its weights or floors, is written in plain terms. The active constraints at each recommendation are listed, together with rough shadow prices where the firm can estimate them, so that the reader knows which limit is shaping the result. The instruments the model is allowed to move are named, as are those it is not. And the evidence behind each demand estimate carries its rank. A sales lead or finance partner should be able to read a recommendation and state, in a sentence, why the model proposed it. If no one can, the recommendation is treated as a proposal without a reason and carries no more weight than an unrecorded hunch. Openness also has a cost: a model simple enough to explain may fit the data less closely than an opaque one. Under thin data that loss is usually small, because a closer fit to a few observations is weak evidence of a better price.

Control closes Optimization by returning to the premise of Part II: prices that can be stated, debated, and revised while a human still owns them. The grammar is now complete in outline. The objective names what price serves and over what horizon; constraints state what may not be traded away and what each costs; instruments map the levers onto both; control keeps the model answerable to the people who use it. What the grammar lacks is owners. Assigning who may set which parameter, who audits the record, and how exceptions are bounded is the task Governance takes up next.

## Chapter 8 — Governance

### Introduction
#### Owners

Chapter 8 of Pricing Tech is Governance. Optimization stated the Policy grammar: objectives, constraints, instruments, and human control of the model as aid. Grammar without owners becomes a free-for-all. Behavior already described early organizations that price by exception—rationale in chat threads, one-off concessions, clogged queues. Governance is the Policy answer to that substrate: who may change which price field, how changes are reconstructed, how exceptions are bounded, and how integrity operates as a constraint rather than as a sermon. Segmentation, differentiation, and competitive stance will later be written under this apparatus; they are not designed here.

The arc runs Authority, Audit, Exceptions, Integrity. Authority assigns rights across list, deal desk, meters, and escalation. Audit requires records that sales and finance can use to reconstruct why a price exists. Exceptions define when deviation is allowed, how long it lasts, and how patterns of exception become evidence of policy drift. Integrity closes by operationalizing efficiency in resource use and accountability for contributions along the value chain as binds on those rights and escapes. Engine inheritance of governance is later; this chapter writes the human-controlled layer Engine must not erase.

#### Authority, Audit

Authority answers who may change price, on which instrument, and by what escalation path. List changes, discount bands, meter definitions, and bundle fences often need different owners; collapsing them into a single “pricing person” recreates founder improvisation under a new title. Role clarity is not bureaucracy for its own sake. It is the condition under which Optimization’s override rights mean something concrete: a recorded decision by a named seat, not an impression formed in a hallway. Escalation paths matter when speed and consistency conflict; Governance states which conflicts escalate rather than leaving every conflict to personality.

Audit is reconstructability. What was changed, by whom, against which objective and constraint, and with which evidence threshold must be recoverable after the fact. Finance and sales need views that can settle disputes without re-litigating Strategy from scratch. Thin data from Uncertainty still limit how much can be proven; Audit does not invent identification. It requires that the firm’s stated rationale and the actual instrument move can be compared. A price that cannot be reconstructed cannot be revised on purpose, only renegotiated again.

#### Exceptions, Integrity

Exceptions are escape valves with expiry and pattern detection. Unlimited exceptions dissolve Policy back into Behavior’s exception culture. Zero exceptions kill speed in markets that move faster than the approval calendar. Governance bounds when an exception is allowed, how it expires, and how repeated exceptions on the same instrument become a signal to revise policy rather than to normalize drift. Pattern detection is organizational learning at the Policy layer: the exception log is evidence, not only noise.

Integrity is the operating constraint that sits on authority and exceptions. Efficiency in the use of scarce attention, capacity, and contribution; accountability for who captures what along multi-step chains—these are the integrity frame Value defined, applied in Optimization, now enforced as governance binds rather than as aspirational prose. An ethics sermon is out of scope. Contested promises, untraceable discounts, and instruments that systematically shift cost onto unmeasured parties fail integrity as Policy objects: they cannot be audited cleanly and they cannot be escalated honestly.

The sequence Authority, Audit, Exceptions, Integrity is analytic. Rights without records invite unaccountable speed. Records without exception rules invite either paralysis or silent drift. Both without integrity leave Optimization’s constraints as decoration. Closing with Integrity returns the reader to Part II’s premise: human-controlled rules that remain revisable because ownership and evidence are explicit.

#### Owner Scope

Governance does not design segments, version fences, or competitive stance—those follow under the apparatus this chapter installs. It does not automate decisions or replace Policy with an Engine loop. It does not restate Optimization’s objective–instrument grammar except where ownership attaches. What this chapter owns is the control layer: authority, audit, bounded exceptions, and integrity as operating constraint.

### 8.1 Authority

#### Decision Rights

Optimization ended with a grammar that had no owners. Authority supplies them. A decision right, in the sense used here, is the standing permission of a named role to change a specific price field within stated limits, together with the obligation to record that change. The definition has three parts, and each matters. The role is named rather than the person, so that the right survives turnover. The field is specific, so that a right over list prices does not quietly extend to meter definitions. And the limits are stated, so that the edge of the right is visible before anyone reaches it.

The case for dividing rights rests on the agency problem. Michael Jensen and William Meckling (1976) defined agency costs as the sum of the principal's monitoring expenditure, the agent's bonding expenditure, and the residual loss that remains when the agent's decisions still differ from those the principal would have made. Pricing authority is a clear instance. A salesperson paid on bookings gains from a deeper discount that closes a deal this quarter; the firm bears the lower margin on every renewal that follows. Neither party needs to act in bad faith for the divergence to be costly. Divided rights do not remove that divergence; they cap the residual loss by bounding how far any single agent can move a price before someone with a different incentive must agree.

Eugene Fama and Michael Jensen (1983) added a distinction that suits pricing well. They separated decision management, meaning the initiation and implementation of decisions, from decision control, meaning their ratification and monitoring. Their argument was that organizations whose decision makers do not bear the main wealth effects of their choices tend to separate the two functions. A pricing function that lets the same seat propose a discount, approve it, and later review its results has merged management and control, and the review it performs is a formality.

#### Deal Desk

The instruments Optimization named need different owners because they fail in different ways. List prices are public commitments that anchor every negotiation, so changes to them are rare, visible, and best held by a small group with a view across products. Discount bands are where most variation occurs, and authority over them is usually tiered by depth. Meter definitions decide what a buyer is charged for, and a change to one reprices every account on that meter at once; that right belongs with whoever can see the whole installed base, not with whoever is closing the next deal. Bundle fences decide which components travel together, and their owner needs to know the cost of support and delivery as well as the price.

A deal desk is the usual institution for the middle tier. It reviews nonstandard terms against policy, prices them with the model's proposal in view, and either approves within its band or escalates. Its value lies in consistency: one group sees many deals and can tell whether a requested concession is a market signal or a negotiating habit. As an invented illustration, consider a firm that closes 200 deals a quarter. Representatives may grant up to 10 percent off list, sales managers up to 20 percent, and the deal desk up to 30 percent; anything deeper goes to a named finance owner. If 150 deals close within the representative band, 40 within the manager band, and 10 reach the desk, then 150 + 40 + 10 = 200 and the desk reviews 5 percent of volume. That share is small enough for careful review and large enough to show where the bands pinch.

Authority over the model itself is a separate right. Who may change an objective weight, a constraint floor, or the evidence behind a demand estimate shapes every subsequent proposal. In a young organization those rights often sit with the person who built the spreadsheet, by default rather than by decision. Writing them down is the first act of Governance.

#### Escalation Paths

Escalation resolves conflicts that the rights themselves cannot. Most such conflicts pit speed against consistency: a buyer needs an answer today, and the answer that policy gives is not the one the representative believes will close. An escalation path states which conflicts move up, to whom, and within what time. Without a stated path every conflict moves to whoever is most senior and available, which recreates the founder bottleneck that Behavior described under a different title.

Philippe Aghion and Jean Tirole (1997) distinguished formal authority, the right to decide, from real authority, effective control over decisions. In their model a principal with formal authority often defers to a better-informed agent, and real authority drifts toward whoever holds the relevant information. Pricing shows the drift plainly. A finance owner with formal authority over deep discounts who lacks time to read deal notes will approve what the representative proposes, and real authority has moved to the field without anyone deciding that it should. The remedy is to keep the request informative enough, stated in the terms of the grammar, that formal authority can be exercised within the time allowed.

Role clarity also protects speed. A representative who knows exactly where the band ends can commit inside it without asking. Ambiguity slows both sides: every deal near an unclear edge is escalated defensively, and the queue lengthens for reasons that have nothing to do with price. Clear rights are thus a condition of fast pricing rather than an obstacle to it. What the rights still lack is a record, because a decision right that leaves no trace cannot be reviewed, and a right that cannot be reviewed has no practical limit. Audit supplies that record.

### 8.2 Audit

#### Change Records

Audit is reconstructability: the capacity to recover, after the fact, why a price stands where it does. A price that cannot be reconstructed can be renegotiated but cannot be revised on purpose, because no one knows which assumption to change. The record that makes reconstruction possible is modest in scope. For each change to a price field it holds the field and its old and new values, the role that made the change and the right under which it was made, the date and the accounts affected, the objective term or constraint the change was meant to serve, and the evidence cited, with its rank under the ordering Uncertainty proposed. Where the model made a proposal, the record also holds the proposal and whether it was followed, which is the override record Optimization required.

Each element answers a question that otherwise becomes an argument. The role and right show whether the change was authorized. The objective term shows what the change was supposed to achieve, so that its result can be compared with its purpose. The evidence rank shows how much confidence the change deserved when it was made, which protects a reasonable decision from being judged solely by an unlucky outcome. Without these elements, review collapses into recollection, and recollection favors whoever argues most confidently.

The record need not be elaborate. A structured entry in the system that already holds quotes and contracts serves better than a separate document, because entries made where the work happens are made at all. What matters is that the fields are fixed in advance. A free-text note invites the unstructured rationale Behavior found in early firms; a required field for the constraint served forces the person making the change to name one, and the act of naming is itself a check.

#### Two Views

Sales and finance read the same record for different purposes. Sales asks what can be offered to this buyer now and what precedent applies. Its view is organized by account and deal: the terms in force, the concessions granted and their expiry, the approvals already obtained. Finance asks what the firm's prices are actually doing in aggregate. Its view is organized by instrument and period: realized price against list, discount depth by band, the contribution lost or gained on overridden proposals, and the share of revenue on nonstandard terms.

A record that serves only one view will be bypassed by the other. A ledger built for finance that is slow to query by account leads representatives to keep their own spreadsheets, and the official record decays. A deal tool built for sales that cannot be summed by instrument leaves finance estimating realized price from invoices, months late. Designing the record once, with fields that support both summaries, is cheaper than reconciling two partial records later.

The two views also discipline each other. Steven Kerr (1975), in "On the Folly of Rewarding A, While Hoping for B," described reward systems that pay for one behavior while the organization hopes for another. A pricing record makes that folly visible. If the sales view shows that the deepest discounts cluster in the last week of each quarter while the finance view shows lower renewal margins on exactly those accounts, the compensation plan is pricing the product, whatever the policy says. Audit cannot change the plan, but it can show the pattern in terms both functions accept.

#### Disputes

Most pricing disputes are disagreements about facts presented as disagreements about judgment: whether a discount was approved, what a buyer was promised, which terms apply after an upgrade. A complete record settles these quickly. The remaining disputes concern judgment proper, such as whether a concession was wise, and the record narrows them to the evidence available at the time of the decision.

Two disciplines keep disputes from reopening Strategy. The first is to judge a decision against the record of what was known, under the procedural standard Behavior took from Simon, rather than against what was later learned. A discount granted on rank-three evidence that turned out badly was a reasonable bet if rank-three evidence was all the firm had; the lesson concerns the evidence, not the person. The second is to route a dispute about policy to the owner of the policy rather than settling it deal by deal. If two representatives dispute which band applies to a contract type, the answer belongs in the band definition, recorded once, and not in an email that resolves one case.

Audit does not create identification that thin data deny. A record can show that a discount was granted and that the account renewed; it cannot show that the discount caused the renewal. What it can show is whether the stated rationale and the actual instrument move agree. When they diverge, the divergence is itself information, either about the model or about the rights. As an invented illustration, a record showing that 30 of 120 discounts in a quarter cite competitive pressure, while only 6 of those 30 name a competitor, tells finance that 24 of the 30, or 80 percent, rest on an untested claim. That pattern is the material Exceptions takes up.

### 8.3 Exceptions

#### Escape Valves

An exception is a price or term outside the bands that Authority defines, granted under a right that exists for that purpose. Exceptions are necessary because policy is written in advance and markets are not. A buyer may bring a constraint the model does not represent, a competitor may change terms between reviews, or a strategic account may justify a concession that no band anticipated. A policy with no exceptions forces each such case either to wait for the next review or to be resolved by quiet rule-breaking, and the second outcome is the more common.

The opposite failure is equally familiar. When exceptions are unlimited, the bands describe the price list a firm publishes rather than the prices it charges, and Policy dissolves back into the exception culture that Behavior described. The design problem is to allow deviation at a cost that is low for the cases policy did not foresee and high for the cases it did.

Three conditions mark a well-formed exception. It is requested by the role with standing to request it and approved by a role with a different incentive, which carries the separation of management and control from Authority into the escape valve itself. It states which term of the grammar it departs from and why, in the same structured fields that Audit requires of any change. And it carries an expiry. The first two conditions make exceptions reviewable; the third keeps them from becoming policy without anyone deciding that they should.

#### Expiry Dates

An exception without an end date persists by default. Renewal is the moment when this matters most in subscription businesses: a first-year concession granted to win a reference account is carried into year two because no one is assigned to remove it, and by year three it is the account's price. Expiry reverses the default. The exception lapses on a stated date unless someone with authority renews it, and renewal is recorded as a new decision with its own rationale.

The length of an exception should match the reason for it. A concession granted to match a competitor's promotion should end when the promotion ends. A concession granted because the buyer's usage is still uncertain should end at the point where usage is measured. A concession granted to secure a strategic reference should end when the reference has been delivered, or be renewed with a statement of what the reference produced. Tying expiry to the reason, rather than to a uniform calendar, keeps each exception connected to the evidence that justified it.

Expiry also bounds the cost of error. An exception granted on weak evidence that proves mistaken costs the firm only until the expiry date, after which the default price returns without a negotiation. Bounded exposure makes it easier to grant exceptions quickly, which is the speed that the escape valve exists to provide. In this respect expiry and speed are complements: a desk that knows a concession will lapse can approve it with less deliberation than one whose approval is effectively permanent.

#### Drift Signals

Individual exceptions are local decisions. Their pattern is evidence about policy. When the same instrument attracts repeated exceptions for the same stated reason, either the policy is wrong for a class of cases or the rights are being used to evade it. Distinguishing the two is the work of pattern detection, and the exception log that Audit maintains is its raw material.

As an invented illustration, suppose a firm has 120 accounts on annual contracts and finds that 25 of them, about 21 percent, hold the same exception, a waiver of the minimum seat count, and that 18 of those 25 have renewed it at least once. A waiver held by roughly one account in five and renewed by most holders has ceased to be an exception. Either the minimum is set too high for a class of buyers the policy did not anticipate, in which case the minimum should change, or representatives use the waiver to close deals the policy was designed to decline, in which case the approval right is too loose. The log cannot settle which; it can show that one of the two holds and direct the policy owner to decide.

Such patterns separate two kinds of change that look identical in individual records. Policy revision is a decision by the owner of a field to change the field, with a rationale, after reviewing the evidence. Policy drift is the same change arrived at through an accumulation of exceptions that no one reviewed as a whole. The resulting prices may coincide, but only revision leaves the firm knowing why its prices are what they are. Drift also tends to move in one direction, toward deeper concessions, because each exception is argued by someone who gains from it and reviewed by someone with less time.

Pattern detection needs a threshold and an owner. A useful rule names the share of accounts or deals holding an exception on a given field above which the field's owner must review it, and a period within which the review occurs. The review produces one of three outcomes: revise the policy, tighten the right, or record why the pattern is acceptable. Each outcome enters the record, so that the next review starts from the last one. Exceptions treated this way act as a sensor on policy rather than a leak in it. The sensor still reads only what the rights and records allow it to see, and the question of whether those rights and records describe what the firm actually does is the subject of Integrity.

### 8.4 Integrity

#### Stated Practice

Value treated integrity in economic terms: efficient use of the resources a price structure consumes, and traceable accountability for contributions along the chain. Governance treats integrity as a property of the institution that sets prices. An institution has integrity in this sense when its stated policy and its practice coincide: the bands that Authority publishes are the bands actually used, the records Audit requires are the records actually kept, and the exceptions granted are the exceptions the rules allow. The definition says nothing about whether the policy is generous or strict. It concerns only whether the policy describes what happens.

The gap between the two has a name in organization theory. Nils Brunsson (1989), in The Organization of Hypocrisy, argued that organizations facing inconsistent demands often satisfy them separately, with talk addressed to one audience and action to another, and that this separation can be stable. Pricing invites the pattern. A firm can publish a list price to satisfy investors and procurement departments while its sales force grants discounts to satisfy quota, and both audiences may be content for a time. The cost appears when someone acts on the stated policy: a buyer who paid list learns what a peer paid, or a model trained on published prices proposes terms that no one actually offers.

Inconsistency between policy and practice has a specific cost for Policy as Part II defines it. The grammar of objectives, constraints, and instruments is useful only if its terms correspond to what the firm does. A constraint stated in policy but routinely waived in practice gives a model a false picture of the feasible set, and every proposal the model makes is then wrong in a predictable direction. Integrity in the institutional sense is thus a condition of the model's accuracy as well as of the firm's credibility.

#### Contribution Records

The accountability condition from Value becomes, at the level of governance, a requirement on records. Value argued that contributions along a chain must be traceable if prices are to reward them. Governance supplies the instruments that make tracing possible within the firm: the change record shows which role moved which price, the exception log shows which concessions were granted to which accounts on what grounds, and the override record shows where human judgment departed from the model and whether the departure paid.

These records allocate accountability internally. A discount that lost contribution can be traced to the right under which it was granted and to the evidence cited, so the lesson attaches to the right or the evidence rather than to whichever person is nearest when the result appears. A concession that won a strategic account can be credited in the same way. Accountability of this kind is the governance counterpart of the metering that Alchian and Demsetz found necessary in team production: without it, the members of a pricing function cannot be rewarded for good decisions or corrected for poor ones, and the incentive to decide carefully weakens.

Efficiency, the other half of the Value frame, appears here as the cost of the apparatus itself. Rights, records, and exception reviews consume attention, which Behavior identified as the scarcest input in a young firm. A governance layer that demands more recording than anyone reads fails the efficiency condition as surely as a tariff that forces buyers into costly workarounds. The test of each required field is whether some review uses it. Fields that no review uses should be removed, because their presence teaches the people who fill them that the record is a formality.

#### Binding Rules

Integrity operates as a constraint when it binds on the other three elements of Governance. On Authority, it requires that every right in use be a right on paper; a right exercised in practice but absent from the written policy is either added or withdrawn. On Audit, it requires that the record be complete for the fields it covers; a change made outside the record is treated as an unauthorized change, whatever its merits. On Exceptions, it requires that the pattern of exceptions be reported at the same level as the policy it modifies, so that the owner who publishes a band also sees how often it is set aside.

A periodic comparison of stated and realized terms makes the constraint operational. As an invented illustration, a firm whose policy caps standard discounts at 20 percent finds that 45 of its 150 new contracts in a half-year, or 30 percent, carry deeper discounts, of which only 12 have a recorded exception. The remaining 33 contracts, 22 percent of the total, are practice without policy. The finding calls for a decision rather than a reprimand: raise the cap, tighten the rights, or enforce the exception process, and record which.

Framed this way, integrity requires no appeal to virtue. It asks for consistency between what an institution states and what it does, measured with the instruments Governance already maintains. That consistency is what allows outsiders to rely on stated terms and insiders to rely on the model, and it is what the following chapters of Part II depend on when they write segment rules, offer lines, and competitive responses under this apparatus. The control layer is now specified: rights with owners, records that reconstruct, exceptions that expire and report, and a standing check that policy and practice agree.

## Chapter 9 — Segmentation

### Introduction
#### Who Pays

Chapter 9 of Pricing Tech is Segmentation. Governance assigned authority, audit, exceptions, and integrity so Optimization’s grammar has owners. Segmentation turns who-pays-what into written policy under that apparatus. Hallway habit—“enterprise gets a discount,” “students self-select,” “this vertical is different”—is not yet Policy. The chapter’s subject is what the firm can observe, how it prices differently across groups in practice, how cost-to-serve enters jointly with willingness to pay, and how those choices become durable, revisable rules rather than deal folklore.

The arc runs Observables, Discrimination, Cost-to-Serve, Codification. Observables state what can be seen, which proxies err, and where privacy or law limits the information set. Discrimination treats second- and third-degree ideas as practical instruments under arbitrage risk, not as a theory dump. Cost-to-Serve puts support load, infrastructure, and unpaid volume beside willingness so capture is not confused with margin. Codification writes the segment policy so deals stop reinventing it under Governance’s audit and exception rules. Differentiation will later design lines and versions that make fences stick; Engine portfolio coupling is later still.

#### Observables, Discrimination

Observables are segment hypotheses grounded in what the firm can see: firmographics, usage, channel, geography, contract form, and other measurable marks—each with proxy error. A segment that exists only in a sales narrative is not an observable. Privacy limits and regulatory envelopes from Optimization’s constraints bind here: some willingness-relevant traits are not usable as pricing inputs even when they are known informally. Uncertainty’s thin-data honesty applies: sparse scraps do not identify a demand system by segment; they support provisional groupings that Governance can revise.

Discrimination in this chapter means pricing differently across groups or self-selected menus in ways the firm can defend as Policy. Second-degree structures—versions, quantity breaks, freemium paths—invite buyers to reveal type through choice. Third-degree structures assign different lists or bands to observable classes. The book does not rehearse welfare theorems as a textbook exercise. It asks which form fits the instrument set, where arbitrage and resale break fences, and how fairness claims are handled as integrity—efficiency and contribution accountability—rather than as ethics sermons. A discount that cannot survive Audit is not a segment policy; it is an exception waiting to be logged.

#### Cost, Codification

Cost-to-Serve refuses to treat willingness alone as the segment story. Support load, compliance touch, infrastructure per cohort, and unpaid volume on free or low tiers change the contribution of a “high willingness” nameplate. Value already separated willingness from capture; Segmentation requires the joint consideration inside Policy so that freemium and low-touch paths are costed as instruments, not celebrated as pure growth. Thin data again limit precision; the prohibition is on ignoring cost-to-serve because it is harder to meter than list price.

Codification is the Governance interface. Written segment definitions, eligibility rules, and default instruments let deal desks execute without reinventing who qualifies. Folklore retires when the rule is stated, owned, and revisable under Audit. Patterned exceptions on the same segment become evidence to revise Codification, not to normalize drift. That is how Behavior’s exception culture is prevented from reappearing after Policy claims to have arrived.

The sequence Observables, Discrimination, Cost-to-Serve, Codification is analytic. Pricing differently without observables invites arbitrary favoritism. Observables without cost-to-serve invite capturing willingness while destroying contribution. Both without codification leave Segmentation as a slide, not as Policy. Closing with Codification returns the reader to Part II’s premise: who-pays-what must be teachable, auditable, and changeable on purpose.

#### Segment Edges

Segmentation does not design product lines, version ladders, or bundles that make fences hold—that is Differentiation. It does not couple SKUs into Engine price vectors. It does not reassign Governance seats except where segment rules need an owner. What this chapter owns is the Policy of who pays what: observables, practical discrimination, joint cost-to-serve, and durable rules.

### 9.1 Observables

#### Visible Marks

A segment is a claim that two groups of buyers differ in willingness to pay, in cost to serve, or in both, and that the difference can be acted on. The claim becomes usable only when membership can be decided from something the firm records. Observables are those records: firm size by headcount or revenue band, industry code, region, contract length, channel of acquisition, deployment form, usage volume, seat count, and the history of the account. Each can be read at the moment of quoting, which is the property that matters. A trait known only after the deal closes cannot sort the deal.

Value framed willingness to pay as the buyer's ceiling and capture as the share the seller takes. Segmentation asks how that ceiling varies across groups the seller can tell apart. Uncertainty described the seller's knowledge of the ceiling as beliefs updated from signals. Observables are the subset of signals stable enough to define a group in advance rather than to inform a single negotiation. A buyer's evident urgency in one call is a signal; a headcount band is an observable. The distinction keeps segment policy from absorbing every impression a salesperson forms.

The practical inventory has three columns. The first lists what is recorded with reasonable accuracy at quote time. The second lists what is recorded but manipulable by the buyer, such as stated company size on a self-serve form or the declared use of an academic license. The third lists what is known informally but not recorded, such as the reputation of a buyer's procurement office. Only the first column supports a segment without further work. The second needs verification or a fence. The third is evidence for a segment hypothesis, not a basis for one.

#### Proxy Error

Observables are proxies. The firm rarely sees willingness to pay; it sees traits correlated with it. Headcount stands in for budget, industry stands in for the value of the workflow, and usage stands in for dependence. Each correlation is imperfect, and the error falls in two directions. Some buyers inside a high band have low willingness and churn or stall at the segment price. Some buyers outside the band have high willingness and pay a price set for others. The first error loses volume; the second leaves surplus with the buyer.

A labeled illustration shows the arithmetic. Suppose, as an invented case, that a seller defines a large-account segment as firms above 1,000 employees, and its records show 400 such accounts. Suppose further that later evidence suggests 300 of them have the budgets the segment price assumes and 100 do not. The proxy is right for 300 of 400, or 75 percent of the segment. If 250 accounts below the threshold also carry the larger budgets, the segment captures 300 of the 550 high-budget accounts in total, about 55 percent. The proxy can look precise inside the segment and still miss most of what it was meant to find. Both rates belong in the segment record, because each points to a different correction: tighten the definition, or add a second observable.

Uncertainty's thin-data rule applies directly. Early segment rates rest on few deals, and the rates by segment rest on fewer. A segment hypothesis therefore states its proxy, the trait it is meant to track, the evidence for the link, and the date by which the link will be reviewed. Proxies that drift, as when a usage metric changes meaning after a product change, are retired on the same schedule as any other belief that has lost its ground.

#### Privacy Bounds

Some observables are off limits even when they are available. Optimization treated legal and regulatory envelopes as constraints on the instrument set; here they constrain the information set. Personal data rules limit what may be collected and what it may be used for, and some traits, such as protected characteristics in consumer markets, cannot serve as pricing inputs whatever their correlation with willingness. The detailed regulatory treatment belongs to Enterprise. For Segmentation the point is procedural: the list of permitted observables is itself a policy, owned by a named role under Governance, and reviewed when data practices or rules change.

There is also a limit that law does not set. Buyers react to how they are classified. A segment that rests on traits buyers consider private or irrelevant invites the perception that the price was set against them rather than for a class of use. That perception damages the reference points discussed under Behavior and raises the cost of every later negotiation. A seller that could infer a buyer's alternatives from browsing data, and prices on that inference, may gain in one deal and lose credibility across many. Restricting observables to traits a buyer would recognize as bearing on the transaction, such as scale, use, and terms, keeps segments explainable.

Segment hypotheses close the section. Each hypothesis names the observable, the direction of the expected difference in willingness or cost, the expected size, and the evidence to date. Written this way, a segment is open to the same audit as any other belief in the policy. The next section asks how the price may differ across segments once they are defined, and what keeps buyers from moving across the line.

### 9.2 Discrimination

#### Three Degrees

Arthur Cecil Pigou, in The Economics of Welfare (1920), classified price discrimination into three degrees, and modern usage has extended his terms. First degree charges each buyer that buyer's maximum. Second degree, in the modern sense, offers the same schedule to everyone, with prices that vary by quantity or terms, so that buyers sort themselves by what they choose; Pigou himself treated it as a stepwise approximation of the first. Third degree assigns different prices to groups identified by observable traits. The classification remains useful because it maps onto instruments a pricing function already holds. Third degree corresponds to segment lists and bands keyed to observables. Second degree corresponds to quantity breaks, commitment discounts, and tiers of terms. First degree is approximated, imperfectly and at high cost, by individual negotiation.

The term discrimination carries a moral charge in ordinary speech. In economics it means only that price differences across buyers are not explained by cost differences. That definition matters for policy, because a price difference that is explained by cost, the subject of the next section, is not discrimination in this sense even when it looks like favoritism. Most real schedules mix both: some of the gap between two segment prices reflects cost, and some reflects willingness.

In practice the choice among degrees follows from the observables. Where a trait is recorded and hard to fake, third-degree pricing is direct: a list for public-sector buyers, a band for accounts above a size threshold, a regional price. Where traits are unrecorded or easy to misstate, the seller offers a schedule and lets choices reveal type. Quantity breaks and term discounts are the common forms in this chapter's scope. Menus built from distinct product versions, which screen buyers by the features they choose, rely on the same logic of self-selection but are a design problem for Differentiation and are left there. Negotiation that tries to read each buyer's maximum sits at the first-degree end. Its cost in staff time and in exception volume was the subject of Governance.

#### Arbitrage

Any price difference invites buyers to move to the cheaper side of it. Arbitrage takes three forms in the settings this book covers. Buyers may misreport the observable, as when a commercial user claims an academic license. Buyers may resell or share, as when one low-price account serves several entities. Buyers may change the trait itself, as when a large customer splits a purchase across subsidiaries to fall into a smaller band. Each form erodes the difference until the segment price converges toward the lowest price offered.

Fences limit arbitrage. A fence is any condition that makes moving across the price line costly: verification of eligibility, contractual limits on transfer, entitlements tied to an identity, or terms that the low-price group values and the high-price group does not. Fences have their own cost, both in administration and in the friction they impose on legitimate buyers. A verification step that slows every academic purchase taxes the group the discount was meant to reach.

The expected cost of leakage can be stated in the segment record. As a labeled illustration with invented numbers, suppose a discounted segment price is 60 against a standard price of 100, and that 20 of 200 discounted accounts are later found to be ineligible. The leakage is 20 accounts at a gap of 40 each, or 800 per period, against total discounted revenue of 200 times 60, or 12,000. Leakage is about 6.7 percent of that revenue. Whether this is acceptable depends on what a tighter fence would cost and how many eligible buyers it would deter. The number makes the trade explicit and lets Governance set a tolerance rather than react to anecdotes.

#### Fair Treatment

Buyers judge price differences, and their judgments affect demand. Behavior described reference points and the reaction to terms perceived as unfair. Segment pricing tends to be accepted when the basis is visible and plausibly connected to the transaction: volume, commitment, use, or ability to pay as in many educational and nonprofit programs. It tends to provoke resistance when the basis is hidden, unrelated to the transaction, or applied to buyers who discover they paid more than a peer for the same thing.

This book treats fairness through integrity rather than ethics. Integrity, as Value defined it, concerns whether the pricing arrangement remains efficient and accountable for contribution, and, as Governance added, whether stated policy and realized practice agree. A segment policy has integrity when its basis is written, applied consistently, and able to survive the audit described in Governance. A discount granted to one buyer because of a relationship, and denied to an otherwise identical buyer, fails that standard whatever its commercial logic. If the relationship carries value, the policy should state the observable that captures it.

Consistency also protects the seller. Segment differences that cannot be explained become negotiating material for every buyer who learns of them, and they push the schedule toward first-degree bargaining. A small number of segments with written rationales and enforced fences keeps price differences stable. The next section adds the other half of the margin: what each segment costs to serve.

### 9.3 Cost-to-Serve

#### Serving Costs

A segment defined only by willingness to pay can look attractive and lose money. Contribution depends on price minus the cost of serving the account, and serving costs vary across segments as much as willingness does. Large accounts often pay more per seat and also demand dedicated support, security reviews, custom contract terms, and integration help. Small accounts pay less and consume support through volume rather than depth. Regulated industries add compliance work. The cost of a segment is the sum of these activities, attributed with enough care to change a decision.

Conventional cost accounting allocates overhead by revenue or headcount, which hides exactly these differences. Activity-based costing, developed by Robin Cooper and Robert Kaplan in the late 1980s, attributes indirect costs to the activities that cause them and then to the products or customers that use those activities. Kaplan and Steven Anderson later proposed a time-driven variant that estimates the time each activity takes and the cost per unit of capacity, which makes the method cheaper to maintain. The pricing use is modest. The seller needs to know whether a segment's serving cost differs enough from the average to alter its price floor or its eligibility rules, not to build a complete cost system.

Infrastructure cost follows the same logic. Margins, in Principles, observed that marginal cost depends on the unit sold. Segments differ in the units they consume: heavy compute, high storage, dedicated environments, or regional hosting that carries its own price. Where these costs scale with usage, a flat segment price leaves margin exposed to the heaviest users inside the segment. The usual remedy is a usage component, a cap, or an eligibility condition that moves heavy users to a different schedule.

#### Free Volume

Low-price and free tiers carry costs that do not appear in revenue reports. Every free user consumes hosting, some support, abuse handling, and a share of product attention. These costs are justified when the free tier functions as an instrument: a route to paid conversion, a source of network value for paying users, or a cheaper channel than sales. They are not justified by user counts alone. Freemium, in this chapter's terms, is a segment priced at zero, and its policy needs the same record as any other segment: the observable that defines it, the expected conversion or other return, and the serving cost.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers clarifies the test. Suppose a free tier has 50,000 users, each costing 0.40 per month to host and support, for a total of 20,000 per month. Suppose 1 percent of them, or 500 users, convert each year to a paid plan that yields contribution of 600 per year after its own serving cost. Annual conversion contribution is 500 times 600, or 300,000, against annual free-tier cost of 12 times 20,000, or 240,000. The tier pays for itself by 60,000 a year under these assumptions, and a fall in conversion to 0.8 percent, or 400 users and 240,000 of contribution, would erase the margin entirely. The arithmetic does not settle whether to keep the tier, since free users may also add value that conversion does not capture. It names the assumptions the decision rests on and the threshold at which it reverses.

Unpaid volume arises inside paid segments as well. Overage that is not billed, usage beyond contracted limits that sales declines to enforce, and support given outside the plan all transfer cost to the seller without a price. Governance treated these as exceptions; here they are cost lines that change the segment's contribution and belong in its record.

#### Joint Margins

The two halves combine in a segment margin: price, set by willingness and fences, minus serving cost. A segment with high willingness and high cost may contribute less than one with moderate willingness and low cost. Ranking segments by list price alone, as sales compensation often encourages, directs effort toward accounts that look large and may earn little. Ranking by contribution corrects the direction.

The joint view also clarifies when a price difference counts as discrimination in the sense of 9.2. If an enterprise segment pays 30 percent more per seat and costs 30 percent more to serve per seat, the gap reflects cost and the price ratio tracks the cost ratio. If it pays 30 percent more and costs the same, the gap reflects willingness. Both may be sound policy, but they rest on different arguments and face different scrutiny from buyers who compare terms. Writing down which argument applies is part of what makes the segment defensible.

Measurement limits remain. Serving costs are estimated, attributions are contestable, and the estimates are thinner for small or new segments. The standard from Uncertainty holds: a rough cost estimate with stated error is better than an omitted cost, and the estimate is revised when the activity data improve. With observables, a degree of discrimination, and a margin in hand, the remaining task is to write the segment down so that it survives turnover and deal pressure.

### 9.4 Codification

#### Written Segments

A segment exists as policy when it is written. The written form states the name of the segment, the observable or observables that define membership, how membership is verified, the instrument that applies, such as a list, band, or schedule, the fence that limits arbitrage, the serving-cost assumptions, the owner, and the review date. The record is short. Its purpose is to let someone who was not present when the segment was created decide whether a given buyer belongs to it and what terms follow.

Optimization separated objectives from instruments and constraints. The segment record is where these meet for a group of buyers. The objective is stated once for the policy as a whole; the segment record states which instrument serves it for this group and which constraints bind. Governance assigned decision rights; the segment record names who may change the definition, who may grant terms inside it, and who may grant exceptions to it. Without that record, segment rules live in the memory of the people who negotiated the first deals, and they change when those people leave.

A written segment also limits its own growth. Segments multiply when every new situation earns a new rule. Each additional segment adds verification work, fence maintenance, and room for buyers to argue that they belong to a cheaper class. A useful discipline is to require that any new segment show a difference in willingness or cost large enough to justify that overhead, measured against the nearest existing segment.

The record should also state what the segment does not cover. Buyers at the boundary, such as a nonprofit with a commercial subsidiary or a small firm owned by a large one, are where disputes arise. A boundary rule written in advance, naming which observable governs when two point in different directions, settles these cases once instead of in every negotiation. Where no rule can be written, the record should say that boundary cases go to the exception path with a named approver, so that the decision is at least logged and comparable over time.

#### Deal Hygiene

Deal hygiene is the practice of executing segment policy at the point of sale. A quote records the segment the buyer was placed in, the observable that placed it there, and any deviation from the segment's terms with its exception code. These fields make the audit described in Governance possible. When the fields are missing, the seller cannot tell whether a low price reflects a segment rule, an exception, or an error.

Hygiene also covers reclassification. Buyers change segments as they grow, shrink, or change use. A rule for when an account moves, at renewal, at a usage threshold, or on verified change of status, prevents both stale discounts and surprise increases. The rule should be visible to the buyer in advance, which connects to the reference points Behavior described: a price change that follows a stated rule is received differently from one that appears arbitrary.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the audit use. Suppose a quarter's records show 240 deals, of which 180 carry a segment code and 60 do not. Of the 180 coded deals, 27 carry an exception, or 15 percent. The 60 uncoded deals, a quarter of the total, cannot be assessed at all. The first correction is to require the code; only then does the exception rate describe the whole book of business rather than three quarters of it.

#### Rule Revision

Codification is meant to retire folklore, the informal understandings that accumulate around pricing, such as the belief that a certain industry never pays list or that a certain region always needs a discount. Some folklore is accurate and should become a written segment with evidence. Some is a residue of single deals and should be dropped. The method is to state each belief as a segment hypothesis in the form of Observables, check it against the deal record, and either write it down or remove it.

Written rules also need a revision path, or they harden into a different kind of folklore. Governance treated patterned exceptions as evidence. In segment policy, a cluster of exceptions on one segment indicates that the definition, the price, or the fence is wrong. The review date in the segment record forces the question even without a cluster. At review the owner compares realized willingness, measured by win rates and discount depth, and realized cost against the assumptions in the record, and either confirms, revises, or retires the segment.

Revision has costs that Behavior noted under menu costs: buyers adjust to rules, and frequent change damages credibility. The answer is a cadence, not a freeze. Segment definitions change at stated intervals and for stated reasons, with existing accounts handled by a transition rule. That cadence makes segment policy teachable, auditable, and changeable on purpose, which is the standard Part II set for Policy. Differentiation takes up the next question, how product lines and versions can make segments sort themselves.

## Chapter 10 — Differentiation

### Introduction
#### Fences

Chapter 10 of Pricing Tech is Differentiation. Segmentation turned who-pays-what into policy—observables, practical discrimination, cost-to-serve, and codified rules. Those rules fail when the offer is a single undifferentiated object that every buyer can arbitrage into the cheap seat. Differentiation is the product and offer structure that makes segment fences hold: lines that support price steps, versioning suited to information goods, bundles and complements chosen deliberately, and a durability check against imitation. Competition will later write rivalry stance; Engine will later automate offers. This chapter designs the structure Policy can govern.

The arc runs Lines, Versioning, Bundles, Imitation. Lines set vertical quality steps and horizontal variety so good-better-best and adjacent variants are fences rather than labels. Versioning treats feature gates, delay, and quality degradation as instruments for information goods, where copies are cheap and adverse selection is easy. Bundles addresses complements, add-ons, pure versus mixed packaging, and metered extras. Imitation asks what travels when rivals copy the menu and what remains hard to copy on a refresh cadence the firm can afford. Foundations’ object definition and Value’s capture problems reappear here as design constraints, not as slogans.

#### Lines, Versioning

Lines are the architecture of priced variants. Vertical lines order quality so higher tiers can carry higher list without inviting every buyer into the top seat through soft fences. Horizontal lines offer variety that is not a pure quality ranking—regions, workflows, compliance skins—so Segmentation’s observables have something solid to attach to. Good-better-best fails when the “better” step is only marketing copy; fence design means the step changes a valued attribute, a right, or a cost-to-serve load that Audit can see. Governance’s authority attaches to who may open a new line or collapse one.

Versioning is the information-goods specialization. Feature gates, delayed release, degraded fidelity, and support tiers are ways to induce self-selection without pretending physical scarcity. Adverse selection risk is first-class: if the high tier is only cosmetic, sophisticated buyers take the low tier and the contribution story collapses. Freemium paths from Principles and Segmentation return as version choices with explicit cost-to-serve. Thin data still limit how finely versions can be tuned; Differentiation requires justifiable version logic under Uncertainty, not a catalog of every combinatorial SKU.

#### Bundles, Imitation

Bundles package complements and add-ons so joint willingness can be captured without forcing every buyer through the same a-la-carte maze. Pure bundling, mixed bundling, and metered add-ons are instruments with different arbitrage and attention properties. Attention from Behavior binds: a bundle that is analytically neat can still exhaust the menu capacity of buyers and of the deal desk. Optimization’s instruments include bundle as a lever; this chapter specifies when that lever supports segment fences and when it merely hides a discount.

Imitation is the durability test. Menus, feature gates, and bundle shapes travel; brand, data advantages, switching costs, and operational cost-to-serve often travel less. A differentiation that rivals can copy in a sprint is a temporary instrument, not a moat narrative. Policy needs a refresh cadence: what will be revised when copycats arrive, under whose Authority, with what Audit trail. War-chest mythology belongs out; so does the claim that differentiation never erodes. Closing with Imitation forces honesty about what the structure buys in time rather than in eternity.

The sequence Lines, Versioning, Bundles, Imitation is analytic. Versioning without lines invites SKU sprawl. Bundles without version logic invite packaging as discount camouflage. Structure without an imitation check invites Policy that works for one quarter. Closing with Imitation returns the reader to Part II’s revisability: offer structure must be governable when rivals move.

#### Fence Limits

Differentiation does not write rivalry playbooks—when to match, soften, or fight—that is Competition. It does not automate version offers or portfolio price vectors—that is Engine. It does not redo Segmentation’s who-pays-what rules except where fences require an offer object. What this chapter owns is the structure that makes those rules stick: lines, versioning, bundles, and durability under imitation.

### 10.1 Lines

#### Quality Ladders

A product line is a set of priced variants that buyers can compare. The comparison is the point. Segmentation sorted buyers by what the seller can observe; a line lets buyers sort themselves by what they choose, which is the self-selection Segmentation set aside for this chapter. The two mechanisms are complements. Observables assign a buyer to a list, and the line offers that buyer a menu within it.
