Lines come in two shapes. A vertical line orders variants by quality on which buyers agree: more capacity, faster response, stronger guarantees, broader rights. Every buyer prefers the higher step at equal price, and buyers differ only in how much more they will pay for it. A horizontal line offers variety on which buyers disagree: a workflow for finance teams and another for logistics, a regional data residence, an integration with one platform rather than another. No variant is better for everyone, and price differences across horizontal variants follow cost and the intensity of preference rather than a shared ranking.

Michael Mussa and Sherwin Rosen (1978) analyzed the vertical case for a monopolist selling to buyers who differ in their valuation of quality. Their result explains why good-better-best ladders look the way they do. A seller who cannot observe each buyer's type and offers a menu has to leave high-valuation buyers enough surplus that they do not drop to a cheaper step. To reduce that surplus, the seller lowers the quality of the lower steps below the level it would choose if types were observable, and may leave the lowest-valuation buyers unserved. The lower steps are shaped as much by their effect on the upper steps as by the buyers who take them.

#### Step Design

The Mussa and Rosen logic can be shown with a labeled illustration using invented numbers. Two buyer types face a two-step line, Basic and Pro. High-type buyers value Basic at 100 and Pro at 180; low-type buyers value Basic at 60 and Pro at 80. If Basic is priced at 60 so that low types buy it, a high-type buyer taking Basic would keep 100 minus 60, or 40. Pro must leave at least that much, so its price can be at most 180 minus 40, or 140. The 40 is the high type's information rent: what the seller gives up because it cannot tell the types apart. Now suppose Basic is redesigned so that high types value it at 70 while low types still value it at 60. The high type's surplus from Basic falls to 10, and Pro can be priced at up to 170. Weakening the lower step raised the price the upper step could carry by 30 per high-type buyer, without changing Pro at all.

The illustration shows what a step has to do. A step is a fence only if it changes something the high type values more than the low type does. A step defined by marketing copy, or by a feature the high type does not care about, leaves the high type's surplus from the cheaper step intact, and the upper price falls to meet it. The design question for each step is therefore which attribute separates the types, and whether Audit can see the attribute in the contract. Capacity limits, response commitments, administrative controls, and rights of use are visible. Labels are not.

Steps also have a count. Each added step is another constraint a buyer can exploit by choosing the next step down, and another object for sales to explain and for Governance to own. Ladders of three or four steps are common in practice, consistent with the attention limits Behavior described. A step that no segment chooses should be removed, and its removal logged under the change rules of Governance.

#### Line Ownership

Lines carry cost as well as price. Each variant has its own cost-to-serve, and Segmentation required that capture be measured beside that cost. A premium step that promises dedicated support may carry a margin below the standard step once support hours are counted. The line is then sorting buyers into the less profitable seat, which the margin floors of Optimization should catch if the floors are scoped to the variant.

Horizontal variety raises a different cost. Variants that share most of their components cost little to add and much to maintain: each has documentation, testing, and a support path. Variety is worth carrying when it attaches to an observable segment that pays for it, or when it fences a group whose willingness differs. Variety added because one large deal asked for it is an exception in product form, and Governance's exception rules apply to it as they apply to a discount.

The authority to open or close a line belongs in the decision rights Governance assigned. Opening a step changes the menu for every buyer; closing one forces migrations and touches the reference points Behavior described, since buyers judge a new price against the one they paid. A line policy therefore names who may change the ladder, what evidence justifies a new step, and how existing accounts move when a step is retired. The next section turns to information goods, where steps are cheap to manufacture and the incentive to build them by removing value is strongest.

### 10.2 Versioning

#### Feature Gates

An information good is costly to produce once and cheap to copy. Its versions therefore differ by what the seller chooses to include rather than by what the seller spends on each unit. Carl Shapiro and Hal Varian, in Information Rules (1998), set out versioning as the standard instrument for such goods: offer a menu of versions built from one underlying product so that buyers with different valuations choose different versions and pay different prices. The logic is the self-selection of the previous section applied where the marginal cost of the higher step is close to zero.

The usual dimensions are known. Feature gates withhold capabilities: administrative controls, integrations, audit logs, advanced analytics. Volume gates cap usage: seats, projects, records, calls. Delay gates withhold timeliness: data refreshed daily instead of in real time, releases available after a lag. Fidelity gates withhold quality: lower resolution, smaller models, reduced accuracy. Support gates withhold attention: response commitments and named contacts. Each dimension works only where buyers differ in how much they value it, and the most useful dimensions are those that track the buyer's stakes. Large organizations value administrative control and audit logs because they have many users and compliance obligations; small teams do not.

Gates should follow the observables Segmentation recorded. If segment policy keys a list to firm size, a gate on administrative controls reinforces the fence, since the trait and the feature move together. A gate unrelated to any observable difference forces buyers to sort on a dimension the firm cannot verify, and the sorting is weaker. Gates also need to be enforceable in the product itself. A gate that depends on the buyer honoring a contract term, rather than on a capability the software withholds, is a promise the seller has to monitor, and monitoring costs belong in the version margin.

#### Damaged Goods

Versioning of information goods often builds the lower version by removing value from the higher one. Raymond Deneckere and Preston McAfee (1996) called these damaged goods and documented cases in which the cheaper version cost as much to make as the better one, or more, because the damage was an added step. Their analysis showed that such damage can be a Pareto improvement over a single high price, when it brings low-valuation buyers into the market at a price that does not undercut sales of the full version.

The Mussa and Rosen result from 10.1 explains why damage pays. Lowering the quality of the cheap version reduces the surplus a high-valuation buyer would keep by choosing it, which raises the price the full version can carry. For information goods the reduction costs almost nothing to produce, so the seller is tempted to damage more than the separation requires. The constraint on damage is that low-valuation buyers still have to want the cheap version, and that the damage cannot be easily undone by the buyer.

Integrity, in the economic sense of Value, sets a further limit. Damage that consumes resources only to withhold value is efficient only to the extent that it serves sorting the firm could not achieve otherwise. When a firm adds capacity throttles or delays that cost it compute and support, the record should state which segment fence the damage protects. Damage with no stated fence is waste that the margin record will eventually show.

#### Pooling Risk

Versioning fails when buyers do not separate. If the lower version satisfies most of what high-valuation buyers need, they take it, and the menu pools types at the low price. The seller has then given away the revenue the full version was meant to carry. The risk is greatest when the gate falls on a dimension buyers can work around: exporting data to avoid a reporting gate, splitting a workload across accounts to avoid a volume cap, or tolerating a delay that turns out to matter little in practice.

The opposite failure is adverse selection in the cost sense. A low version priced for small buyers attracts heavy users who can live without the gated feature but consume far more than the price assumed. Freemium, as Principles and Segmentation described it, is the extreme case: the free version is a version with a price of zero, and its cost-to-serve depends on who chooses it. Volume gates are the usual defense, and they belong in the version definition rather than in an acceptable-use clause enforced after the fact.

Both failures appear in the data before they appear in revenue. A rising share of large accounts on the lower version, or of high-usage accounts on the free one, is evidence that the fence is leaking. Thin data limit how precisely this can be measured, as Uncertainty argued, but the direction of the shift is usually visible. Version policy therefore states, for each version, the buyer it is meant for, the gate that keeps others out, and the evidence that would trigger a review. A version that no buyer takes as intended is revised on the schedule Segmentation set for segment rules. The next section combines products rather than dividing one.

### 10.3 Bundles

#### Mixed Bundling

Optimization introduced bundling as an instrument and cited Adams and Yellen (1976) for the result that bundling can raise profit when buyers' valuations of the components differ. That result is taken as given here. The design question is which form of bundling to offer and how it supports the fences built in the previous two sections.

Pure bundling sells only the bundle. Mixed bundling sells the bundle and its components separately, with the bundle usually priced below the sum of the component prices. Adams and Yellen showed that mixed bundling, suitably priced, does at least as well as pure bundling, and since separate sales are also a special case of it, at least as well as selling the components alone. It serves buyers who want only one component without giving up the sorting the bundle provides. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the effect. Buyer A values product X at 70 and product Y at 30; buyer B values X at 30 and Y at 70; buyer C values X at 90 and Y at nothing. A pure bundle priced at 100 sells to A and B, for revenue of 200, and loses C, who values the bundle at 90. Offering X alone at 90 alongside the bundle at 100 keeps A and B in the bundle, since X alone would leave A with 70 minus 90, a loss, and adds C at 90. Revenue rises to 290. The illustration assumes that a buyer indifferent between buying and not buying buys.

The component price in mixed bundling is a fence. It has to be high enough that buyers who value both components choose the bundle, and low enough that single-component buyers are served. Pricing a component close to the bundle price, as in the illustration, is common for that reason. A component priced well below the bundle invites bundle buyers to assemble their own cheaper version.

#### Add-Ons

An add-on is a component sold only alongside a base product. Add-ons let a line carry optional value without multiplying versions: a base tier plus an analytics add-on and a compliance add-on covers four combinations with three priced objects. They are also the natural form for complements, goods whose value rises when used together. When the base product is the complement that draws participation and the add-on carries the margin, the scope of margin floors matters, as Optimization argued: a floor applied to each line item will block a structure that is profitable at the level of the account.

Metered add-ons attach a usage charge to the optional component, such as a fee per report generated or per thousand calls beyond an allowance. Oi's two-part logic, cited in Optimization, applies directly: the base fee captures a share of surplus, and the meter tracks the use that drives both value and cost. A metered add-on is the right form when the component's cost-to-serve rises with use and buyers differ widely in how much they use it. A flat add-on is the right form when use is predictable and the administrative cost of metering exceeds what it recovers.

Add-ons carry an attention cost. Behavior described buyers with limited capacity to evaluate menus, and a page of add-ons is a menu. Each added option raises the chance that buyers defer the decision or that sales improvises a package. Add-ons should be few, named for the job they do, and recorded so that Audit can tell which combinations accounts actually buy.

#### Large Bundles

Information goods allow bundles too large to make sense for physical products. Yannis Bakos and Erik Brynjolfsson (1999) showed that for goods with near-zero marginal cost, bundling many goods makes each buyer's valuation of the whole more predictable than valuations of the parts, by a law-of-large-numbers effect. The seller can then price the bundle close to the typical valuation and capture a larger share of total surplus than separate prices would. Their argument depends on low marginal cost; where each component carries real cost-to-serve, as with compute-intensive features, the case weakens because buyers who value a component little still consume it.

The result explains the appeal of all-inclusive suites and the persistence of mixed forms beside them. Large bundles also raise a policy problem. A suite priced as a whole hides the price of each component, which complicates the margin record and the evaluation of single components. Governance's audit applies: the firm should be able to state an internal allocation of the bundle price across components, even if buyers never see it, so that a component's contribution can be judged.

Bundles can also hide a discount. A bundle created for one deal, at a price that serves only to bring the deal under a threshold, is a concession with a product name. The test from Governance applies: if the bundle is offered to every buyer who meets its stated conditions and recorded as such, it is a bundle; if it exists for one account, it is an exception. The last section asks how long any of these structures last once rivals see them.

### 10.4 Imitation

#### Copied Menus

Lines, versions, and bundles are visible. A rival can read a pricing page, sign up for a trial, and reproduce the menu in a quarter. Imitation is therefore the durability test for differentiation. It is also a competitive response, and Competition takes up how the firm should respond to rivals in general. This section asks a narrower question: which parts of the offer structure survive being copied, and how often the structure needs to be revised.

What travels easily is the shape of the menu. Step names, the number of tiers, the placement of gates, the anchor price, and the form of the meter can all be copied from public information. Copying the shape does not copy the economics behind it. A rival who copies a three-step ladder without the cost-to-serve that justified each step may find the copied structure unprofitable, and a rival whose buyers differ may find that the copied gates fail to separate them. Menus converge quickly across a category because the shape is cheap to adopt, not because each seller has derived it from its own buyers.

The firm's interest is to know which of its fences depend on the shape alone. A fence that holds only because rivals have not yet offered the gated feature in their lower version will erode once they do. Buyers who would have paid for the higher step move to the rival's lower one, or demand the same terms in negotiation. The fences most exposed are those added most recently, because they rest on features that rivals are already building. An inventory that marks each fence by what holds it, shape or asset, tells the firm where erosion will come first.

#### Durable Assets

What travels slowly are assets that a rival cannot copy from a pricing page. Installed base and switching costs, which Principles treated under lock-in, make a step valuable to existing buyers in ways a new rival cannot replicate at the same price. Data accumulated through use can make a higher version better for each buyer in a way that depends on that buyer's history. Integrations, certifications, and compliance records take time to build and are verified by third parties. Operational cost-to-serve differs across firms: a seller that serves a version at lower cost can hold a price that a copying rival cannot match profitably.

Differentiation that attaches fences to such assets lasts longer. A higher step defined by a capability tied to the buyer's own data, or by a compliance commitment the firm can document, is harder to undercut than a step defined by a feature a rival can ship. The design implication is to place the most important price steps on attributes that depend on assets, and to treat feature-based steps as temporary.

None of this makes differentiation permanent. Assets depreciate, standards spread, and rivals acquire certifications. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows how to state the bet. Suppose a new premium feature costs 80,000 to build and supports a price premium of 30 per seat per quarter on 1,000 seats, or 30,000 per quarter. If rivals are expected to match the feature within two quarters, after which the premium disappears, the feature earns 60,000 on the premium, short of its cost. It pays only if it also retains accounts or the lead lasts at least three quarters, which yields 90,000. The firm should write down the assumed lead time before building, so that the outcome can be compared with the assumption.

#### Refresh Cadence

Since copied structures erode, the offer structure needs a review schedule. Segmentation set a cadence for segment rules; differentiation needs its own, tied to the pace at which rivals in the category adopt features and change menus. The review asks three questions of each step, version, and bundle: whether buyers still separate as intended, whether rivals now offer the gated attribute at a lower step, and whether the cost-to-serve assumptions still hold.

The review has limits. Frequent menu changes impose the costs Behavior described: buyers compare new prices with the ones they paid, sales relearns the menu, and existing contracts need transition rules. A cadence that is too fast destroys the credibility of the menu, and one that is too slow leaves fences standing after rivals have removed them. A quarterly review of evidence with an annual revision of structure is one workable pattern, with an exception path, owned under Governance, for a rival move that removes a fence outright.

The record of each review belongs with the rest of the pricing record. It states what was assumed about imitation, what happened, and what changed. That record lets a later team distinguish a structure that eroded on schedule from one that failed for other reasons. It also keeps the assumed lead times honest: a firm whose recorded leads are consistently longer than the leads it observes has evidence that its estimates of rival speed are biased, and can correct them in the next round of design. How the firm responds to rivals beyond revising its own structure is the subject of Competition, which follows.

## Chapter 11 — Competition

### Introduction
#### Stance

Chapter 11 of Pricing Tech is Competition. Interaction sketched rivals as Strategy objects under thin data; Differentiation asked which offer structures survive copycats. Competition writes rivalry as revisable Policy: who the firm faces, how entry and switching shape feasible prices, when to soften versus fight, and how promotions and share contests are designed with exit criteria. War-chest mythology and endless-price-war hero narratives are out of scope. The chapter’s subject is a stated competitive stance with rationale that Governance can own and Learning can revise.

The arc runs Rivalry, Entry, Softening, Contests. Rivalry names identifiable competitors, observable moves, and response versus non-response rules. Entry treats contestable margins, switching costs, incumbent pricing, and accommodation as policy choices rather than as instincts. Softening covers differentiation-led softening, limited matching, and avoidance of doom loops that Optimization’s constraints would forbid if they were stated. Contests treats promotions and share fights as designed episodes with margin tradeoffs and exit criteria. Engine may later simulate rival conjectures; here only pointers appear. Interaction’s honesty about unidentified reaction functions still binds.

#### Rivalry, Entry

Rivalry begins with who can respond on which instrument and lag. Matching, undercutting, and shifting competition into Differentiation’s lines are observable; interpreting them without a written stance recreates Behavior’s exception culture at the market edge. Response rules and non-response rules belong in Policy: when the firm matches within a band, when it holds list, when it escalates under Governance Authority. Thin data from Uncertainty limit identification of reaction functions; Competition requires justifiable rules under that limit, not a calibrated game the firm cannot estimate.

Entry and switching reshape the feasible set before any fight begins. Contestable margins invite accommodation or deterrence; switching costs and contractual fences from Differentiation change how much price must move to win or hold. Incumbent pricing that ignores entry is not “strong”; it is a stance that has not been stated. Accommodation is sometimes the integrity-consistent choice when capacity, support, or promise constraints from Optimization bind. The chapter treats those as Policy objects under Audit, not as bravery tests.

#### Softening, Contests

Softening is deliberate use of Differentiation and limited matching to reduce the gain from pure price fights. Escape into variety, version, or bundle structure can dominate a doom loop that destroys contribution while Governance watches exceptions multiply. Match-with-limits is an instrument: a band, a duration, an owner. Ego-driven matching—“we never lose a deal on price”—is not a stance; it is an unowned override. Softening therefore inherits Optimization’s constraints and Governance’s exception pattern detection: repeated “temporary” matches are policy drift.

Contests are designed episodes—promotions, win-back offers, share pushes—with explicit share-versus-margin tradeoffs and exit criteria. A contest without exit criteria is a permanent list cut by another name. Learning will later store what was tried; Competition requires that the try be designed as a contest rather than as an open-ended war. Interaction’s one-shot folklore misleads when the same rivals return; Contests acknowledges repetition without claiming an identified repeated game.

The sequence Rivalry, Entry, Softening, Contests is analytic. Softening without named rivals is decoration. Contests without entry logic mis-measure what a win costs. Closing with Contests returns the reader to Part II revisability: competitive moves must be governable episodes, not identity.

#### Rivalry Scope

Competition does not build full Engine libraries of rival strategies—pointers only. It does not redesign Differentiation’s lines except where stance attaches to an offer object. It does not redo Interaction’s multi-agent map. What this chapter owns is competitive pricing as Policy: rivalry rules, entry and switching, softening versus fighting, and contests with exits—without war-chest mythology.

The chapter therefore treats a competitive response as a policy object: stated in advance, owned by a named role, bounded by margin and integrity constraints, and revised on evidence. A response that cannot be stated in these terms remains an improvisation, however decisive it appears, and leaves the organization unable to learn from its own record of contests.

### 11.1 Rivalry

#### Rival Map

Interaction described rivals as agents whose prices depend on the firm's price, and it showed that the same observed move can come from several reaction functions. Competition turns that description into a written stance. The first object in the stance is a list: the rivals the firm actually faces, named, with the segments where each one meets the firm's buyers. A category map drawn from analyst reports names many sellers; the rival list names the few whose prices buyers cite in negotiation, whose trials appear in the same evaluations, and whose wins and losses the sales team records. A seller that never appears in a lost-deal record is part of the market but not part of the stance.

For each named rival the map records four facts. The first is the instrument the rival moves: list price, discount band, free-tier limits, contract length, or bundled services. The second is the lag, the time between a move by the firm and the earliest response the rival could make. The third is the rival's position on the slow variables Interaction identified, chiefly committed capacity, sales headcount, and installed base under fixed renewals. The fourth is the rival's apparent objective, whether contribution, share, or growth funded by investors who reward volume ahead of margin. Each entry is a hypothesis, dated and owned, in the same sense that a willingness estimate is a hypothesis about buyers.

The map also records what the firm can observe. Public lists, changelogs, and announcements are observable within days. Discounts given in private negotiation are observed only through lost deals and buyer reports, which arrive late and are biased toward the cases where the rival won. A stance built on observable moves should say which of its triggers rest on public evidence and which on anecdote, because anecdote tends to overstate how aggressive rivals are.

#### Response Rules

A response rule states, in advance, what the firm does when a named rival makes a defined move. The rule has a trigger, an instrument, a band, a duration, and an owner. An illustrative rule reads: if a named rival lowers list price in the mid-market segment by more than ten percent, the regional sales lead may match within the existing discount band for ninety days on deals where that rival is in the final evaluation; anything beyond the band goes to the Authority that Governance assigned. The rule is narrow on purpose. It limits the response to the contested buyers, keeps the list price intact for everyone else, and expires unless someone renews it on evidence.

Response rules gain value from being known inside the firm and, within the limits of law, from being predictable outside it. Interaction noted that every price change is also a message to rivals. A rival that observes consistent, bounded responses learns that undercutting the firm buys little in the contested segment and nothing elsewhere. A rival that observes erratic responses, sometimes deep matching and sometimes none, cannot infer what a cut will cost it and may cut to find out. Consistency is therefore part of the instrument, and Governance's exception records are the evidence that the rule is applied as written.

Rules also need a basis in cost. Interaction's undercutting illustration showed that a cut pays when the rival cannot follow. The same logic sets the floor of a response rule: the firm should not commit to match a rival whose cost to serve is lower than its own on the contested work, because the rule would then commit it to a loss on every matched deal.

#### Holding Still

A non-response rule states which moves the firm will not answer. It is the less visible half of the stance and often the more valuable one. Many rival moves do not reach the firm's buyers: a cut in a segment the firm does not serve, a promotion aimed at the rival's own churned accounts, a new free tier sized below the firm's smallest paid step. Answering such moves spends margin on buyers who were not at risk and teaches rivals that any announcement provokes a reaction.

The non-response rule names the moves, the evidence that would change the decision, and the review date. Holding still is then a decision with an owner rather than an absence of attention. The record matters for learning, because a firm that writes down why it ignored a move can later see whether the move cost it accounts. Without the record, the losses that follow inaction are blamed on the decision to hold, and the losses that follow reaction are attributed to the rival.

The stance does not attempt to estimate an equilibrium. Uncertainty showed that thin data rarely identify a reaction function, and a rival list with response and non-response rules is a justified policy under that limit. Its test is whether the firm can explain, after each contested quarter, which rule fired, what it cost, and whether the rule should change. Rivals rarely move only on price, and the most consequential move is often the arrival of a new one, which Entry takes up.

### 11.2 Entry

#### Contestable Margins

A margin is contestable when a seller outside the current rival list could enter and serve the same buyers at a cost that the current price covers. Baumol, Panzar and Willig developed the limiting case in their theory of contestable markets (1982): if entry and exit were free and fast, the threat of entry alone would discipline price, even with a single incumbent. Technology markets rarely meet the limiting conditions, because building, certifying, and selling a product takes time and capital that cannot be recovered on exit. The theory remains useful as a reference point. The closer a segment comes to free entry, the less any incumbent price above cost can persist, whatever the number of current rivals.

The policy question is where the firm's margins are most exposed. Margins earned on work that is cheap to reproduce, sold to buyers who can switch at low cost, through channels that newcomers can reach, are contestable. Margins earned on assets that take time to build, which Differentiation listed as installed base, accumulated data, integrations, and certifications, are protected for as long as those assets take to replicate. A segment-by-segment inventory of exposed margin tells the firm where entry is likely to come first and where an unusually high price is a standing invitation.

Bain's study of barriers to new competition (1956) classified conditions of entry as blockaded, effectively impeded, or ineffectively impeded, which later work reads as the incumbent's choice among blocking, deterring, and accommodating entry. Sylos-Labini's analysis of oligopoly in the same period set out the limit-price assumption under which an entrant expects incumbents to hold output. Interaction already reviewed the credibility problem that later work by Dixit raised: a threat to fight after entry deters only if fighting would then be in the incumbent's interest. Competition takes those results as given and asks which option the firm states as policy.

#### Switching Costs

Switching costs decide how much an entrant must give up to win the firm's buyers. A buyer who has integrated a product, trained staff on it, and stored records in its format faces a cost of moving that a rival must cover through price, migration help, or both. Klemperer's analysis of markets with consumer switching costs (1987) showed the resulting pattern: sellers compete hard for new buyers and then price above cost to the buyers they have locked in. The pattern is familiar in software as low first-year prices followed by renewal increases.

The policy consequences run in two directions. Switching costs protect the installed base, so the firm can decline to match entrant prices on existing accounts with less risk than on new ones. They also cap what the firm can safely extract at renewal, because Interaction's discussion of repeated play applies: a buyer exploited at renewal remembers it, and an entrant offering to pay the migration cost becomes attractive. A labeled illustration with invented numbers states the bound. Suppose an account pays 50,000 per year and the buyer's cost to switch, counting migration and retraining, is 30,000 spread over a three-year contract, or 10,000 per year. An entrant that prices the same service at 38,000 leaves the buyer 12,000 better off each year before switching cost and 2,000 better off after it, so the account is at risk because the entrant's annual saving exceeds the buyer's annualized cost of switching by 2,000. A renewal increase of 5,000 would widen the buyer's gain from switching to 7,000 per year.

#### Accommodation

An incumbent whose margins are contestable has three stated options. It can price low enough that entry does not pay, at the cost of forgoing margin now. It can price high and plan to fight entrants, which works only if the fight is credible. Or it can accommodate, keeping its price on the segments it values and conceding a segment where an entrant's cost or product fits better. Fudenberg and Tirole (1984) catalogued these postures with memorable names, from the top dog that invests to look aggressive to the puppy dog that stays small to appear unthreatening and the fat cat that overinvests to look soft. The general lesson is that the right investment depends on whether the incumbent's commitment makes rivals more or less aggressive in response.

Accommodation is often the integrity-consistent choice. When Optimization's capacity and support constraints bind, fighting an entrant in a low-margin segment diverts resources from buyers the firm has promised to serve. Conceding that segment, while stating the margin floor and the conditions under which the firm would return, keeps commitments credible and preserves contribution where it is earned. Accommodation in this sense is a written decision, distinct from passivity, that names the segment conceded, the entrant expected, and the evidence that would reverse the decision.

Deterrence through price, by contrast, has a cost the firm pays whether or not entry would have come. A limit price that forgoes margin across a whole segment to discourage a single plausible entrant is a large insurance premium. The stance should state the premium, the probability of entry it assumes, and the review at which the assumption is checked against what entrants actually did. Once entrants are present, the firm's choice shifts from keeping them out to living with them on terms that preserve margin, which is the subject of Softening.

### 11.3 Softening

#### Matching Clauses

Softening means reducing what each seller gains from a pure price fight, so that rivals compete on the terms buyers value rather than on price alone. Differentiation supplied the main instrument: lines, versions, and bundles that separate the firm's buyers from the rival's and make a cut by one seller attract only the buyers for whom the difference was small. Interaction located the escape into product space; Softening asks how the firm's pricing rules, and not only its products, change the payoff from cutting.

Interaction noted that a public promise to match a rival's lower price can soften competition while appearing aggressive. Salop's analysis of practices that facilitate coordination (1986) gave the reasoning for two such commitments. A meet-competition clause promises a buyer that the seller will match a lower offer; a most-favored-customer clause promises a buyer the best price the seller gives anyone else. Both reduce a rival's gain from undercutting, the first because buyers need not switch to get the lower price and the second because a seller that cuts for one buyer must cut for all buyers covered by the clause. Each looks generous to the buyer who receives it and raises the cost of price cutting for the market as a whole.

That double reading makes these clauses a Policy matter with legal content. The same clause can protect a buyer and dampen rivalry, and competition authorities have examined both effects. The book leaves the legal analysis to Enterprise. For Competition the relevant point is narrower: the firm should know which of its contractual terms change rivals' incentives, record why each term exists, and treat a clause adopted to soften rivalry with the same scrutiny as any other commitment that binds future prices.

#### Doom Loops

A doom loop is a sequence of matched cuts in which each seller's response provokes the next, and neither recovers the margin given up. Interaction's undercutting illustration showed the single step: two firms with low incremental cost cut a third off their price for no change in share. The loop is the repetition of that step without a rule that ends it. It is most likely when rivals have slack capacity, near-zero incremental cost, and sales teams paid on bookings rather than contribution, which is the configuration of many software categories.

The firm's protection is a matching rule with limits written in advance: a band, a duration, an owner, and a floor tied to cost. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows how quickly an unbounded rule fails. Suppose a seat lists at 40 with an incremental cost of 8, so contribution is 32. Three rounds of matched ten percent cuts take the price to 40 x 0.9 x 0.9 x 0.9 = 29.16, and contribution to 21.16, a loss of about a third of contribution, while both sellers keep the shares they started with. A band that allows a single matched round on contested deals caps the loss at 4 per seat on those deals and leaves the list intact elsewhere.

Optimization would forbid most doom loops if its constraints were written down, because a margin floor that holds across the portfolio cannot coexist with an open-ended matching habit. The loop persists when the constraint lives only in a spreadsheet and the matching decision lives in each deal. Governance's exception records reveal the pattern: repeated temporary matches in the same segment, approved one at a time, are policy drift and should be read as a change in stance that nobody decided.

#### Ego Checks

Some price fights are driven less by economics than by identity. A sales culture that never loses a deal on price, or a founder who reads a rival's cut as an insult, turns matching into a measure of resolve. Behavior described how reference points and loss framing shape buyers; the same tendencies shape sellers, and a lost deal is felt more sharply than the margin given up across many matched ones.

The remedy is procedural. The stance names the owner of each matching decision, requires a stated reason tied to the response rule, and records the outcome. Reviews compare matched and unmatched deals in the same segment on retention and contribution, so the firm learns whether matching bought anything. Where a matched account churns at the same rate as an unmatched one, the match was a transfer to the buyer.

Softening has limits. Differentiation erodes as rivals copy, and clauses that soften competition draw scrutiny. Coordination that arises from software setting prices against rivals' prices is a question for the Engine part and for Enterprise, and the book returns to it there. Within Policy, Softening is the deliberate choice not to fight where fighting destroys contribution. Where the firm does choose to fight, it should do so as a designed episode, which is the subject of Contests.

### 11.4 Contests

#### Promotion Design

Contests are price fights that the firm chooses to enter, bounded in time and scope. Promotions, win-back offers, launch discounts, and share pushes in a target segment all qualify. Each is a temporary price below the stated list, aimed at a defined group of buyers, intended to change what those buyers do. The design question is what the contest is meant to buy and how the firm will know whether it bought it.

A designed contest has a written objective, a target segment, an instrument, a budget expressed in forgone contribution, a start and an end date, and an owner. The objective names the behavior sought, such as trials converted, accounts won from a named rival, or renewals secured, and not a share number alone. The budget makes the cost visible before the contest starts. The end date is part of the offer, communicated to buyers, so that the contest does not become the reference price that Behavior warned about. A promotion that buyers expect to recur teaches them to wait, and the firm then pays the discount on purchases that would have happened anyway.

Promotions are also visible to rivals, and the stance should say how the firm reads a rival's response. A rival that answers with a matched promotion of the same length is defending, and the contest will cost more than planned. A rival that holds still has judged that the contested buyers are not worth the fight, which tells the firm something about the rival's map. Either result is evidence for the next revision of the rival list.

#### Share, Margin

Share and margin trade against each other in every contest. Share has value when it brings later contribution: through renewals, through usage that grows, through network effects that Interaction described, or through cost that falls with volume. Share bought from buyers who leave when the promotion ends has little value. The firm's estimate of what an added account is worth over its life sets the most it should pay to win one, and the estimate should be written before the contest, not reconstructed after it.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the arithmetic. Suppose a win-back offer gives 25 percent off a 20,000 annual contract for the first year, a cost of 5,000 per account won. If each won account has a 60 percent chance of renewing at full price for a second year and contribution is 70 percent of price, the expected second-year contribution is 0.6 x 0.7 x 20,000 = 8,400. First-year contribution at the discounted price of 15,000 is 15,000 - 6,000 = 9,000, since incremental cost at full price is 6,000. The account therefore yields 9,000 + 8,400 = 17,400 over two years, against 14,000 + 0.6 x 14,000 = 22,400 had the same buyer been won at full price with the same renewal rate. The offer pays only to the extent that it wins accounts that would not otherwise have come, and the contest should measure that by comparison with a holdout group or a matched segment.

Contests often fail on the comparison. A promotion credited with a rise in sign-ups during a season when sign-ups rise anyway has bought nothing. Optimization's discipline applies: the objective and the constraints are stated, and the result is judged against the alternative the firm actually faced.

#### Exit Criteria

A contest without exit criteria becomes a permanent list cut by another name. The criteria are written at the start and are of two kinds. Success criteria end the contest when the objective is met, so that the firm does not keep paying for accounts it has already won. Failure criteria end it when the cost per won account exceeds the budget, when a rival's response has neutralized the offer, or when the accounts won churn faster than assumed. The owner reports against both at fixed intervals, and the decision to extend requires the same approval as the decision to start.

The record of each contest is what makes later contests better. It states the objective, the budget, what rivals did, what the firm won and at what cost, and which assumption failed. A firm that keeps such records learns which rivals defend, which segments respond to price, and how long a won account stays. The systematic use of that record, in experiments and in revisions of the stance, is the subject of Learning, which follows. The automation of contests by pricing engines, and the risk that engines setting prices against each other coordinate without instruction, belongs to the Engine part.

The hero narrative of the endless price war, won by the side with the larger war chest, survives because the costs of such wars are spread across quarters and the victories are announced. A contest designed with a budget, a comparison, and an exit leaves a record that the narrative cannot survive.

## Chapter 12 — Learning

### Introduction
#### Improvement

Chapter 12 of Pricing Tech is Learning. Part II has stated Optimization’s grammar, Governance’s owners, Segmentation and Differentiation’s who-pays-what structure, and Competition’s stance. None of that apparatus improves itself. Learning is Policy that revises on purpose: affordable experiments, updating that resists overfitting, organizational memory that survives churn, and loops that feed the next revision of objectives and instruments—still human-controlled, not yet an unsupervised engine. Bootstrapping will later encode these rules as machine-readable inputs; this chapter keeps the human loop closed.

The arc runs Experiments, Updating, Memory, Loops. Experiments design tests the firm can afford and interpret, including what not to A/B when promises, integrity, or sample size forbid it. Updating carries Uncertainty’s priors and signal discipline into Policy change without worshipping a week of conversion scraps. Memory stores what was tried, why, and under which Authority so personnel churn does not erase rationale. Loops set revision cadence and the hand-off toward Engine: learning rules Engine may inherit, not autonomy Engine may seize. Soft behavioralism tours remain out; Carnegie bounds from Behavior still limit how much “rational updating” an early team can perform.

#### Experiments, Updating

Experiments are designed under cost and integrity constraints. Affordable tests respect support load, brand promises, and Governance’s exception budget. Design hygiene—pre-stated metrics, stop rules, eligible segments—prevents Contests from Competition from becoming permanent list cuts disguised as learning. Sample size and selection matter as much as clever instrumentation: a test that only high-touch accounts ever see does not update policy for the freemium mass. Some instruments should not be A/B’d when the move is irreversible in the Options sense from Uncertainty, or when the promise integrity Optimization named would be broken by the test itself. Integrity here is efficiency and accountability for contributions, not an ethics sermon about laboratory subjects.

Updating moves from prior stance to revised policy with documented changes. Overfit risk is first-class: small samples, selected cohorts, and noisy freemium crumbs invite false precision. Act under residual uncertainty remains allowed; inventing identification is not. Audit from Governance is the record of what changed and why. Thin-data honesty from the book introduction and from Uncertainty is the epistemic parent: Learning improves Policy without pretending the spreadsheet suddenly matches academic identification assumptions.

#### Memory, Loops

Memory is organizational, not only personal. Tooling for rationale—linked to Authority seats and exception logs—keeps Strategy and Policy reusable when people leave. Without Memory, every quarter reinvents freemium logic, segment definitions, and competitive bands. With Memory, patterned exceptions and contest outcomes become inputs to Codification and stance revision rather than folklore.

Loops institutionalize revision cadence: when objectives, constraints, segment rules, or competitive bands are reopened, by whom, with what evidence threshold. Loops feed Optimization’s stated aims and Competition’s exit criteria back into the next Policy draft. The Part II close is explicit: human-controlled improvement that Engine can later encode. Autonomously bootstrapped loops and real-time portfolio vectors belong in Part III; Learning stops at the supervised institutional cycle.

The sequence Experiments, Updating, Memory, Loops is analytic. Updating without experimental design invites narrative revision. Memory without loops stores archives nobody revisits. Closing with Loops returns the reader to the book’s journey: Policy that can be taught and changed is the input Bootstrapping needs, not a slogan about being data-driven.

#### Learning Edges

Learning does not bootstrap autonomous engine loops or portfolio price vectors. It does not reopen Competition’s stance design except where revision attaches. It does not redo Uncertainty’s information account except as the epistemic constraint on experiments. What this chapter owns is Part II’s closing apparatus: experiments, disciplined updating, organizational memory, and human-controlled loops that hand structured learning rules toward Engine.

Learning in this sense is an organizational property rather than an individual skill. Boundedly rational teams forget, reinterpret, and overfit; written experimental rules, retained records, and scheduled revision economize on that scarce attention, so that what one cohort of decision makers discovers remains available to the next and to the engine that later inherits it.

### 12.1 Experiments

#### Affordable Tests

The introduction to Policy promised that learning would close the loop with experiments the firm can afford. Uncertainty set the ground for that promise. It ranked evidence, warned that cohort comparisons are confounded by timing and selection, and assigned structured experiments with randomized assignment to Policy. An experiment is the one source of evidence in which the firm controls who sees which price, so differences in outcome can be attributed to the price rather than to the season, the channel, or the type of buyer who happened to arrive.

Control is not free. Every test has a direct cost in revenue forgone on the losing arm, an operating cost in support load and sales confusion, and an attention cost that Behavior placed at the center of early decision making. A small team running four tests at once has usually run four poorly read tests. The useful question for a pricing function is which uncertainty, if resolved, would change a decision of consequence, and which test resolves it at the lowest of these costs. Options framed the same comparison for waiting: the value of information is high when the next decision is large and the current belief is wide.

Volume sets the limit. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows how quickly it binds. Suppose a product receives 600 qualified trials a month, splits them evenly between two prices, and converts about 5 percent of trials. Each arm then gains 300 x 0.05 = 15 conversions a month. If the team has stated that it will not read a result before each arm holds 200 conversions, the test runs 200 / 15, or about 13 months. Few pricing questions can wait that long. The firm then has three honest choices: test a larger difference, measure a more frequent outcome such as trial starts with its weaker link to contribution stated, or accept that the question will be answered by judgment and recorded as such.

#### Test Hygiene

Design hygiene is what makes a test readable after the fact. Four elements are written before launch. The first is the metric that decides, tied to the objective Optimization stated, usually contribution or contribution-weighted conversion rather than raw sign-ups. The second is the population: which segments, which channels, which geographies are eligible, and why. The third is the stop rule, stating the sample or duration at which the result is read. The fourth is the decision each plausible outcome would trigger, including the outcome in which the arms cannot be distinguished.

The stop rule carries most of the weight. Peter Armitage, C. K. McPherson, and B. C. Rowe (1969) showed that repeatedly testing accumulating data for significance, and stopping at the first favorable look, inflates the rate of false positives well above the nominal level. A pricing team that checks a dashboard daily and ends the test when the new price looks better commits exactly this error. Sequential designs exist that permit interim looks at a stated cost, and the multi-armed bandit literature, starting with William Thompson's 1933 proposal to assign trials in proportion to the probability that each option is best, shifts traffic toward the better arm as evidence accrues. Bandit allocation reduces revenue lost during the test; it does not remove the need to state in advance what counts as better, and it weakens the estimate of the losing arm, which matters when the losing arm is the current list price.

Selection is the other common failure. Competition required that contests be designed with exit criteria so that a promotion does not become a permanent list cut. The same discipline applies to tests. A price shown only to high-touch accounts teaches nothing about the self-serve base, and a discount offered only to buyers who asked for one measures the behavior of buyers who ask. Eligibility written in advance, and assignment the sales team cannot override, keep the comparison clean.

#### Untestable Moves

Some prices should not be tested by randomization at all. Options identified moves that are irreversible or costly to reverse: a public list cut that teaches buyers to wait, a grandfathered plan, a published commitment to a price path. Showing a lower list price to a random half of the market creates the reference point Behavior described for every buyer who sees it, and the firm cannot withdraw it from the half that saw it. For such moves the firm learns from staged rollout by segment or geography, with the confounding stated, or from the record of analogous decisions, rather than from a randomized split.

Promises set the second limit. Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, requires that stated policy and realized practice agree. A firm that has published one price for a plan cannot quietly charge a random subset of buyers more for the same plan without breaking that agreement, and buyers who compare quotes will discover the break. Tests that vary the offer visibly, such as a new tier, a different bundle, or a trial length, keep the promise intact because each buyer receives what was stated to that buyer. The criterion is efficiency and accountability for contribution: a test that saves forecast error but destroys the credibility of the list has a cost the test metric does not record.

Small samples set the third limit. Where the volume illustration shows that a clean answer would take a year, a randomized test still produces a number, and that number will be read. An experiment that cannot reach its stated sample is better replaced by a declared judgment than run as a test whose result is noise with a confidence interval attached. Updating, which follows, treats what to do with the evidence the firm does manage to gather.

### 12.2 Updating

#### Light Posteriors

Beliefs, in Uncertainty, asked the firm to state its priors and what would change them. Updating is the Policy counterpart: the procedure by which evidence from tests, contests, and ordinary sales revises a written belief and, through it, a written rule. The full apparatus of Bayesian inference is rarely needed. What is needed is the habit of treating each new result as an addition to what the firm already believed, weighted by how much evidence each side carries, rather than as a replacement for it.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the light version. Suppose a team believes its self-serve conversion at the current price is about 5 percent, and it records that belief as worth roughly 400 trials of evidence, meaning 20 conversions in 400 trials. A week at a new price produces 6 conversions in 60 trials, or 10 percent. Read alone, the week doubles conversion. Pooled with the prior, the estimate is 26 conversions in 460 trials, or about 5.7 percent. The week moves the belief, by less than a dashboard would suggest, and the size of the move depends openly on the weight the team assigned to its prior. Writing that weight down is the substantive step, because it exposes the prior to criticism before the data arrive. The pooled estimate also has a width. Evidence equivalent to 460 trials still leaves a wide range around 5.7 percent, and the size of any price change should match that range, as Thin Data advised for decisions taken within ranges.

The same arithmetic disciplines the reverse error. A team that refuses to move after strong, clean evidence has assigned its prior a weight no one would defend if it were stated. Stating the weight in trials converts a disagreement about intuition into a disagreement about a number, which a review can settle.

#### Overfitting

Thin data invite overfitting, and pricing data are thin in particular ways. Signals described conversion crumbs and cohort scraps: small counts, cohorts that differ in arrival channel, and freemium activity that correlates weakly with willingness to pay. A team that slices such data by segment, plan, geography, and month will find some cell where the new price did remarkably well. The finding is produced by the number of slices, not by buyers.

Three practices limit the damage. The first restricts the analysis to comparisons named before the data were seen, with any others labeled exploratory and carried forward as hypotheses for the next test rather than as grounds for a rule change. The second discounts effects in small cells toward the overall result, the same pooling the illustration applied to the prior, so that a segment with 30 trials does not receive a rule of its own. The third asks whether a result would survive a change in measurement: a lift in sign-ups that vanishes in paid conversion, or in conversion that vanishes in contribution after cost-to-serve, was a lift in the wrong metric.

Updating also guards against learning from selected outcomes. Organizations tend to study the decisions that went badly and to leave successful ones unexamined, and Behavior noted that Cyert and March found routines revised mainly after failure. A price that worked may have worked for reasons the firm did not intend. Reviewing a sample of successes along with failures, by the same criteria, removes part of that bias.

#### Change Notes

Uncertainty allowed the firm to act on residual uncertainty, and Updating keeps that permission. Evidence will rarely settle a pricing question to the standard of an academic identification strategy, and waiting for that standard is itself a decision with costs Options described. The obligation is to record the residual. A rule changed on partial evidence carries a note stating the evidence, the prior, the size of the revision, the uncertainty that remains, and the observation that would reverse the change.

That note is the unit of updating. Governance required that the pricing record allow Audit to reconstruct what changed and why. A change note meets that requirement at the moment of change, when the reasons are still known, instead of during a later review, when they must be inferred from the outcome. It names the owner who approved the change under the Authority that Governance assigned, the date, and the review date at which the evidence will be examined again.

Change notes also make updating cumulative. Each revision starts from the belief recorded at the previous revision rather than from whatever the current team remembers. A sequence of notes on the same rule shows whether the firm has been moving steadily toward a stable estimate or oscillating between two positions in response to noise. Oscillation is a sign that the prior weight is too low or that the metric is too volatile for the cadence of review. Either diagnosis is available only when the sequence exists in writing, and keeping that sequence beyond the tenure of the people who wrote it is the subject of Memory.

### 12.3 Memory

#### Retained Rationale

Learning that lives only in the people who did it leaves with them. Barbara Levitt and James March (1988) described organizational learning as the encoding of inferences from history into routines that guide behavior, so that lessons persist when the individuals who drew them have gone. Their account fits pricing closely. A discount band, a segment definition, or a competitive response rule is a routine, and each was written because someone drew an inference from experience. When the rule survives and the inference does not, the organization keeps a behavior it can no longer explain or revise.

What a pricing function needs to remember is therefore the rationale as well as the rule. For each rule in force, Memory keeps the evidence on which it was adopted, the alternatives considered and rejected, the change notes that Updating produced, the owner under Authority, and the conditions that would trigger review. For each test and contest, it keeps the design written before launch, the result, and the decision taken, including tests that found nothing. Null results are the part most often lost and among the most valuable, because they prevent the same weak idea from being tested again by the next team.

Memory also records decisions not to act. Competition asked the firm to write down why it ignored a rival's move. Kept and retrievable, those records let a later review see whether holding still cost accounts, which is information no market dataset contains.

Memory is selective by design. Recording every quote and every conversation would bury the inferences under transactions. The record keeps decisions and the evidence behind them, and leaves transaction detail to the systems that already hold it, linked by reference. The criterion for what to keep is whether a successor proposing to change the rule would need the item to judge the proposal.

#### Turnover

Early firms lose people quickly, and pricing knowledge is concentrated in few of them. The founder who set the first price, the salesperson who negotiated the largest exceptions, and the analyst who built the cost-to-serve model may all leave within a few years. Behavior described how an exception culture forms when rules are unwritten. Turnover converts that culture into permanent loss: the exceptions remain in the contracts, and the reasons for them leave with the people.

Levitt and March noted that routines can outlast their reasons and that organizations may become skilled at a procedure that no longer suits their environment, a pattern they called a competency trap. Turnover deepens the trap. A successor who inherits a rule without its rationale either follows it rigidly, because there is no basis for change, or discards it, because there is no basis for keeping it. Neither response is learning. With the rationale on record, the successor can test the inference that produced the rule against current conditions and revise only the part that no longer holds.

Memory therefore belongs to the function, not to individuals. Each record names the role that owns it, so that responsibility transfers with the role when the person changes. Handover between owners includes the open review dates and the rules approaching them. The practice is administrative, and its value lies in being routine: the record is kept because the procedure requires it, not because a particular person thought to keep it.

#### Retrieval

A record that cannot be found at the moment of decision is not memory. Pricing records accumulate in contract systems, spreadsheets, chat messages, and slide decks, each searchable in its own way and none linked to the rule it concerns. Tooling for rationale links each rule to its history, so that a person proposing a change sees the earlier tests, the change notes, and the exceptions granted under that rule in one place.

The link is what prevents reinvention. Without it, each planning cycle reopens freemium limits, segment boundaries, and competitive bands as if for the first time, and the firm repeats tests whose results it already holds. With it, patterned exceptions from Governance and contest outcomes from Competition become evidence for Codification and for revisions of the stance rather than folklore told by whoever remembers them. Memory also lowers the attention cost of review, which Behavior identified as the binding constraint on a small team, because a reviewer reads a summary of what is known rather than reconstructing it.

Retrieval imposes a discipline on writing. Records are useful only if they share a structure: the same fields for every rule, the same design template for every test, the same vocabulary for segments and instruments that Codification fixed. A free-text archive grows faster than anyone can read it. A structured record grows more slowly, and it can later be queried by a person and, in Part III, read by a system. That structure is what Loops uses to schedule revision, and it is the form in which learning rules will eventually reach the engine.

### 12.4 Loops

#### Revision Cadence

Experiments, Updating, and Memory supply evidence, procedure, and record. Loops make them recur. A loop is a schedule and a set of triggers that bring each pricing rule back for review: who reopens it, how often, on what evidence, and with what threshold for change. Without a loop, revision happens when a crisis forces it or when a new executive arrives, and both occasions favor wholesale change over calibrated adjustment.

Cadence differs by rule. Objectives and the constraints that Optimization named change slowly and warrant review perhaps once a year, or when the firm's funding or market position shifts. Segment definitions and version fences move with the product and the buyer base and suit a quarterly review. Discount bands and competitive response rules face the fastest change and may need monthly attention, together with event triggers such as a rival's list move or an exception rate above the level Governance set. Each rule's cadence is written into its record in Memory, and the review calendar is derived from those records rather than from habit.

Each review ends in one of three recorded outcomes: the rule is confirmed, revised with a change note, or retired. A review that produces no record has not occurred for the purposes of Memory, and the next review starts without its evidence.

Behavior placed attention at the center of early decision making, and cadence is how a small team rations it. A schedule that reopens every rule every month exhausts the team and invites the oscillation Updating warned against. A schedule that reopens rules too rarely lets them drift away from conditions. Matching cadence to the speed at which each rule's environment changes is the economy Loops provides.

#### Double Loops

Chris Argyris and Donald Schön, in Organizational Learning (1978), distinguished single-loop learning, which corrects actions to meet given goals and norms, from double-loop learning, which questions the goals and norms themselves. Both occur in pricing. Adjusting a discount band because win rates fell below target is single-loop. Asking whether win rate was the right target, when contribution per account fell while win rates held, is double-loop.

Most pricing review is single-loop by default, because the objective is treated as fixed and only the instruments are revisited. Loops reserve a place for the second kind. The annual review of objectives takes as evidence the record of what instruments achieved and at what cost, and asks whether the stated aims of Optimization still describe what the firm is trying to do. Competition's exit criteria feed the same review: a contest that met its share target and failed its margin limit is evidence about the stance, not only about the contest.

James March (1991) described the tension between exploration, the search for new possibilities, and exploitation, the refinement of what is already known, and argued that adaptive processes tend to favor exploitation because its returns are nearer and more certain. A pricing function that only refines its current tiers will become efficient at a structure that may be wrong. Loops make exploration explicit by budgeting a share of tests for alternatives the current policy does not contain, such as a different meter or a new bundle, and by recording those tests as exploration so that their lower success rate is expected rather than counted against the team.

#### Engine Handoff

Learning closes Part II. Policy now has a grammar in Optimization, owners and records in Governance, structures for who pays what in Segmentation and Differentiation, a stance in Competition, and in Learning a procedure for improving all of them. Every element remains under human control. A person approves each change, a review reads each record, and a loop runs at the pace people can sustain.

That pace is the limit Part III addresses. As the catalog grows and the environment moves faster, the number of rules due for review exceeds what any team can attend to, and the latency between evidence and revision becomes a cost in its own right. The response is not to abandon the loop but to encode it. The stop rules from Experiments, the pooling and change notes from Updating, the structured records from Memory, and the cadences and triggers from Loops are learning rules: stated procedures for turning evidence into revised prices. Because they are written, they can be inherited.

Engine inherits these rules as inputs; it does not acquire authority to rewrite them. A system that updates prices within bands set by a learning rule is executing Policy. A system that changes the objective it is updating toward has taken a double-loop decision that Loops reserved for people. The handoff therefore carries both the rules and their boundary: what the engine may revise on its own, what it must escalate, and which reviews remain human. Bootstrapping, which opens Part III, begins with that inheritance, encoding the inputs Policy built into a minimum engine that a person can still supervise and stop.

# Part III — Engine

## Introduction
### Item Failure

Part III of Pricing Tech concerns the Engine: pricing when item-by-item human control fails. The book proceeds from Strategy through Policy to Engine. Strategy recovered economic reasoning under thin data. Policy turned that reasoning into human-controlled rules—objectives, governance, segments, competitive stances, learning loops. Both parts assume that a person can own the next price move for the next SKU. At sufficient portfolio size and environmental speed, that assumption fails. Exception queues overflow, approval latency becomes a cost, and the firm needs a disciplined mapping from environment to prices that does not require a founder in every cell of the catalog.

The Engine is that mapping. It is neither a general commitment to being data-driven nor a vendor's implementation guide. It is a function the enterprise can state: it takes encoded inputs from Policy, together with the economic structure Strategy made explicit, and produces a real-time price vector for the existing portfolio, along with indications of how the portfolio should evolve. Simulation, conjectures about buyers and rivals, and an accountability envelope belong to the function itself.

Growth makes the failure mode concrete. A catalog of three plans can be priced in a meeting. A catalog of three hundred offers across regions and contract generations cannot. Policy can be correct and still too slow if every exception requires a human signature. In Carnegie terms, the organization's attention becomes the binding constraint. The Engine exists to carry Policy at speed, not to replace it with a separate doctrine of automation.

### Engine Scope

An engine consumes what Parts I and II built. It does not re-derive willingness to pay from first principles, and it does not construct governance after the fact. Objectives, constraints, segment logic, competitive conjectures, and learning rules arrive as inputs—machine-readable enough to run and legible enough to audit. Auditability is a defining requirement: a price that cannot be traced to inputs and policy substitutes an opaque system for folklore, which leaves the firm no better able to explain or revise its prices.

The substance of Part III is therefore operational as well as mathematical. Bootstrapping asks which minimum loop is worth supervising first and which data debt can be deferred. Dynamics asks what fails when prices move over time—path dependence, oscillation, feedback that amplifies noise. Intelligence asks how conjectures and scenarios remain open to discussion within the firm. Portfolio asks how hundreds of SKUs are coupled through cannibalization and shared constraints into a single vector problem. Scaling asks what fails as throughput and the number of markets grow. Enterprise asks who owns the function when regulators and customers still require someone to answer for a price.

Integrity remains the frame for constraints: efficiency in resource use and accountability for contributions along the value chain. Formalism stays light enough that a reader who skips an equation still retains the argument.

### Six Stages

Bootstrapping moves policy rules into inputs and supervised first loops. Dynamics adds movement, installed base, stability, and feedback. Intelligence adds conjectures, simulation, and scenarios that remain auditable. Portfolio couples SKUs into a constrained price vector. Scaling addresses limits, throughput, multi-market rollout, and failure modes. Enterprise closes with a dedicated function, regulatory and contractual envelopes, and accountability.

The order follows dependence. Dynamics presupposes a running loop. Intelligence without bootstrapped inputs has nothing to reason about. A portfolio vector without constraints and conjectures reduces to an unconstrained spreadsheet. Scale without portfolio logic multiplies errors. An enterprise function without accountability reproduces the opaque system under a new name.

Typical problems illustrate the scope: encoding discount and segment policy so that a system can propose prices that humans can still override; stabilizing prices that follow noisy demand; pricing many SKUs while accounting for cannibalization; deciding when to remove approval from a class of moves; and establishing a pricing function that outsiders can hold to account.

### Inheritance

Part III extends founder judgment and policy governance rather than abolishing them. Strategy remains the economic structure, Policy the rule structure, and the Engine the execution structure under speed and portfolio load. A hand-set freemium decision can, if the firm builds the intermediate stages, become one input to an auditable engine that prices a portfolio in real time. The middle chapters are prerequisites for that outcome, not preliminaries to be skipped. Autonomy in this account is the operational form of inherited rules, justified by the failure of item-level control and bounded by the accountability the enterprise retains.

## Chapter 13 — Bootstrapping

### Introduction
#### First Loop

Chapter 13 of Pricing Tech is Bootstrapping. Learning closed Part II with human-controlled experiments, updating, memory, and loops that hand structured learning rules toward Engine. Part III’s introduction already stated the Engine as a mapping from environment to a real-time price vector when item-by-item human control fails. Bootstrapping is the first passage from Policy into a runnable system: encode inputs, shrink to a minimum useful loop, rank data debt honestly, and supervise early runs with override and kill switches. Dynamics, portfolio coupling, and enterprise design come later. Autonomy as fashion is out of scope; inheritance of Strategy and Policy as operational inputs is the point.

The arc runs Inputs, Minimum Engine, Data Debt, Supervision. Inputs make objectives, constraints, segment rules, and the instrument catalog machine-readable enough to execute and human-legible enough to audit. Minimum Engine defines the smallest useful loop, its success criteria, and explicit non-goals. Data Debt separates gaps that block the loop from gaps that can wait behind proxies. Supervision keeps Optimization’s human-in-the-loop and Governance’s authority attached: approval classes, kill switches, and graduation criteria for widening autonomy. Intelligence’s conjectures and Portfolio’s vector problem are not yet required for the first supervised loop.

#### Inputs, Engine

Inputs are Policy made executable. Objectives and constraints from Optimization, segment eligibility from Codification, instrument catalogs from Differentiation, competitive bands from Competition, and learning rules from Loops must arrive in forms a system can apply without reinterpreting hallway folklore each night. Encoding is not a full formalization of Strategy. It is enough structure that a proposed price can be traced to a rule set Audit can reconstruct. If the input cannot be stated, the engine cannot inherit it; Bootstrapping forces that confrontation before Dynamics multiplies motion.

Minimum Engine is a scope cut. The smallest useful loop might cover one region, one product family, or one instrument class—list proposals inside a band, for example—while the rest remains on Policy exception paths. Success criteria are operational: latency, override rate, contribution within constraints, reconstructability. Non-goals matter equally: no claim to price the whole catalog, no unsupervised rival response, no portfolio-optimal vector on day one. Behavior’s attention bounds apply to the build team as much as to buyers; a sprawling first engine recreates exception culture inside engineering.

#### Human Review

Data Debt is honesty about gaps. Some missing measurements block safe automation; others can wait behind proxies and human review. Uncertainty’s thin-data stance returns: Bootstrapping does not invent identification to justify a loop. It ranks debt—what blocks, what can wait, which proxies are temporary—and records that ranking under Governance so debt does not silently become doctrine. Proxy inputs that systematically bias a segment or a contest exit fail integrity in the sense Value defined and Governance extended: they misdirect resources, obscure contributions, or let stated policy and practice diverge.

Supervision is the control plane for early loops. Human override, approval classes by instrument risk, and kill switches are not transitional embarrassment; they are how Policy survives speed. Graduation criteria state what evidence widens a class of moves from approval-required to supervised-automatic. Without graduation criteria, the firm either never leaves manual mode or removes approval by fatigue. Bootstrapping therefore binds Engine to Governance from the first commit.

The sequence Inputs, Minimum Engine, Data Debt, Supervision is analytic. A minimum loop without encoded inputs is a spreadsheet with a scheduler. Inputs without supervision recreate the black box Part III rejects. Closing with Supervision returns the reader to the book’s end-state claim: an auditable engine that carries Policy at speed, not a substitute for it.

#### Loop Scope

Bootstrapping does not treat full price dynamics, portfolio coupling across hundreds of SKUs, or enterprise organizational design. It does not reopen Learning’s experimental design except where rules become inputs.
