What this chapter owns is the first supervised passage: encode, shrink, defer debt honestly, and keep humans able to stop the loop.

### 13.1 Inputs

#### Executable Policy

Learning ended Part II with a claim that its rules, because they are written, can be inherited. Inheritance requires a format. A policy document can state that discounts above a threshold need approval, that renewals may not rise more than a stated percentage, and that a segment qualifies on firm size. A system applies only what it is given, and it applies it every time, without the tacit corrections a reviewer adds when a rule reads strangely. The first task of Bootstrapping is therefore translation: restating Policy so that a machine can execute it and an auditor can still read it.

Objectives come first. Optimization required the firm to state what it maximizes, over what horizon, and with what weights when goals conflict. For an engine, that statement becomes a scoring rule that ranks candidate prices. The scoring rule need not be elaborate. Expected contribution over twelve months, net of a stated penalty for churn risk, is a usable objective if the terms are defined and the inputs that feed them are named. What fails is an objective held only as a sentiment, such as growth with discipline, because no candidate price can be ranked against it.

Constraints come next. Margin floors, promise limits, and the other bounds Constraints in Optimization introduced become hard limits or penalized limits in the engine. A hard limit removes every candidate price that violates it. A penalized limit admits violations at a stated cost, which is how the shadow prices of 7.2 enter operational form. Choosing which constraints are hard is a policy decision that the encoding makes visible, and Governance owns it. An engineer who quietly converts a floor into a penalty has changed Policy, and the change belongs in the change record rather than in a configuration file nobody reviews.

#### Segment Rules

Segmentation and Codification left the firm with eligibility rules: which buyers qualify for which schedule, on what evidence, and with what fences. In a human process, eligibility is often settled in conversation. A sales representative knows the customer is really a mid-market account despite a headcount just over the line. Encoded, the rule either admits that judgment as a named exception class or excludes it. Silent reinterpretation is the indefensible option, because it produces two eligibility rules, one written and one practiced, which Governance identified as drift.

Each segment rule therefore needs three parts in the input layer: the attributes it reads, the source of each attribute, and the action taken when an attribute is missing or stale. The third part is where most early failures occur. A rule that reads firm size from a customer record will meet records with no size, records last updated three years ago, and records entered by the customer with an obvious incentive to understate. Specifying the default for missing data is a pricing decision. Defaulting to the higher schedule protects margin and invites disputes; defaulting to the lower schedule invites leakage. The input layer records which default Policy chose and why, so that 13.3 can treat the missing attribute as debt rather than as an invisible assumption.

Fences carry over in the same way. Differentiation treated fences as conditions that keep buyers in the schedule meant for them: contract length, volume commitments, feature tiers, channel. An engine that proposes prices across segments has to know which fences are enforced by the product, which by contract, and which only by the expectation that buyers will not ask. The last category is fragile under automation. A human sales process may never quote the lower schedule to a buyer who has not asked; an engine exposed through a self-service channel will quote it to anyone who meets the encoded conditions.

#### Instrument Catalog

The final input is the catalog of instruments the engine is permitted to move. Instruments in Optimization listed list prices, discount rules, meters, and bundles as the levers Policy controls. An engine needs the same list in explicit form, with each instrument tagged by its permitted range, its step size, its owner, and its reversibility. A renewal uplift, a volume tier boundary, and a promotional discount are separate instruments with different consequences for buyers who have already contracted.

Reversibility deserves its own field because it governs supervision later. A temporary promotional price can be withdrawn at the end of its term with modest cost. A change to a published list price shifts the reference point Behavior described, and reversing it is visible to buyers as a second change rather than a correction. A change to contract terms may be irreversible for the life of the contract. Tagging instruments by reversibility lets 13.4 attach approval classes to instruments instead of to individual price proposals, which is the only arrangement that remains workable as volume grows.

Competition contributes a further field. Where Policy set a stance toward a rival, such as matching within a band on a named product line, the catalog records the band, the observed rival price it references, and the source of that observation. The engine at this stage does not respond to rivals on its own initiative; it applies the stated stance to the observed input. Strategic interaction in motion, with installed base and feedback, belongs to Dynamics.

Taken together, objectives, constraints, segment rules, and the instrument catalog form the input layer. Its test is reconstruction. For any price the engine proposes, a reviewer should be able to name the objective that ranked it, the constraints that bounded it, the segment rule that placed the buyer, and the instrument that moved, each traceable to a dated policy decision. Where reconstruction fails, the gap is either a missing input or an unrecorded policy choice, and both belong in the change record before the first loop runs.

### 13.2 Minimum Engine

#### Smallest Loop

A pricing engine in its full form, as the introduction to Part III described it, maps the environment to a price vector for the whole portfolio. The first engine a firm builds should do far less. The minimum engine is the smallest loop that proposes real prices, sends them through Policy, records outcomes, and returns evidence that the loop is worth widening. Anything smaller is a model that never touches a price. Anything larger accumulates failure modes before the team has learned which ones matter.

Simon's account of satisficing, carried from Principles and Behavior, applies to the builders. A search for the optimal first engine has no stopping rule, because the space of possible designs is large and the evidence about which design is best arrives only after one has run. An aspiration level stops the search: an engine that proposes prices in a narrow scope, inside existing constraints, with an override rate low enough to save reviewer time, clears the bar. The aspiration can rise once the loop has run. Setting it high at the outset produces a design document rather than a running loop.

The loop itself has four steps. The engine reads encoded inputs and current observations, proposes a price for each item in scope, routes each proposal through the approval class its instrument requires, and writes the proposal, the decision, and the subsequent outcome to the record Memory established. The last step is the one most often deferred and the one that matters most. A loop that proposes and applies prices without recording why, and what followed, cannot be audited and cannot learn, which leaves it as opaque as the folklore it replaced.

#### Scope Cuts

Scope is cut along dimensions that limit exposure while preserving a meaningful test. One product family rather than the catalog, one region rather than every market, one instrument class rather than all levers, one segment where data are cleanest. A common first scope is list-price proposals for a single family within a band Policy has already approved, with discounts left to the existing deal desk. Such a scope touches real revenue, exercises the input layer, and keeps the consequences of an error within limits a review meeting can absorb.

The choice of scope should favor areas where the engine has a reason to outperform the current process. Items that rarely change price offer little to learn and little to save. Items whose prices are revised often, under a stable policy, with outcomes observed quickly, give the loop many cycles and the team many observations. High-stakes negotiated contracts are poor first candidates for the opposite reason: few cycles, slow outcomes, and large consequences per error.

Scope cuts are recorded as policy, not as engineering convenience. Items outside the scope remain on the Policy paths Part II built, including exceptions under Governance. The boundary between the two regimes is itself a source of error, because buyers and sales staff will notice when two similar items are priced by different processes. Recording the boundary, and the reason for it, prevents the boundary from hardening into an accident nobody remembers choosing.

#### Success Criteria

Success criteria for the minimum engine are operational before they are economic. The first questions are whether proposals arrive on time, whether they respect every hard constraint, whether reviewers can reconstruct each one from inputs, and how often reviewers override. Only after those hold does contribution become the measure, and then against the objective encoded in 13.1 rather than against a general sense of improvement.

An illustration shows how the criteria combine. The numbers are invented. Suppose the scope covers 250 items, each eligible for one list-price proposal per week, and reviewers currently spend 6 minutes per item reviewed, so a full weekly review would take 250 x 6 = 1,500 minutes, or 25 hours. If the engine's proposals are accepted unchanged for 80 percent of items and reviewers spend 2 minutes confirming each accepted proposal and the original 6 minutes on each of the rest, weekly review time becomes 200 x 2 + 50 x 6 = 400 + 300 = 700 minutes, a saving of 800 minutes. The saving is real only if the accepted proposals are no worse on contribution than the manual prices they replace, which is why the record of outcomes in the loop is a success criterion and not an afterthought.

Non-goals belong beside the criteria. The minimum engine does not price the whole portfolio, does not respond to rival moves beyond the stance encoded in its inputs, does not set prices that couple items through cannibalization, and does not run without approval in any class. Each of these is deferred to a later chapter: movement and feedback to Dynamics, conjectures about rivals and buyers to Intelligence, the coupled vector to Portfolio, and growth across markets to Scaling. Writing the non-goals down protects the team against scope added by enthusiasm. It also protects the engine against judgment by the wrong standard, since a minimum engine evaluated as though it were the full engine will always appear to fail.

### 13.3 Data Debt

#### Hidden Costs

Every engine starts with gaps in its data. Some attributes the input layer needs are missing for many records, some are stale, some are measured by a proxy that roughly tracks the quantity Policy cares about. The term data debt names the accumulated cost of those gaps: work deferred now that will have to be paid, with interest, as the engine widens.

The metaphor comes from software practice. D. Sculley and coauthors, in "Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems" (2015), argued that machine-learned components incur maintenance costs that ordinary code does not. Among their observations were that the learned model is a small part of a deployed system surrounded by much larger infrastructure, that data dependencies cost more than code dependencies because they are harder to see, and that in an entangled system changing any input changes everything downstream. Their warning applies directly to pricing. A price engine that reads a dozen inputs, several of them proxies, will inherit any drift in those inputs without announcing it, and the drift will surface first as a pattern in overrides or disputes rather than as an error message.

The interest on data debt is paid in three ways. Proposals become wrong in ways reviewers cannot detect, because the inputs look plausible. Audits become slower, because reconstructing a proposal requires investigating its sources. And Policy decisions become entangled with data accidents, so that a revision intended to change a margin floor also changes which buyers qualify for a segment because both read the same flawed attribute.

#### Blocking Gaps

Not all debt is equal, and Bootstrapping requires ranking it. The first class is debt that blocks the loop. A gap blocks when the engine cannot respect a hard constraint without the missing data, when the absence would place buyers in the wrong segment in ways that violate commitments, or when it makes proposals impossible to reconstruct. Incremental cost for a product line whose margin floor is a hard constraint is an example: without it, the engine cannot know whether a proposal clears the floor. Contract terms that cap renewal increases are another. These gaps are paid before the first loop runs, or the items affected are cut from scope under 13.2.

The second class is debt that can wait. A gap can wait when its absence makes proposals less precise without making them unsafe, and when human review in the supervision layer can catch the errors it produces. A demand estimate based on thin data, flagged as such under the stance Uncertainty set, is often of this kind. The engine proposes conservatively within a band, reviewers see the uncertainty flag, and the gap shrinks as the loop records outcomes.

The third class is debt that can wait indefinitely because its value is low. Attributes that would refine a segment rule marginally, or measurements of conditions that rarely bind, can remain unpaid. Recording them as debt, rather than forgetting them, keeps the decision open for review if conditions change.

#### Proxy Honesty

Proxies fill most of the gaps that can wait. Company headcount stands in for willingness to pay, seat count for value delivered, list price of a rival's nearest product for the rival's realized price. Each proxy is defensible as a temporary measure. Each also biases proposals in a predictable direction for some group of buyers, and the bias is invisible unless it is stated.

The honest use of a proxy requires four entries in the debt register: the quantity the proxy stands in for, the direction in which it is likely to err, the segment or item where the error is largest, and the evidence that would retire it. A rival's list price, for instance, overstates the realized price in markets where discounting is common; the error is largest in enterprise deals; and it is retired when the firm has enough win and loss records to estimate realized rival prices. Written this way, the proxy becomes a dated assumption that Governance can review rather than a fact the engine treats as settled.

Integrity sets the limit. Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, concerns efficiency in resource use, accountability for contributions, and agreement between stated policy and practice. A proxy that consistently charges one segment more than its contribution warrants, or that excludes a class of buyers from a schedule for reasons unrelated to cost or value, fails that standard, however convenient it is for the engine. Such a proxy is blocking debt even when the loop runs without it.

The debt register lives under Governance alongside the change record. It is reviewed on the cadence Loops set, and each review asks whether any waiting debt has become blocking, whether any proxy has drifted from the quantity it represents, and whether the loop's outcomes now allow a proxy to be retired. Debt that is never reviewed becomes doctrine: a gap that the engine treats as truth because nobody remembers that it was a stand-in.

### 13.4 Supervision

#### Approval Classes

The first loop runs under supervision, and the supervision is not new. Control in Optimization argued that a model aids a decision but does not replace the person accountable for it, and that override rights belong with the owner Governance names. Authority in Governance assigned decision rights and escalation paths; Audit required that changes and their reasons be recorded. Bootstrapping inherits all of this. What it adds is an arrangement in which supervision scales with volume, so that human attention goes to the proposals where it matters most.

The arrangement is a set of approval classes attached to instruments. The instrument catalog in 13.1 tagged each instrument by range, step size, and reversibility. Approval classes group them by risk. A low-risk class, such as small list-price moves within an approved band on reversible instruments, may be applied after review in batches. A middle class, such as larger moves or changes to items with many existing contracts, requires item-level approval by the owner. A high-risk class, including any change to contract terms, published price architecture, or segment eligibility, stays outside the engine's authority altogether and returns to the Policy paths Governance defined.

Classes make override cheaper. A reviewer who sees a batch of proposals, each with its reconstruction from inputs, can override the few that look wrong without examining the rest in detail. Every override is recorded with a reason, and the override record becomes evidence. A cluster of overrides on one segment points to a flawed rule or proxy; a cluster on one reviewer points to a disagreement about Policy that belongs in Governance rather than in repeated corrections.

#### Kill Switches

A supervised loop also needs a way to stop. A kill switch halts the engine's proposals for a defined scope and returns the affected items to their last approved prices or to manual handling. Its design questions are who may use it, what triggers it, and what happens to prices already applied.

Authority over the switch should be wider than authority over prices. The owner of an instrument class may stop it, but so may the person responsible for audit, and so may an automatic trigger. Triggers are stated in advance: a proposal that would violate a hard constraint, an input whose value falls outside its historical range, a share of overrides above a threshold in a single cycle, or a sudden change in observed volume on items in scope. The aim is to stop the loop when its inputs or outputs suggest it is operating outside the conditions under which it was approved, before anyone has to decide whether a particular price is wrong.

Rolling back is the harder part. Reversible instruments can return to prior values. Irreversible ones cannot, which is why 13.1 tagged them and why the high-risk class keeps them outside the engine. A switch that is never tested will fail when needed, so the first loop's success criteria include a test stop, recorded like any other event.

#### Graduation Criteria

Supervision is a stage, not a permanent state, and widening autonomy requires stated criteria. Graduation criteria specify what evidence moves a class of moves from item-level approval to batch review, or from batch review to supervised automatic application with sampled audit. Without them, two failures are common. Some firms never leave manual approval, so the engine saves no attention. Others drop approval informally as reviewers tire of confirming proposals they no longer read, which removes supervision without a decision anyone made.

Criteria are written in terms of the records the loop already keeps: override rate, reconstruction failures, constraint violations caught by review, and contribution relative to the prior process. An illustration, with invented numbers: suppose a class is eligible to graduate after eight consecutive weeks with an override rate below 5 percent and no constraint violations. If the class produced 400 proposals over the eight weeks and reviewers overrode 12, the override rate is 12 / 400 = 3 percent, which clears the threshold. If 4 of those 12 overrides concerned the same segment, the review examines that segment's rule before graduating the class, because a low aggregate rate can conceal a concentrated problem.

Graduation is reversible. A class that has graduated returns to closer supervision if its criteria are breached, and the reversal is recorded under Governance like the graduation itself. The decision belongs to the owner Governance names, informed by the records, rather than to the engineering team that built the loop, since the team that built it has the strongest reason to believe it works.

With inputs encoded, a minimum loop running, debt ranked, and supervision in place, the engine exists in its first useful form. It runs in a stable environment and prices items one at a time. Dynamics takes up what changes when prices and buyers move over time, and Portfolio takes up what changes when items are priced together.

## Chapter 14 — Dynamics

### Introduction
#### Moving Prices

Chapter 14 of Pricing Tech is Dynamics. Bootstrapping produced a supervised first loop: encoded inputs, a minimum engine, ranked data debt, and human override. A static policy encoded once still fails when the environment moves. Dynamics asks what changes when prices are paths rather than points: environmental movement and re-pricing cadence, path dependence from installed base and legacy promises, stability versus oscillation, and feedback that carries signal into the engine without amplifying noise. Intelligence will later add conjectures and scenarios; Portfolio will later couple many SKUs. This chapter owns motion inside a running loop.

The arc runs Movement, Path Dependence, Stability, Feedback. Movement names environment change, price paths, re-pricing cadence, and the cost of staleness. Path Dependence treats installed base, switching history, ratchets, and legacy promises as constraints on feasible moves—Options from Uncertainty reappear as irreversible or costly-to-reverse commitments already in the field. Stability diagnoses oscillation risk, damping, and bounds on move size when responsiveness fights quiet. Feedback designs lag structures and policy-in-the-loop so Learning’s signal discipline survives automation. Soft behavioralism is out; Carnegie attention bounds still limit how often the firm can sensibly re-price.

#### Installed Base

Movement is the failure of frozen Policy under time. Demand, cost-to-serve, rival lists, and capacity shift; a price that was within Optimization’s constraints yesterday can violate them tomorrow without any change in the rule text. Paths matter as much as levels: an early-bird window, a promotional contest, and a quiet list hold are different trajectories even when they share a terminal price. Re-pricing cadence is therefore an Engine design choice: too slow and staleness taxes contribution; too fast and the firm pays Behavior’s attention cost and Governance’s audit load. Bootstrapping’s graduation criteria must include cadence classes, not only instrument classes.

Path Dependence records that history binds. Installed-base contracts, grandfathered tiers, early-bird cohorts, and public list anchors change what a “simple” cut or hike does. Ratchets—once taught to wait for discounts, buyers wait—are Interaction and Uncertainty problems now executed at Engine speed. Legacy promises fail integrity when the engine “optimizes” them away without Authority and Audit. Dynamics therefore treats history as input, not as noise to be smoothed over.

#### Stability, Feedback

Stability is control under responsiveness pressure. Engines that chase every conversion scrap oscillate: list up, conversion down, list down, contribution destroyed. Bounds on move size, damping rules, and dead bands are instruments for quiet, not for cowardice. The tradeoff between stability and responsiveness must be stated as Policy constraint the engine inherits—otherwise Softening’s doom loops from Competition reappear as algorithmic matching.

Feedback closes the loop from market signal into engine state. Lag structures matter: acting on yesterday’s noise as if it were today’s demand repeats Uncertainty’s overfit error at higher frequency. Which signals enter—conversion crumbs, support load, rival list moves, capacity—must be named the way Segmentation named observables, or the engine will optimize a convenient metric that is not the objective. Policy-in-the-loop means Learning’s update rules and Governance’s exception patterns still gate what feedback is allowed to change. Kill switches from Bootstrapping remain the hard stop when feedback amplifies.

The sequence Movement, Path Dependence, Stability, Feedback is analytic. Stability without movement diagnosis treats symptoms. Feedback without path dependence ignores why some moves are costly. Closing with Feedback returns the reader to Part III’s premise: an auditable mapping that moves with the environment without becoming a noise amplifier.

#### Motion Limits

Dynamics does not fully specify Intelligence’s conjecture and simulation stacks, nor Portfolio’s multi-SKU vector problem. It does not redesign Bootstrapping’s input encoding except where cadence and move bounds attach. What this chapter owns is motion in the running engine: environmental change, historical binds, stability, and disciplined feedback.

Dynamics thus adds time to the engine's accountability. A price that is defensible at a single moment can still be indefensible as a path, and the chapter requires that the path itself, not only its points, be traceable to inputs and policy.

### 14.1 Movement

#### Drifting Inputs

A price set once and left alone is a bet that the conditions behind it will hold. They rarely do. Unit cost moves with input prices and volume, cost-to-serve moves with the mix of accounts, rivals revise their lists, and capacity fills and empties with the season. Bootstrapping built a loop that reads encoded inputs and proposes prices inside approved bands. Movement asks what happens to that loop when the inputs drift between one proposal and the next, and when the drift itself has a direction.

Two kinds of change need separate treatment. Some movement is transient: a week of unusual demand, a rival's short promotion, a supplier delay that clears. Other movement is persistent: a permanent rise in input cost, a new entrant that stays, a shift in what buyers expect to pay. The engine cannot always tell them apart at the moment they arrive, and the cost of error differs by direction. Treating a transient shock as persistent moves the price for nothing and teaches buyers to watch the list. Treating a persistent shift as transient leaves the price wrong for as long as the misreading lasts. Feedback, later in this chapter, takes up how the engine separates the two. Movement establishes why the separation matters.

A price path is the sequence of prices an item carries over time, and the path carries information that a single level does not. Principles introduced price paths as commitments. In the Engine the same idea becomes operational: a series of small increases, a single large step, and a hold followed by a correction can reach the same terminal price while producing different contribution along the way and different expectations at the end. Policy states which paths are acceptable. The engine chooses among them.

#### Staleness Cost

Staleness cost is the contribution lost while a price lags behind the conditions it was meant to reflect. It accumulates quietly, because a stale price produces no error message, only a margin that is lower or a volume that is thinner than it would otherwise be.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows its size. An item sells 1,000 units a month at a price of 100, and its unit cost rises from 60 to 66 at the start of a quarter. Suppose Policy calls for passing the full increase through, so the target price is 106, and suppose volume does not respond to a change of that size. Under a quarterly cadence, the old price stands for three months and the firm forgoes 6 per unit on 3,000 units, or 18,000. Under a monthly cadence the lag is one month, and the loss is 6 per unit on 1,000 units, or 6,000. The faster cadence saves 12,000 in the quarter. If each re-pricing review costs 4,000 in analyst and approver time, the two extra monthly reviews cost 8,000, and the faster cadence still comes out 4,000 ahead. Had the cost rise been 2 rather than 6, the saving would have been 4,000 against the same 8,000 in review cost, and the quarterly cadence would have been the better choice.

The illustration makes two points. Staleness cost scales with the size of the drift and the length of the lag. Re-pricing cost does not scale with drift at all, since a review costs about the same whether it moves a price by 2 or by 6. A sound cadence therefore depends on how fast and how far each item's inputs move, and a single cadence for the whole catalog will be too slow for some items and too fast for others.

#### Cadence Classes

Bootstrapping graduated instruments into approval classes. Movement adds cadence classes alongside them. A cadence class groups items whose inputs drift at similar rates and assigns each group a review interval, a trigger, or both. Commodity-linked items whose cost tracks a published index may warrant a monthly review or a rule that fires when the index moves past a stated threshold. Items with stable cost and slow-moving demand may warrant an annual review. Negotiated contracts follow their renewal calendars.

The cost of re-pricing includes more than review time. Behavior described menu costs and the attention limits that make frequent change expensive for an organization, and those limits apply to buyers as well. Each change asks buyers to notice, compare, and sometimes renegotiate, and a list that moves too often invites the waiting that Uncertainty described. Governance adds its own load, since every change enters the change record and may draw an audit question. The Carnegie view of the firm as a system with bounded attention implies that cadence is rationed, and the engine spends that ration where staleness costs most.

Triggers complement calendars. A calendar review catches slow drift that no single observation would flag. A trigger catches a sharp move between reviews. The engine records, for every price change, whether a calendar or a trigger caused it and which input crossed which threshold. That record lets Learning revise the classes later, and lets Governance trace any price on the path back to the condition that produced it. Movement thus turns time from a background condition into a stated design variable: how often the engine looks, what it looks at, and how much drift it tolerates before it acts.

### 14.2 Path Dependence

#### Lock-In

History constrains what a price change does. Two firms with the same costs, the same rivals, and the same list price can face different consequences from the same cut, because one sold most of its base on multiyear contracts and the other on monthly plans. Path dependence names that condition: the effect of a move depends on the sequence of earlier moves, and some earlier moves cannot be undone at reasonable cost.

The economics literature gave the term its sharpest form. Paul David's 1985 account of the QWERTY keyboard argued that an early standard can persist after its original reasons have lapsed, because the users trained on it and the equipment built for it make change costly. Brian Arthur's 1989 paper on competing technologies showed how increasing returns to adoption can let small, early, partly random events decide which technology a market locks into. Both authors applied the idea to technologies and markets as a whole. This book uses it in a narrower sense, as the dependence of a single firm's feasible prices on its own installed commitments; the logic of costly-to-reverse history is the same, though the mechanism runs through contracts and expectations rather than increasing returns to adoption, and the unit of analysis is the firm's price history rather than an industry standard.

For a pricing engine the installed base is the main carrier of history. Each account bought at a stated price, under stated terms, at a stated time, and each carries expectations about what will happen at renewal. Principles treated lock-in as a commitment problem and Competition treated switching costs as a margin that an entrant can contest. Path Dependence adds the view from inside the running loop: every proposal the engine makes lands on a base that remembers earlier proposals.

#### Ratchets

A ratchet is a move that is easy to make in one direction and hard to reverse. Price cuts are the common case. A discount offered to win a quarter's volume becomes the reference point Behavior described, and buyers who received it treat its removal as a loss rather than a return to normal. A cut made in public is harder still to reverse, because rivals have seen it and may have matched it, which repeats Competition's account of how matching turns a single move into a lower level for the whole market.

Learned waiting is a second ratchet. When a firm discounts at predictable times, buyers learn to defer purchases until the discount arrives. Uncertainty described the option value of waiting from the buyer's side. Path Dependence describes its consequence for the seller: once buyers have learned the pattern, a firm that stops discounting does not recover full-price sales at once, because the learned expectation decays only as buyers observe that the discount no longer comes.

The engine handles ratchets by pricing in their reversal cost. A proposal to cut is evaluated against the contribution it gains while in force and the contribution it will cost later, when the cut is withdrawn or becomes permanent. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the comparison. A temporary cut of 10 on a price of 100 adds 500 units in a quarter at a margin of 30 after the cut, a gain of 15,000, but gives up 10 on 2,000 units that would have sold anyway, a cost of 20,000. The cut is a net loss of 5,000 on its own terms. If it also teaches a quarter of those 2,000 buyers, 500 in all, to wait for the next cut, and the firm offers the cut again to sell to them, it gives up a further 10 on each of the 500, another 5,000, in every later quarter the pattern holds. A move that looked marginal becomes a recurring cost.

#### Legacy Terms

Legacy terms are the commitments a firm made under earlier policy and still honors: grandfathered tiers, early-adopter rates, price locks, most-favored-customer clauses, published promises about future increases. They are path dependence in its most explicit form, written into contracts and announcements.

An engine that optimizes over current inputs alone will propose to remove them, since each appears as a price below what the current model recommends. Integrity, in the sense Value and Governance established, treats that proposal as a breach rather than an improvement: a promise that is withdrawn whenever the engine finds it costly was never a promise, and buyers who see it withdrawn discount every later commitment the firm makes. The cost appears in renewals and in the credibility of future offers, not in the quarter the term is removed.

The engine therefore carries legacy terms as constraints, each with an owner, a scope, and an expiry if one exists. Changing one is a Policy decision routed through Authority and Audit, not a proposal the engine generates by default. The register of legacy terms also serves Intelligence later, since conjectures about how buyers will respond to a move depend on which buyers hold which commitments.

### 14.3 Stability

#### Cobweb Cycles

An engine that responds to its own recent results can produce cycles that no single decision intended. The pattern has a long history in economics. Mordecai Ezekiel's 1938 statement of the cobweb theorem described agricultural markets in which producers set this season's output by last season's price: a high price prompts a large crop, the large crop drives the price down, the low price prompts a small crop, and the market circles its equilibrium. Whether the circles shrink or grow depends on the relative slopes of supply and demand. Ezekiel's mechanism ran through quantities planted by many producers. This book applies the same lag structure to a single engine's price responses, where the lag runs from observed conversion to the next proposed price.

The engine version is easy to produce. Suppose the engine raises price when conversion runs above target and cuts it when conversion runs below. If the response is large relative to the gap and the observation arrives late, the engine overshoots: a raise pushes conversion below target, the next cycle cuts too far, conversion overshoots in the other direction, and the price swings around the level it was seeking. Each step follows the rule. The oscillation is a property of the rule and its lag together.

Oscillation costs more than the average price suggests. Buyers who observe a swinging list learn to time purchases, which is the waiting ratchet of Path Dependence. Rivals who match moves amplify them, which is Competition's account of price wars restated for an automated loop. Governance cannot trace a rationale for each swing, because no single proposal was wrong given the one before it.

#### Damping

Damping reduces each response to a fraction of the observed gap, so that corrections shrink rather than overshoot. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the threshold. Measure the price's deviation from the level that would meet target conversion, and suppose the engine's response, after the lag, changes that deviation by a multiple c of its size. The next deviation is then the current deviation times one minus c. With c at 0.5, a deviation of 10 becomes 5, then 2.5, and the price converges without crossing the target. With c at 1.5, a deviation of 10 becomes minus 5, then 2.5, then minus 1.25: the price crosses the target each cycle but still converges. With c at 2.5, the factor is minus 1.5, and the deviation grows from 10 to minus 15 to 22.5. The loop diverges. Responses that correct more than twice the gap in one step destabilize a loop of this kind, and lags make overcorrection easier to commit, because the engine is correcting a gap that has already changed.

The illustration is linear and deterministic, and real demand is neither. The qualitative lesson carries: a response strength that feels prudent with fresh data can destabilize a loop when observations arrive late. The engine does not need the exact threshold to use the lesson. It needs to know the lag between a price change and the observation it responds to, and to set its response strength with that lag in view, starting low and raising strength only when the record shows the loop converging. A loop whose deviations alternate in sign from one cycle to the next is already near the threshold, and the alternation itself is a signal the engine can monitor and report before the swings grow. Supervision from Bootstrapping supplies the reviewer who reads that report.

#### Move Bounds

Bounds and dead bands are the two other instruments of stability. A move bound caps the size of any single change, or of the cumulative change over a window, regardless of what the model recommends. A dead band suppresses response to gaps smaller than a stated threshold, on the reasoning that small gaps are more likely noise than signal and that each change carries the re-pricing cost Movement described. Optimization treated bounds as constraints that carry shadow prices. Stability gives them a second function: a bound that binds often signals that the engine wants to move faster than the firm has agreed to move, and the shadow price of the bound measures what that restraint costs.

Stability and responsiveness trade against each other. A heavily damped engine with tight bounds rarely oscillates but tracks persistent shifts slowly and so pays staleness cost. A lightly damped engine tracks quickly but risks cycles and amplifies noise. No setting is right in general. Policy states the tradeoff for each cadence class: how large a move may be, how long a gap must persist before the engine acts, and how much tracking error the firm accepts in return for a quiet list.

These settings belong in the change record like any other policy parameter. When Learning revises them, the record shows which oscillation or which staleness loss prompted the revision. When the engine later scales across markets, the same settings travel with it, which is one reason Scaling will treat them as part of the engine's specification rather than as tuning details.

### 14.4 Feedback

#### Signal Choice

Feedback is the path by which market outcomes return to the engine and change what it proposes next. Movement established that inputs drift, Path Dependence that history binds, and Stability that a responsive loop can oscillate. Feedback concerns what the loop listens to, how quickly, and with what weight.

The first choice is which signals enter. Conversion rates, win and loss records, support volume, churn, rival list changes, and capacity utilization are all candidates, and each measures something different. Segmentation required that the observables used to sort buyers be named and justified. The same discipline applies here: each feedback signal is named, its relation to the firm's objective is stated, and its known distortions are recorded. An engine fed conversion alone will learn to maximize conversion, which is rarely the objective; a cut that lifts conversion while lowering contribution looks like success to a loop that cannot see contribution.

Signals also differ in how much they can be gamed or distorted. A sales team rewarded on volume will report losses in ways that favor discounting, which is Kerr's folly from Governance reappearing as a data problem. Feedback design therefore prefers signals that are recorded mechanically over signals that pass through someone with a stake in the result, and it records the provenance of each.

#### Lags

Every feedback signal arrives late, and the lags differ. Conversion on a web list may be visible within days. Renewal outcomes on annual contracts arrive a year after the price that produced them. Reputation effects and learned waiting may take several cycles to show. An engine that weights all signals as if they were current will respond quickly to fast signals and ignore slow ones, which biases it toward moves whose costs arrive late, such as discounts that teach waiting.

Smoothing addresses noise within a single signal. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows its effect. Suppose the engine estimates weekly conversion by exponential smoothing, keeping a share of the old estimate and adding a weight w times the new observation. With w at 1.0, a single week in which conversion jumps by 30 points moves the estimate by the full 30. With w at 0.2, the same week moves it by 6, and a jump that persists for further weeks pulls the estimate up gradually as each week adds its share. The heavier smoothing protects the price from one week's noise at the cost of recognizing a persistent shift later, which is the stability tradeoff restated for estimation.

Rudolf Kalman's 1960 paper on linear filtering gave a formal answer to the weighting problem for systems that meet its assumptions: the weight on a new observation should rise with the uncertainty of the current estimate and fall with the noise in the observation. Kalman's setting was engineering control with known noise structure. This book borrows only the principle, that the weight given to new evidence depends on how noisy it is relative to what is already known, which also matches the revision rule Uncertainty set out. In practice the engine rarely knows the noise structure well enough to compute the optimal weight. It can still order its signals by reliability, give less weight to those with known distortions, and record the weights so that Learning can revise them as evidence accumulates. A weight that is stated and recorded can be audited and argued over. A weight buried in model code cannot, and it tends to drift with each retraining without anyone deciding that it should.

#### Policy Gates

Industrial dynamics, as Jay Forrester developed it from the late 1950s and John Sterman later extended it, showed that delays and feedback in ordinary business processes can generate oscillation and amplification that no participant intends. Forrester's best-known demonstration concerned production and distribution chains in which small changes in retail orders grew into large swings upstream. A pricing engine coupled to rivals, buyers, and its own sales organization is a system of the same kind, and its behavior depends on the structure of its loops as much as on any single rule. The practical consequence is that each rule is reviewed together with the loop it sits in: its lag, its partners, and the signals it reads.

Policy-in-the-loop is the structural answer. Feedback may change what the engine proposes, within the bounds and cadence classes Policy has set, but it may not change the bounds, the classes, or the objective. Those change only through Learning's revision cadence and Governance's change records. The distinction mirrors the double loop Learning described: the inner loop adjusts prices within rules, and the outer loop, run by people at a slower cadence, adjusts the rules themselves.

Bootstrapping's kill switches remain the hard stop when a loop amplifies despite these gates. Dynamics adds the diagnostic record that shows why it amplified: which signal, at what lag, through which response rule. Intelligence takes up the next step, conjectures and simulation that test a loop's behavior before it runs on live prices. Portfolio extends the same feedback questions to many items whose signals interact.

## Chapter 15 — Intelligence

### Introduction
#### Discussable Models

Chapter 15 of Pricing Tech is Intelligence. Dynamics gave the engine motion, path dependence, stability, and feedback. Motion without models of buyers and rivals is still a reactive loop. Intelligence adds conjectures that can be falsified, simulation before hard commitment, scenarios the organization can discuss, and auditability that keeps those tools under Governance—not a black box that emits prices nobody can explain. Portfolio will later couple many SKUs; Enterprise will later staff the function. This chapter owns the cognitive layer of the Engine.

The arc runs Conjectures, Simulation, Scenarios, Auditability. Conjectures state light buyer and rival models with explicit falsifiers and update rules inherited from Learning. Simulation evaluates strategy alternatives and counterfactuals under a compute budget and stated limits. Scenarios stress regimes and shifts so decisions use intelligence rather than decorate decks. Auditability requires that a price be traceable to inputs, conjectures, and policy. Interaction’s honesty about unidentified extensive forms still binds: unnamed games stay unnamed; Competition’s stance supplies what Policy already claimed. Freemium paths, early-bird windows, and version fences reappear here as objects of conjecture—how buyers select, how rivals match—not as folklore the engine is allowed to treat as physics.

#### Conjectures, Simulation

Conjectures are testable claims about how buyers choose among instruments and how rivals respond on observable moves—not full demand systems the firm cannot identify under Uncertainty. A usable conjecture names the instrument, the observable, and the predicted direction of response under a stated horizon. Falsifiers matter: a conjecture without a kill criterion becomes mythology. Update rules link to Learning’s loops and Bootstrapping’s graduation: when evidence revises a rival band or a willingness hypothesis, Authority and Audit record the change. Soft behavioralism tours are out; Carnegie bounds still limit how elaborate a model an early team can maintain.

Simulation is rehearsal before commitment. Alternatives—match within a Softening band, hold list, open a Contest, shift a version fence—can be scored against Optimization’s objectives and constraints before Dynamics executes a path. Counterfactuals inherit Path Dependence: installed base and legacy promises change simulated payoffs. Compute budget and limits of simulation are first-class. Infinite scenario theater that nobody can audit fails the same integrity test as fake econometric precision: it consumes attention without improving accountable decisions.

#### Scenarios, Auditability

Scenarios are stress cases and regime shifts the firm can discuss: demand collapse, rival list war, capacity shock, regulatory envelope change. Discussability is the design goal—sales, finance, and product must be able to argue about a scenario without needing the modeler’s private notebook. A scenario that only the pricing engineer can interpret fails Behavior’s attention test as surely as an overbuilt menu. Decision use means scenarios gate commitments and graduation criteria; they are not wallpaper behind an already chosen price.

Auditability closes the anti-black-box requirement Part III stated at the outset. Explain a price: which inputs, which conjecture, which constraint bound, which override. Trace to Policy. Governance’s reconstructability extends from human exceptions to engine proposals. If intelligence cannot be audited, Bootstrapping’s supervised loop has been abandoned for fashion. Intelligence therefore remains an aid under Control, the same grammar Optimization required before any Engine existed.

The sequence Conjectures, Simulation, Scenarios, Auditability is analytic. Simulation without falsifiable conjectures is numerology. Scenarios without auditability are theater. Closing with Auditability returns the reader to the Engine’s inheritance claim: speed without explainability is not progress.

#### Model Edges

Intelligence does not solve multi-SKU portfolio coupling or enterprise staffing of a pricing function. It does not redo Dynamics’ feedback design except where models feed the loop. What this chapter owns is auditable engine cognition: conjectures, simulation, scenarios, and explainable prices.

Intelligence is accordingly judged by legibility as much as by accuracy. A conjecture that improves forecasts but cannot be stated, challenged, and revised by the people accountable for prices adds risk rather than capability, because the organization can no longer attribute outcomes to identifiable assumptions.

### 15.1 Conjectures

#### Stated Responses

A conjecture, in the sense this chapter uses, is a written claim about how buyers or rivals will respond to an observable move by the firm. Bootstrapping encoded objectives, constraints, and segment rules as inputs, and Dynamics gave the engine a cadence and bounds on movement. Neither supplied a view of what happens on the other side of a price change. Every engine carries such a view, whether stated or not. A rule that cuts list price when conversion falls assumes that buyers respond to list price and that rivals will not erase the cut. Making these assumptions explicit is the first task of Intelligence.

The term has an older and narrower meaning. Bowley (1924) and Frisch (1933) introduced conjectural variations: the change in a rival's output that a firm expects in response to a change in its own output, entered as a parameter in the firm's optimizing condition. Modern industrial organization treats that parameter mainly as a summary of conduct rather than as a model of how beliefs form, since a static setting gives no account of why a rival would respond at all. This book uses conjecture more broadly, for any testable belief about buyer or rival response, and keeps the dynamic account Interaction drew from repeated play.

Buyer conjectures stay light. Uncertainty showed that a founder-scale firm rarely identifies a demand curve, and Segmentation built rules on observables rather than estimated elasticities. A usable buyer conjecture therefore names an instrument, a segment, an observable, a direction, and a horizon: annual-plan uptake in the small-firm segment rises when the monthly price exceeds one twelfth of the annual price by more than a stated margin, visible within one quarter. Such a claim can be wrong in a way the firm will notice. A conjecture that buyers are value-sensitive cannot be wrong in any observable way and does no work in the engine.

#### Rival Conjectures

Rival conjectures follow the same form. Competition wrote the firm's stance toward each rival in the rival map: softening where fighting destroys contribution, contest where a budgeted push can win. The engine needs the beliefs behind that stance in executable form: which rivals match list cuts, within what delay, on which segments, and whether they respond to discounts they cannot observe. Interaction left the extensive form of most pricing games unidentified, and that honesty still binds. A rival conjecture states an observed regularity and the period over which it held. It does not claim to know the rival's payoff function.

Competition also deferred a harder case to the Engine part. When rivals set prices by software that reads the firm's prices, the responses the firm observes are partly produced by its own engine. Calvano, Calzolari, Denicolò, and Pastorello (2020) showed in simulated repeated price competition that independent Q-learning algorithms, with no communication, learned to sustain prices above the competitive level and to punish deviations with temporary price cuts. For the engine designer, the implication is practical. A conjecture that rivals match quickly may describe an interaction between two algorithms rather than a fact about the rival, and a loop that learns to avoid cuts because cuts are always matched may drift toward an outcome that regulators treat as coordination. The legal side belongs to Enterprise. The design side belongs here: rival conjectures record whether the observed response came from people or from software, and any rule that learns its stance from rival responses is reviewed under Bootstrapping's approval classes before it gains autonomy.

#### Falsifiers

Each conjecture carries a falsifier, the observation that would lead the firm to drop or revise it. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the form. Suppose the conjecture is that rival A matches any list cut on the mid-market plan within two weeks, and the falsifier is that A fails to match in two or more of the next four cuts. If A matches in one of four cuts, it failed to match in three, which exceeds the threshold, and the conjecture is retired. The rival map is then revised, and the contest budget that assumed matching is reopened. Writing the falsifier before the evidence arrives prevents the team from reading every outcome as confirmation.

Update rules connect falsifiers to Learning. Learning distinguished single-loop updating of parameters from double-loop revision of the rules that produce them. A conjecture's parameters, such as the matching delay, may update automatically within stated limits as evidence accumulates. Retiring or replacing a conjecture is a double-loop change and goes through Governance's authority and audit, with the date, the evidence, and the owner recorded. Bootstrapping's graduation criteria apply in the same way: a conjecture earns weight in the engine by surviving its falsifier over a stated number of cycles.

Carnegie bounds limit how many conjectures an early team can maintain. A short register of a dozen conjectures, each with an owner, a falsifier, and a review date, supports more pricing decisions than a hundred that nobody checks. The register is also the input for the next step. Simulation needs stated conjectures to run, because a simulated outcome is only as informative as the response assumptions that generate it.

### 15.2 Simulation

#### Rehearsal

Simulation runs proposed price moves against stated conjectures before the firm commits to them. Optimization defined the objective and constraints, Competition defined the stance, and Dynamics defined the paths along which prices may move. Simulation combines these with the conjecture register to score alternatives on paper: hold list, match a rival within the softening band, open a budgeted contest, or move a version fence. The output is a comparison of contribution, volume, and constraint pressure under each alternative, together with the conjectures each result depends on.

The value of rehearsal is asymmetric. Dynamics showed that some moves are costly to reverse: cuts teach buyers to wait, and legacy terms persist for years. Simulation is worth most where reversal is expensive and the response is uncertain. A small move on a web list that can be undone next week rarely justifies a simulation run. A change to renewal terms on enterprise contracts, which binds for the contract life, usually does.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers, the running case that Scenarios also uses, shows the basic comparison. Suppose list price is 100, contribution per unit is 40, and monthly volume is 1,000 units, for contribution of 40,000. The proposal is a cut of 5, which leaves contribution of 35 per unit. If rival B does not match, volume rises to 1,200 and contribution is 1,200 times 35, or 42,000, a gain of 2,000. If B matches, volume rises only to 1,050 and contribution is 36,750, a loss of 3,250. The cut pays only if the probability that B does not match exceeds 3,250 divided by 5,250, about 0.62. The simulation does not decide the question. It converts it into a question about one rival conjecture, which the register can answer with evidence or flag as unknown. If the register shows B matched in most recent cuts, the proposal fails before any price moves, and the cost of learning that was one run rather than one quarter of lost contribution.

#### Counterfactual Runs

Counterfactuals ask what would have happened under a policy the firm did not follow. They serve two uses. Before a move, they compare alternatives. After a move, they separate the effect of the price from the effect of everything else that changed in the period, which Learning required before a result is entered into memory.

Counterfactual runs inherit path dependence. The installed base, the legacy terms register, and the waiting buyers Dynamics described all change the payoff of a move, so a simulation that starts from a blank market overstates the gain from cuts and understates the cost of increases. The simulation state therefore includes the commitments the firm already carries, drawn from the same records the engine uses. A counterfactual that ignores which buyers hold price protection answers a question about a different firm.

Simulation can also test the engine itself. Dynamics introduced damping, dead bands, and move bounds to keep the loop stable. Running the loop against simulated demand, with injected noise and delayed signals, shows whether those settings prevent oscillation before the loop touches live prices. The same harness can run the firm's response rules against a simulated rival that learns, which is the cheapest way to see whether the pattern Calvano and coauthors found, prices settling above the competitive level through learned punishment, can emerge from the firm's own rules. A rule that produces that pattern in simulation is revised before it runs live, and the run is kept in the record as evidence that the test was made.

#### Compute Budget

Simulation consumes compute and, more scarcely, attention. A model with many parameters can produce thousands of runs, each defensible and none decisive, and the review meeting ends without a decision. The compute budget is therefore set as Policy: how many alternatives are scored for each class of decision, how many conjectures are varied, and how long a run may take before a decision proceeds without it. Bootstrapping's approval classes provide a natural scale. Routine moves receive no simulation or a standard one; moves in the highest class receive a full run with the key conjectures varied.

The limits of simulation follow from its inputs. A simulation recombines stated conjectures and constraints and cannot discover a response nobody wrote down. It cannot identify demand that Uncertainty showed the firm lacks the data to identify, and it gives false comfort when its outputs are reported with more precision than its inputs carry. Results are stated as ranges, with the conjectures that drive the range named. Where the result flips sign within a plausible range of one conjecture, as in the illustration above, the report says so and the decision turns on that conjecture rather than on the point estimate.

Simulation also stops at the edge of the regime its conjectures describe. Response patterns estimated in normal trading may fail when demand collapses or a rival changes ownership. Testing decisions against such breaks is the work of scenarios, which take up the cases the conjecture register does not cover.

### 15.3 Scenarios

#### Stress Cases

Scenarios describe coherent alternative states of the market in which the firm's current pricing policy might fail. Simulation scores moves under the conjectures the firm currently holds. Scenarios ask what happens if those conjectures stop holding together: demand falls sharply, a well-funded rival cuts list price across the board, a key input cost doubles, capacity is lost, or a regulatory envelope tightens. Each scenario is written as a short narrative with stated values for the inputs it changes, and the engine's policy is run against it.

The practice comes from strategic planning. Pierre Wack described in two Harvard Business Review articles (1985) how scenario work at Royal Dutch Shell was used to change the mental models of managers rather than to forecast, and Peter Schwartz's The Art of the Long View (1991) set out the method for wider use. In that tradition scenarios cover long horizons and the whole enterprise. This book applies the method to a narrower object, the pricing policy and the engine that executes it, over horizons of quarters rather than decades.

A labeled illustration continues the running case from Simulation as a stress case: list price is 100, contribution per unit is 40 on monthly volume of 1,000 units, for contribution of 40,000, and fixed costs the pricing function must cover are 30,000. A demand-collapse scenario cuts volume by 30 percent to 700 units, which yields contribution of 28,000 and leaves a shortfall of 2,000 against fixed costs. The scenario then asks which policy responses the engine would propose. If the engine's response rule cuts list price by 10 percent to recover volume, list price falls from 100 to 90 and contribution per unit from 40 to 30, and volume must reach 1,000 units, back to the pre-collapse level, to cover the 30,000. The scenario shows that the response rule depends on a full volume recovery the collapse itself makes unlikely, and that holding price while cutting serving cost is the stronger candidate.

#### Regime Shifts

Some scenarios describe gradual drift, which Dynamics already handles through cadence and feedback. Others describe regime shifts: changes after which the conjectures, rather than the parameters, are wrong. A rival acquired by a larger firm may stop matching cuts and start leading them. A move from seat-based to usage-based pricing across a category changes which instruments buyers compare. A new entrant whose engine responds to the firm's prices within hours changes the meaning of every observed match.

Regime scenarios are built from the conjecture register. Each scenario takes a set of conjectures that would fail together and states the replacement beliefs. The engine's policy is then run under the replacement set, and the outcome shows which rules depend on the old regime. Rules that perform acceptably across regimes are robust. Rules that perform well in one regime and badly in another are flagged, and their owners state which observable signals would indicate that the regime has changed. Those signals join the drift signals Dynamics already monitors, so that a regime shift appears as a named event rather than as a slow deterioration attributed to noise. The owner of each flagged rule also states in advance what the engine does when the signal fires: narrow its move bounds, return the rule to a lower approval class, or hand the decision to a person until the conjectures are rewritten.

#### Decision Use

Scenarios earn their cost only when they change decisions. Behavior described the organizational habit of commissioning analysis after a choice has been made and using it to justify the choice. A scenario run after the price is set, and presented as support, is analysis of that kind. Decision use means scenarios are attached to specific gates: graduation of a rule to wider autonomy under Bootstrapping, changes to move bounds under Dynamics, and contest budgets under Competition. A rule that fails a stress case does not graduate until the failure is addressed or accepted in writing by the owner with authority under Governance.

Discussability is the design requirement that makes decision use possible. Sales, finance, and product must be able to read a scenario, dispute its assumptions, and propose alternatives without access to the modeler's code. A scenario therefore fits on one page: the narrative, the changed inputs with their values, the conjectures replaced, the policy outcome, and the decision it bears on. A scenario that only its author can interpret moves pricing judgment back into a single head, which is the condition Governance was designed to end.

The set of scenarios stays small. Wack's account stressed that a few well-chosen scenarios change thinking where many dilute it. For a founder-scale firm, three to five scenarios reviewed each planning cycle, each tied to a decision gate, carry more weight than a library nobody consults. Scenarios for the coupled catalog, where a shock to one product shifts demand across others, belong to Portfolio. The requirement that every scenario result can be traced to its inputs belongs to the last section of this chapter.

### 15.4 Auditability

#### Explained Prices

Auditability is the requirement that any price the engine proposes can be explained after the fact: which inputs it used, which conjectures it relied on, which constraints bound, and which human approved or overrode it. Part III opened with the claim that an engine inherits Policy rather than replacing it. Auditability is the test of that claim. A price that cannot be traced to Policy was set by the engine on its own authority, whatever the organization chart says.

An explained price has a standard form. For each proposal the engine records the input values at the time, the rule or model that produced the number, the conjectures that rule depends on with their register entries, the constraints that were binding with their shadow prices where Optimization's method supplies them, the approval class, and the approver. Governance required that human exceptions be reconstructable from the record. The same standard now applies to the engine's proposals. A reviewer who picks any price from last quarter can recover why it was set, from the record alone, in minutes rather than days.

Explanation also serves the firm's buyers and partners. An account manager who can state that a renewal rose because a usage tier was crossed and a discount expired can defend the price in a negotiation. An account manager who can only say the system set it has nothing to defend, and the deal desk's exception culture returns as soon as buyers push back. Disclosure obligations toward regulators and customers extend this requirement outward and belong to Enterprise.

#### Input Trails

Tracing a price to its inputs requires that inputs be versioned. Bootstrapping encoded objectives, constraints, and segment rules as machine-readable inputs with owners. Each change to an input is recorded with its date, author, and reason, so the trail for a price points to the specific version in force when the price was set. Without versioning, an audit compares a price to today's rules and finds inconsistencies that were correct at the time, or misses errors that were later fixed.

Conjectures and scenarios enter the same trail. A price that depends on the conjecture that rival A matches within two weeks records the register entry and its status. If the conjecture is later retired by its falsifier, an audit can list every price that relied on it and decide whether any should be revisited. A scenario that gated a rule's graduation is linked to that rule, so a later failure can be traced to the scenario that should have caught it. The trail turns model risk into something the organization can inspect: which assumptions carry which prices, and how much revenue rests on each.

Software rivals add a specific entry. Where a rule responds to rival prices, the trail records which rival prices were read, when, and what response followed. If questions about coordination arise, this record shows whether the engine's responses followed the stated stance and conjectures or emerged from learned behavior nobody approved. Building the record costs little when the engine is designed. Reconstructing it after a regulatory inquiry begins is expensive and often impossible.

#### Black Boxes

The strongest form of auditability uses models whose logic can be read directly. Rudin (2019) argued that for high-stakes decisions, explaining an opaque model after the fact is a weaker remedy than using an interpretable model from the start, since post hoc explanations can be unfaithful to what the model actually computes, and that interpretable models often perform comparably on structured data. Rudin's distinction between interpretable and explainable models is narrower than this book's use of auditable, which covers the whole trail from Policy to price, not only the model's internal logic. The argument still applies. A pricing rule built from stated conjectures, encoded constraints, and a small number of readable parameters can be audited by reading it. A large learned model that maps features to prices can be audited only through approximations whose fidelity is itself uncertain.

The practical rule follows. Where an interpretable model performs within a stated tolerance of an opaque one on the firm's own data, the interpretable model is used. Where an opaque component is adopted, it is confined to a role whose output enters an interpretable rule as one input, such as a demand forecast, and the rule, not the component, sets the price within Policy's bounds. Bootstrapping's kill switches and approval classes apply to the component as to any other input.

Intelligence therefore remains an aid under Control, as Optimization required before any engine existed. Conjectures make beliefs testable, simulation rehearses moves against them, scenarios stress them across regimes, and auditability keeps every step traceable to an owner. Portfolio extends the same requirement to the coupled price vector, where one explanation must cover many items at once. Scaling asks whether the trail survives growth in volume and markets, and Enterprise assigns the people who answer for it.

## Chapter 16 — Portfolio

### Introduction
#### One Problem

Chapter 16 of Pricing Tech is Portfolio. Intelligence made conjectures and simulations discussable and auditable inside a running loop. That loop still fails if each SKU is priced as if the others did not exist. Portfolio treats the catalog as one coupled problem: multi-SKU structure with shared constraints and demand links, cannibalization and cross-effects, firm-level binds across items, and a real-time price vector as the engine’s output—plus pointers toward how the portfolio should evolve. Scaling will later treat throughput and failure modes; Enterprise will later staff the function. This chapter owns the vector problem itself.

The arc runs Multi-SKU, Cannibalization, Constraints, Price Vector. Multi-SKU states the portfolio as the unit of analysis and names the complexity tax of shared links. Cannibalization treats trade-up, trade-down, and internal rivalry with light cross-effect language and honest proxies under thin data. Constraints apply Optimization’s margin, promise, and regulatory envelopes across SKUs so a locally clever list does not violate the firm’s feasible set. Price Vector defines the real-time output, partial updates, evolution pointers, and a human-readable summary Governance can audit. Differentiation’s lines and bundles are the offer objects being jointly priced; Segmentation’s who-pays-what rules still attach to those objects.

#### Shared Demand

Multi-SKU pricing rejects the fiction that three hundred offers are three hundred independent Optimization problems. Shared capacity, shared brand promises, shared support pools, and overlapping buyer sets couple decisions even when SKUs look separate in a spreadsheet. The complexity tax is operational as well as mathematical: Behavior’s attention bounds and Bootstrapping’s minimum-engine lessons warn against encoding every combinatorial link on day one. The portfolio as unit means the engine’s success criteria include joint contribution and constraint satisfaction, not only per-SKU conversion.

Cannibalization is internal rivalry made explicit. Trade-up and trade-down along Differentiation’s lines, freemium diverting paid seats, and regional SKUs stealing from each other are cross-effects the firm often observes poorly. Light cross-elasticity language is enough: direction and rough magnitude under proxies beat fake precision. Uncertainty’s honesty applies—sparse scraps do not identify a full cross-demand system. Ignoring cannibalization is still a stance: it silently optimizes one SKU against the firm’s own contribution. Intelligence’s scenarios should stress internal rivalry the same way they stress external rivals.

#### Joint Feasibility

Constraints at portfolio scale are firm policy binds: margin floors that apply to mixes, integrity of promises that cannot be kept if every SKU discounts independently, regulatory envelopes that span products, and capacity that is shared. Feasibility is joint. A price vector that is elementwise “optimal” under independent problems can be infeasible once shared binds are stated. Governance Authority must own which constraints are hard across the catalog; Audit must reconstruct why a SKU moved when the bind was portfolio-level.

Price Vector is the Engine output Part III promised: a real-time assignment of prices (and related instruments) across the active portfolio, with partial updates when only a subset must move, and pointers toward portfolio evolution—which lines to open, retire, or re-fence—without pretending those pointers are themselves a full product strategy. Human-readable summary is mandatory: Intelligence’s auditability requirement scales to the vector. Partial updates inherit Dynamics’ move bounds and Bootstrapping’s approval classes so the firm does not re-price everything because one cell changed.

The sequence Multi-SKU, Cannibalization, Constraints, Price Vector is analytic. Cannibalization without a portfolio unit is a complaint. A vector without joint constraints is a list of independent prices written in vector notation. Closing with Price Vector returns the reader to the Engine’s definition: environment to prices under inherited Policy, now explicitly many-to-many.

#### Vector Scope

Portfolio does not cover scale failure modes, multi-market rollout throughput, or enterprise organizational design—those are Scaling and Enterprise. It does not redo Intelligence’s conjecture design except where cross-effects enter models. What this chapter owns is coupled catalog pricing: multi-SKU structure, cannibalization, firm constraints, and the real-time price vector.

### 16.1 Multi-SKU

#### Catalog Unit

A firm with one product can price it as Optimization described: state the aim, write the constraints, choose the instrument, and leave Control with the right to override. A firm with many products can repeat that procedure item by item only if the items do not affect one another. In technology catalogs they almost always do. Tiers of the same product share buyers, add-ons share the accounts of the base plan they attach to, regional editions share a brand and a reference price that travels across borders, and every product draws on the same support pool, the same infrastructure, and the same sales capacity. The unit of analysis for an engine that prices such a catalog is the portfolio, and the object it produces is a vector of prices rather than a list of separate answers.

The distinction matters because the per-item procedure gives the wrong answer in a predictable direction. An owner who prices one tier to maximize that tier's contribution counts every buyer gained as a gain, including buyers who would otherwise have bought a sibling tier at a higher margin. The owner of the sibling tier does the same in reverse. Each decision is correct within its own accounts, and the sum of the decisions is worse for the firm than a coordinated choice. The engine described in Part III was defined as a mapping from environment to a price vector for this reason: only a mapping that sees the whole catalog can count the effect of one price on the others.

Multiproduct economics has treated the same unit from the cost side. William Baumol, John Panzar, and Robert Willig, in their 1982 work on contestable markets and industry structure, analyzed firms whose costs depend on the whole bundle of outputs they produce, and they formalized economies of scope, the saving from producing several outputs jointly rather than separately. When costs are shared in this way, the cost attributed to any one product depends on an allocation rule, and different reasonable rules give different per-item margins. Their purpose was to explain which industry structures are sustainable. This book uses only the cost concept, as a reason the engine cannot treat a per-item margin as a fact independent of the rest of the catalog.

#### Coupling Links

Two kinds of link couple prices across a catalog. Demand links run through buyers: a change in the price of one item changes how many buyers choose another, because the items substitute for one another, complement one another, or share a reference point that buyers compare. Differentiation built these links deliberately when it designed lines, versions, and bundles so that buyers would sort themselves among offers, and Segmentation attached who-pays-what rules to the resulting objects. Portfolio takes those offer objects as given and asks how to price them together.

Constraint links run through the firm. Several items may draw on one capacity, one support budget, one margin commitment made to investors, or one promise made to customers about how tiers relate to each other. A constraint link couples prices even when no buyer ever compares the items, because moving one price changes how much of the shared limit remains for the others.
