Constraints, the third section of this chapter, treats these links as the feasibility condition for the whole vector.

The two kinds of link call for different evidence. Demand links are estimated from buyer behavior, which is noisy and often sparse. Constraint links are read from the firm's own commitments and resources, which are known with more precision but are often recorded in places the engine cannot read. Bootstrapping's encoded inputs are the first test of whether a constraint link can enter the engine at all.

#### Complexity Tax

Coupling has a cost that grows faster than the catalog. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the scale. A catalog of 20 items has 20 times 19 divided by 2, or 190, distinct pairs that could be linked. A catalog of 300 items has 300 times 299 divided by 2, or 44,850 pairs. No firm can estimate tens of thousands of cross-effects, and an engine that tried would fit noise in most of them, the overfitting risk Intelligence warned against in conjectures with too many parameters.

The practical response is to encode few links and to choose them by expected consequence. Most pairs in a large catalog are weakly linked or not linked at all. Tiers within one product, a base plan and its add-ons, and regional editions of the same offer are the strong links, and they can be named in advance from the structure Differentiation built. The engine then treats the catalog as a set of clusters, coupled within each cluster and approximately independent across them, and it records which links it has omitted so that an omission can be revisited when evidence arrives.

Behavior's account of bounded attention applies to the people who supervise the engine as well as to buyers. A portfolio model with many weak links is harder to explain than one with a few strong ones, and an engine whose proposals cannot be explained fails the auditability requirement Intelligence set. Bootstrapping's minimum engine priced items one at a time and listed cross-item coupling among its non-goals. The first portfolio extension should add the strongest cluster, measure whether joint pricing improves contribution against the per-item baseline, and add further clusters only when the gain covers the added cost of estimation and review.

### 16.2 Cannibalization

#### Cross-Effects

Cannibalization is the portfolio name for internal rivalry: a sale gained by one item that would otherwise have gone to another item of the same firm. In the language of modern industrial organization it is a cross-price effect among substitutes in the firm's own catalog. When the price of one item falls, its own volume rises, and part of the rise comes from buyers who leave a sibling item. The firm's gain from the cut is the contribution on the new volume minus the contribution lost on the sibling.

The textbook treatment of a multiproduct seller states the consequence in one sentence. The condition that sets each price includes, besides the item's own margin and own response, the margins of the other items weighted by how their volumes respond to this price. Among substitutes that added term argues for a higher price than independent pricing would choose, because part of what a cut gains is taken from the firm itself. Among complements it argues for a lower price, because a sale of one item carries sales of the other. The engine does not need the full system of cross-elasticities to act on this. It needs the direction of each strong link and a rough size, and Uncertainty's discipline applies: sparse evidence does not identify a full cross-demand system, and a model that claims to have identified one is reporting its assumptions as findings.

#### Trade Paths

Cannibalization in a technology catalog follows paths that Differentiation's lines create. Trade-down runs from a higher tier to a lower one when the lower tier becomes cheaper or richer. Trade-up runs the other way when the gap between tiers narrows. A free tier diverts buyers who would have paid, which was the cost of freemium that Strategy priced as an investment in reach. A regional edition priced below another invites buyers to cross the border if the fence between editions is weak.

Product-line theory explains why trade-down is the central risk. K. Sridhar Moorthy's 1984 analysis of product line design under self-selection showed that when buyers choose among quality levels, a seller who offers a lower-quality item to reach low-valuation buyers must price the higher-quality item so that high-valuation buyers do not prefer the cheaper one, and that the seller may reduce the quality of the lower item to make that easier. Moorthy's cannibalization is specifically this self-selection of high-valuation buyers into the lower-quality item. This book uses the term more broadly, for any diversion of sales among the firm's own items, including diversion across regions and between paid and free tiers.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the size of the effect. A Basic tier sells at 40 with contribution 30 per account on 1,000 accounts, and a Pro tier sells at 100 with contribution 80 on 300 accounts, for joint contribution of 30,000 plus 24,000, or 54,000. A proposal cuts Basic to 36, leaving contribution of 26, and Basic rises to 1,250 accounts, for 32,500. Judged alone, the cut gains 2,500. If 100 of the 250 added accounts come from Pro, Pro falls to 200 accounts and 16,000, a loss of 8,000. Joint contribution falls to 48,500, a loss of 5,500, from a proposal that the Basic owner would have scored as a success.

#### Diversion Proxies

The illustration turns on one number, the share of the added Basic volume that came from Pro: 100 of 250, or 0.4. Merger analysis in modern industrial organization calls this share the diversion ratio, and it is the most useful single proxy for cannibalization because it can be estimated from evidence a young firm already has. Upgrade and downgrade logs show which accounts moved between tiers after a price change. Exit surveys at downgrade ask which offer replaced the old one. Cohort comparisons across regions with different prices show how much of a gap buyers cross. None of these is clean, and each should be recorded with the error Segmentation assigned to proxies, but each gives direction and rough magnitude.

The diversion ratio also gives the engine a simple screen before any model is fitted. A cut on one item pays for the firm only if the contribution on the added volume exceeds the contribution lost on the diverted share. In the illustration the comparison is 2,500 gained against 8,000 lost, and the screen rejects the cut before any experiment runs. The break-even ratio follows from the same numbers. Each diverted account costs 80 on Pro, and the diverted accounts are already counted in the Basic gain of 2,500, so the cut breaks even when 80 times the diverted accounts equals 2,500, which is 31.25 accounts out of 250, a diversion ratio of 0.125. Above that ratio the cut loses contribution for the firm, however well it scores for the tier.

Ignoring cannibalization is still a stance. An engine that scores each item alone has assumed every diversion ratio is zero, and its proposals will cut prices on lower tiers more often than the firm would choose with the links stated. Intelligence's scenarios stress the conjectures that the engine holds about rivals. The same scenarios should stress the conjectures it holds about the firm's own items, since an error in a diversion ratio costs contribution as surely as an error about a rival's response.

### 16.3 Constraints

#### Shared Binds

Optimization stated constraints for a pricing decision: margin floors, promises that limit how prices may move, and the shadow price that measures what a binding constraint costs. Those constraints were written as if each applied to one price at a time, and for a single product they do. In a catalog many of them apply to several prices at once. A margin commitment made to investors applies to the blended result of the catalog, not to each item. A support budget is consumed by all items in proportion to their volume. A promise that a higher tier always gives more for the price applies to the relation between tiers, not to either tier alone. These are shared limits, and their defining property is that whether one price satisfies them depends on the other prices.

Feasibility in a catalog is therefore a property of the whole vector. A vector can contain only prices that would each pass a per-item check and still violate a shared limit, and a vector containing a price that fails a per-item check can still satisfy every limit the firm has actually committed to. The engine's constraint layer has to evaluate the vector, and the per-item checks that Bootstrapping encoded for the minimum engine become, at portfolio scale, approximations to be replaced where the true limit is shared.

Shared costs make the point sharper. Where costs are joint, in the sense Baumol, Panzar, and Willig analyzed, the margin of any one item depends on how the joint cost is allocated, and a per-item floor encodes the allocation rule as if it were a commitment. The commitment the firm has made is usually to a total: contribution that covers the joint cost of the catalog. A floor on the mix states that commitment directly.

#### Mix Floors

A separate labeled illustration with invented numbers, not the 16.2 case, shows how a shared floor differs from per-item floors. A firm commits to a blended contribution margin of at least 50 percent across two tiers. Basic sells at 40 with variable cost 22, a margin of 45 percent, on 1,000 accounts. Pro sells at 100 with variable cost 40, a margin of 60 percent, on 300 accounts. Revenue is 40,000 plus 30,000, or 70,000. Variable cost is 22,000 plus 12,000, or 34,000. Contribution is 36,000, and the blended margin is 36,000 divided by 70,000, about 51.4 percent. A per-item floor of 50 percent would reject the Basic price, although the catalog meets the commitment.

Now suppose, with no price changed, that 100 accounts trade down from Pro to Basic. Basic has 1,100 accounts, for revenue of 44,000 and variable cost of 24,200. Pro has 200 accounts, for revenue of 20,000 and variable cost of 8,000. Revenue is 64,000, contribution is 31,800, and the blended margin is about 49.7 percent. The commitment fails, although every price is unchanged and every per-item check gives the same answer as before. A shared limit depends on the mix that the prices induce, so the constraint layer needs the cross-effects that Cannibalization estimated, and an error in a diversion ratio becomes an error in feasibility as well as in contribution.

The Ramsey rule describes the structure of the best vector under a shared floor. Frank Ramsey's 1927 contribution to the theory of taxation asked how to raise a fixed revenue with least loss of surplus, and Marcel Boiteux's 1956 analysis of public monopolies required to balance their budgets asked how to price several outputs so that the total covers cost. Both arrived at the same rule for independent demands: the markup over marginal cost, as a share of price, on each item should be inversely proportional to the item's elasticity of demand, so that items whose buyers respond least carry the largest markups. Ramsey's problem was a tax and Boiteux's a regulated public enterprise. This book applies the rule to a private firm's own contribution floor, where it says which items should carry the burden when the floor binds, and it adds cross-effects where demands are linked.

#### Vector Feasibility

Promise envelopes are shared limits written for buyers. Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, holds when the resources a price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, when contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them, and when the firm's stated policy and its realized practice agree. Applied to a catalog, the third condition makes stated relations among items binding on the vector. If the firm has stated that each tier gives more for its price than the tier below, an engine that cuts the lower tier past the point where a buyer gets more per unit of price from it than from the tier above has broken a stated policy without changing any single promise. The engine encodes such relations as ordering constraints among prices, and Audit records which ordering constraint blocked a proposal.

Rules set outside the firm can also span products. Disclosure requirements, consumer rules about reference prices, and contractual parity terms may apply to a product family or to every offer sold in a market. The engine treats them as shared limits on the vector, and their full statement belongs to Enterprise.

Infeasibility is a finding, not a malfunction. When no vector satisfies every shared limit at once, the engine reports which limits conflict and what each would cost, using the shadow prices Optimization introduced, and Authority decides which limit gives way. Scaling takes up what happens when such conflicts arrive faster than any owner can decide them.

### 16.4 Price Vector

#### Emitted Vector

The price vector is the output Part III promised: an assignment of prices, and of the related instrument settings, to every active item in the catalog at a given time. Each entry carries more than a number. It carries the instrument fields the item uses, such as list price, discount band, meter rate, or bundle price; the approval class that Bootstrapping assigned to a change of that size; the shared limits that bind at the proposed values; and a reason code that names the input or conjecture responsible for the move. An entry without these fields is a price, and a vector of such entries cannot be supervised.

Real time in this sense means that the vector is current with respect to the inputs the firm has chosen to track, at the cadence Dynamics assigned to each class of item. It does not mean that every price changes continuously. Most entries in a large catalog are unchanged in most cycles, and an engine that moved them without cause would create the noise in buyer reference points that Dynamics warned against. The vector is better understood as a standing state that the engine revises when its inputs change, with every revision recorded against the state it replaced.

The vector also carries the joint quantities that per-item lists omit: forecast contribution for the catalog, the slack remaining on each shared limit, and the cross-effects assumed among the items in each cluster. These are the terms in which Governance's Authority states its commitments, so they are the terms in which the engine reports whether the commitments hold.

#### Partial Moves

Most revisions touch a few items. A cost change, a rival move, or a new segment rule affects one cluster and leaves the rest of the catalog alone. The engine then solves a partial problem: it frees the items directly affected and their strongly linked neighbors, holds the rest at their current values, and checks the shared limits against the full vector. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the saving. In a catalog of 300 items, a cost change affects 12 items, and 18 further items have a diversion ratio above 0.1 with one of the 12. The engine re-solves 30 items, or 10 percent of the catalog, and checks the result against shared limits computed over all 300.

Partial updates inherit Dynamics' move bounds and Bootstrapping's approval classes. A neighbor freed only because of a link may move within a narrow band without review, while the directly affected items follow the class their change implies. When a partial solution breaks a shared limit that only a wider solve can satisfy, the engine widens the set in steps and records each widening, so that a reviewer can see why a cost change in one cluster moved prices in another.

The choice of links to free is itself a conjecture. A diversion threshold of 0.1 in the illustration is a policy parameter, set by the owner of the engine and revised under Learning's rules when evidence shows that omitted neighbors moved more than assumed. Each partial solve records the threshold it used, the neighbors it held fixed, and the slack it left on every shared limit, so that a later full solve can show whether holding those items fixed cost contribution. A firm that never runs a full solve cannot know what its partial solves have left on the table, and a periodic full solve, outside the live cycle, is the check.

#### Evolution Pointers

The same records point toward changes in the catalog itself. An item whose demand is drawn almost entirely from a sibling, with diversion near one in both directions, adds complexity without adding buyers, and it is a candidate to merge or retire. A shared limit that binds in most cycles, with a high shadow price, signals that the catalog is asking one resource or one promise to carry too much. A segment that persistently trades across a fence signals that Differentiation's fence has worn. The engine reports these as pointers, with the evidence attached, and leaves the decision to the owners of lines and segments. It does not design products, and a pointer is an input to Policy, not a product strategy.

A human-readable summary closes every material revision. It states, in a few sentences, which items moved and by how much, which inputs or conjectures caused the moves, which shared limits bind, what joint contribution the vector forecasts against the previous one, and which pointers are open. Intelligence required that one price be explainable. At portfolio scale the summary extends that requirement to the vector, so that one explanation covers many items, and a reviewer can confirm integrity in the sense Value defined and Governance extended: the resources the price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them, and the firm's stated policy and its realized practice agree.

Portfolio thus completes the engine as a mapping from environment to a coupled vector under inherited Policy. Scaling asks what breaks when that mapping runs across many markets at speed, where coupled feedback can reproduce the oscillation Dynamics damped for one family. Enterprise asks who owns the vector, who answers for its summaries, and how the external rules on which the shared limits rest are stated and kept.

## Chapter 17 — Scaling

### Introduction
#### Scale Limits

Chapter 17 of Pricing Tech is Scaling. Portfolio defined the coupled catalog and the real-time price vector. Scaling asks what happens when that apparatus must run under portfolio size and environmental speed that defeat human item-by-item control: the limits where exception queues overflow, the throughput and decision rights that keep latency acceptable, multi-market rollout with localized constraints, and failure modes with rollback plans. Enterprise will later place a dedicated function and regulatory envelopes. This chapter owns the scale problem of the Engine in operation.

The arc runs Limits, Throughput, Markets, Failure. Limits name when humans fail—exception overload, catalog size, and environment speed. Throughput designs decision latency, approval bottlenecks, automation classes, and service levels for prices. Markets treats geo and segment rollout, localization of constraints, transfer of learning, and sequence risk. Failure pre-mortems oscillation at scale, data breaks, organizational rejection, and rollback. Bootstrapping’s supervised first loop is assumed; Scaling is what happens when that loop must cover more of the firm without becoming a black box.

#### Limits, Throughput

Limits are the Part III premise made operational. A founder can price three plans in a meeting; a catalog of hundreds across regions and contract generations cannot. Policy can still be right and still be too slow if every exception needs a signature. Exception overload is the Behavior substrate Governance tried to bound, now returning as a scale signal: when exception volume and approval latency tax contribution more than automation risk, the human-item limit has been hit. Sales working around the desk, finance reconciling after the fact, and product opening one-off SKUs are symptoms of the same limit. Portfolio size and environmental speed are joint triggers—either alone can force Engine coverage; together they force it sooner.

Throughput is the operating design for decisions under that limit. Decision latency, approval bottlenecks, and automation classes inherit Bootstrapping’s graduation criteria and Governance’s Authority map: which moves stay approval-required, which run supervised-automatic, which need kill-switch readiness. An SLA for prices—how stale a cell may be, how fast a Contest may be answered—is a Policy object Dynamics’ cadence must satisfy at scale. Throughput without stated rights recreates hallway improvisation inside the engine ops team.

#### Markets, Failure

Markets are rollout design. Geo and segment expansion transfers Learning’s memory and Intelligence’s conjectures imperfectly; constraints localize—regulatory envelopes, cost-to-serve, rival sets. Sequence risk matters: rolling the engine into a market before Codification and stance are owned there imports Behavior’s exception culture under a new flag. Transfer of learning is allowed; pretending transfer is identification is not. Thin-data honesty from Uncertainty still binds in each market.

Failure modes at scale are expected objects, not surprises. Oscillation that Dynamics damped on one family can reappear when Portfolio couples many SKUs under noisy feedback. Data breaks—stale cost, missing eligibility, broken proxies—turn Data Debt into outages. Organizational rejection—sales bypass, finance distrust—fails Audit even when the math is fine. Rollback plans and kill switches are Supervision scaled: how to shrink automation classes without stranding customers on inconsistent lists. Pre-mortem is Policy discipline, not theater.

The sequence Limits, Throughput, Markets, Failure is analytic. Throughput without limits mis-sizes the build. Markets without failure planning export mistakes. Closing with Failure returns the reader to Part III’s accountability claim: speed that cannot roll back is not inheritance; it is exposure.

#### Scale Edges

Scaling does not finalize enterprise organizational design or the full regulatory and contractual envelope—that is Enterprise. It does not redo Portfolio’s vector mathematics except where scale changes feasibility. What this chapter owns is Engine operation under size and speed: limits, throughput, multi-market rollout, and failure with rollback.

Scaling thus tests whether the earlier chapters were built to carry load. Rules, inputs, and supervision that hold for one market and a modest catalog must either extend to many, with failure modes stated in advance, or be revised before expansion rather than after it.

### 17.1 Limits

#### Human Ceiling

Every pricing organization has a ceiling on the number of price decisions its people can make well in a period. The ceiling is set by attention rather than by intelligence. Simon's account of bounded rationality, which Behavior and Governance applied to founders and committees, implies that a reviewer who must judge more cases than attention allows will either slow down or judge carelessly, and usually both. Part III began from this premise. Bootstrapping placed a first supervised loop under it, and Portfolio showed that coupling among items multiplies the work, since a change to one price may require review of its neighbors. Scaling asks when the ceiling binds and how to see it binding before the costs appear in contribution.

The ceiling binds through three channels that can be measured separately: the rate at which exceptions arrive, the size of the catalog that must be kept current, and the speed at which the environment changes the prices the firm would choose. Each can force wider engine coverage alone. Together they force it sooner, because a larger catalog in a faster environment generates more exceptions per period than either change implies on its own. The firm's people then face a choice the earlier chapters anticipated. They can widen the automation classes under stated rules, or they can let decisions be made informally by whoever is closest to the customer, which returns the exception culture that Governance set out to bound.

#### Exception Load

Exception load is the first and most visible channel. Little's law, proved by Little (1961) for queueing systems in steady state, states that the average number of items waiting equals the average arrival rate times the average time each item waits. Applied to a deal desk or an approval queue, it turns a vague complaint about slowness into an accounting identity. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the use. Suppose exceptions arrive at 40 per working day and each waits on average three days for a decision. The queue then holds 40 × 3 = 120 open exceptions on a typical day. If two reviewers can each close 25 per day, capacity is 50, and utilization is 40 / 50 = 0.8. Queueing theory shows that waiting time rises steeply as utilization approaches one, so a rise in arrivals to 48 per day, a utilization of 0.96, would lengthen waits far more than the 20 percent rise in arrivals suggests.

The law does not say why exceptions arrive. It says that waiting time, queue length, and arrival rate cannot be managed independently. A firm that caps the queue without adding capacity or reducing arrivals has only moved the waiting elsewhere, usually into sales staff who stop filing exceptions and grant concessions on their own authority. That behavior appears in the change record as a fall in exceptions and in the contribution record as a rise in unexplained discounts. The two records together reveal the ceiling; either alone can hide it.

The response follows from the identity. Arrivals fall when Codification writes the common exceptions into rules, so that a case that recurs weekly stops being an exception. Waiting falls when Bootstrapping's approval classes route small changes past review. Capacity rises only by adding reviewers, which is the costliest option and the one that leaves the ceiling in place.

#### Catalog Speed

Catalog size and environmental speed form the second and third channels, and they interact. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the arithmetic. A catalog of 600 items sold in four regions has 600 × 4 = 2,400 price cells. If inputs drift enough to justify review of 5 percent of cells each week, the firm faces 0.05 × 2,400 = 120 reviews per week from drift alone, before any rival move or customer request. If the environment speeds up so that 15 percent of cells drift each week, reviews rise to 360. Doubling the regions to eight at the faster rate gives 0.15 × 4,800 = 720. Review time per cell does not fall as cells multiply, and Portfolio's coupling means that some reviews require looking at neighbors, so the human work grows faster than the cell count.

Dynamics assigned cadence classes to items so that prices move no more often than their inputs justify. At scale, the cadence classes become a capacity plan. Each class implies a review rate, and the sum across classes must stay below what the organization can absorb with the reviewers it has. When it does not, the classes with the lowest stakes per review are the first candidates for supervised automation, since errors there cost least and the review time saved is largest.

The ceiling is reached, in practice, when the cost of waiting exceeds the expected cost of automation error for some class of decisions. Waiting has a cost Dynamics measured as staleness: prices that lag their inputs lose contribution on every unit sold in the interval. Automation error has a cost the supervision regime bounds through move limits and kill switches. Comparing the two, class by class, tells the firm where to widen automation and where human review still pays. Throughput takes up how the remaining decisions should be routed so that the review the firm keeps is timely.

### 17.2 Throughput

#### Serial Steps

Throughput, in modern operations usage, is the rate at which a system completes work, here the rate at which justified price changes reach the market. A pricing engine can compute a new vector in seconds, yet the time from a cost change to a live price may run to weeks. The difference lies in the serial steps that computation cannot shorten: data arriving from finance, a reviewer reading a proposal, an owner signing it, a system publishing it to channels. Throughput design starts by listing these steps and timing each.

Amdahl (1967) made the governing point for parallel computing: if a fraction of a task must run serially, speeding up the rest cannot shorten the whole below that fraction. The book applies his argument to decision cycles rather than processors, a borrowing noted here because his concern was hardware speedup. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the consequence. Suppose 20 percent of the elapsed time from input change to live price is serial approval and 80 percent is work the engine can accelerate. If the engine makes that 80 percent ten times faster, the cycle shrinks to 0.2 + 0.8 / 10 = 0.28 of its former length, a speedup of about 3.6. Even infinite acceleration of the computable part yields at most 1 / 0.2 = 5. The firm that invests only in faster models therefore hits a limit set by its approval structure.

#### Approval Lanes

The approval structure is where throughput is won or lost. Governance's Authority map assigned decision rights; Bootstrapping divided changes into approval classes by size and risk. At scale these become lanes. A routine lane carries changes within stated move bounds and binding rules, published automatically and sampled afterward by a reviewer. A review lane carries changes that exceed a bound or touch a promise, held until a named owner approves. An escalation lane carries changes that cross a shared limit Portfolio defined or that rest on a newly registered conjecture, held until Authority decides.

The lanes work only if their boundaries are stated as inputs rather than left to judgment at the moment of routing. A change routed by whoever happens to see it first produces the improvised decision rights that Governance warned about, now inside the team that runs the engine. Each lane therefore has an owner, an entry rule, a capacity in decisions per period, and a time limit after which an unanswered proposal is either published at its default or withdrawn, as the lane's rule specifies. The default matters. A lane that publishes on silence trades risk for speed; a lane that withdraws on silence trades speed for safety. Both are acceptable when chosen and recorded, and neither is acceptable when it happens by accident.

Little's law applies within each lane. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows how lane design meets a target. If proposals enter the review lane at 20 per day and 60 are typically waiting, average wait is 60 / 20 = 3 days. To bring the wait to one day at the same arrival rate, the queue must fall to 20, which requires either more reviewing capacity or a rule that moves part of the inflow to the routine lane. Moving the smallest third of proposals, about 7 per day, cuts arrivals to 13 and, at an unchanged three-day wait, the queue to 39; shortening the wait requires that reviewers close faster as well.

#### Price Service

The output of lane design is a service level for prices, a stated standard for how stale a price may become and how quickly a challenge to it is answered. Modern operations usage calls such a standard a service level agreement; here it is an internal Policy object owned by Authority rather than a contract with a customer, and the book uses the term in that internal sense. A service level might state that cost-driven changes in the routine lane are live within one day, that review-lane changes are decided within three, and that a customer's formal challenge to a price is answered within five.

The service level ties the earlier chapters to operating fact. Dynamics measured staleness cost; the service level caps it. Governance gave customers and sales a path to contest a price; the service level states how long that path may take. Intelligence required that every price be explainable; the service level requires that the explanation be available when the challenge arrives rather than reconstructed later.

Service levels also expose capacity limits honestly. When a lane misses its level for several periods, the record shows which step caused the delay, whether data arrival, review, or publication, and Learning's revision rules decide whether to widen a lane, add capacity, or relax the level. Missing a stated level is information. Missing an unstated one is invisible until buyers or sales staff work around it. Markets takes up what changes when the same lanes must serve several regions and segments with different constraints.

### 17.3 Markets

#### Rollout Order

An engine proven in one market is a candidate for others, and the order of rollout is a decision with its own risks. Each market added brings new buyers, rivals, channels, and rules, and each tests whether the engine's inputs were written generally or were quietly tied to the market where they were first set. Rollout by geography and rollout by segment raise the same questions: which markets come first, how long each runs under supervision before the next begins, and what evidence permits the step.

The case for sequence over simultaneity rests on learning and containment. A market added alone produces evidence that can be attributed to it. Several added at once mix their effects, and a failure in one may not be traced before it spreads to the others. Bootstrapping's graduation criteria apply market by market: a new market begins in the review lane, graduates classes to the routine lane only after meeting stated criteria, and keeps its own kill switch. A market that misses its criteria stays in review, and the next market waits until the cause is understood.

Sequence risk runs in both directions. Rolling out to a market before its owners have codified segment rules and a competitive stance imports the exception culture of that market into the engine, where it becomes harder to see. Waiting too long leaves the market on manual prices whose staleness cost Dynamics measured. A practical order begins with markets whose rules most resemble the home market, whose data are adequate, and whose stakes allow error, and reserves until later those with distinct regulation or concentrated accounts.

#### Local Bounds

Constraints localize. A margin floor set in one currency may not translate at the exchange rate, because cost to serve differs by region. Regulatory envelopes differ in disclosure duties, permitted forms of discrimination, and rules on price changes to existing contracts. Rival sets differ, so a response rule written against one rival's observed conduct has no counterpart where that rival is absent. Promises differ, since a commitment made to buyers in one market binds there and may not exist elsewhere.

The engine handles this by separating shared inputs from local ones. Shared inputs, such as the objective, the definitions of contribution and of each instrument, and the integrity standard, are owned centrally and change only through central revision. Local inputs, such as floors, envelopes, stances, and promise constraints, are owned in each market and recorded with their owner and date. A price in a given market is then explained by the shared inputs plus that market's local ones, so Intelligence's audit trail remains complete.

Integrity applies in each market on the same terms: it holds when the resources a market's price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, when contributions to the value produced there can be traced to those who made them, and when stated policy in that market and realized practice agree. A firm that states one policy globally and practices another locally fails the third condition even if every local price is defensible by itself. The full content of regional regulation and contracts is the subject of Enterprise; this section requires only that the engine hold local bounds as named, owned inputs rather than as patches.

#### Borrowed Evidence

Learning in one market is evidence about another, but weaker evidence. Uncertainty's thin-data rules apply: a new market with few observations should lean on prior information, and the home market's estimates are one source of that prior. The book uses partial pooling, in the sense of modern hierarchical estimation, to describe the balance. Each market's estimate is a weighted blend of its own data and the pooled estimate across markets, with weight on its own data rising as observations accumulate.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows one simple weighting rule. Let the weight on a market's own estimate be n / (n + k), where n is its number of observations and k is a constant the owner sets to express how much the markets are believed to differ. With k = 600, a new market with 200 observations gives its own data weight 200 / 800 = 0.25 and the pooled estimate 0.75. After 1,800 observations, the weight on its own data rises to 1,800 / 2,400 = 0.75. The constant k is a conjecture in Intelligence's sense, registered with a falsifier: if markets with large n consistently diverge from the pooled estimate by more than the rule implies, k is set too high.

Transfer of learning is permitted. Treating transferred learning as if it were identified in the new market is not, since buyers, rivals, and channels differ in ways the home data cannot reveal. Each market therefore records which of its parameters are borrowed and which are its own, and a reviewer can see when a price rests mostly on another market's evidence. Failure takes up what happens when the borrowing, the coupling, or the supporting organization breaks.

### 17.4 Failure

#### Coupled Swings

Failure at scale differs in kind from failure in one market. Perrow (1984) argued that systems combining interactive complexity, in which components affect one another in unplanned ways, with tight coupling, in which effects spread before anyone can intervene, will produce accidents that no single component failure explains. He wrote about high-risk technologies such as nuclear plants and chemical processing; the book borrows only his two dimensions, noted here because a pricing engine is far less dangerous and far easier to halt. A large engine nevertheless acquires both properties. Portfolio coupled items through cannibalization and shared limits; Markets coupled regions through borrowed evidence and arbitrage. A change that is stable for one family can become unstable across many.

Oscillation is the characteristic case. Dynamics showed that a price responding too strongly to lagged feedback can cycle, and set move bounds and damping to prevent it. At scale, several damped loops can interact. Two regions that respond to each other's prices through cross-border buyers, each within its own bounds, can together produce a cycle neither would produce alone, because each region's response is the other's input. The defense extends the same tools: move bounds stated on joint quantities as well as single prices, a cadence that staggers revisions in coupled markets, and a monitor that flags any cluster whose prices have reversed direction several times within a set number of cycles.

#### Broken Feeds

Data breaks are the most common failure and often the costliest, because an engine acts on bad inputs as confidently as on good ones. Reason (1990, 1997) distinguished active failures, the errors of people at the point of action, from latent conditions, flaws built into a system earlier that wait for a trigger. A stale cost table, a renamed field, or an eligibility list that stopped updating is a latent condition. The engine's proposal on the day the condition meets a live price is the active failure, though no one at that moment made an error.

A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows why detection speed matters more than model quality here. Suppose a cost feed stops updating after a cost rise of 8 per unit, and the engine continues to price 2,000 units per day at a margin floor computed on the old cost. Each day loses 8 × 2,000 = 16,000 of contribution the floor was meant to protect. Detected after three days, the loss is 48,000; detected within a day, it is at most 16,000. Input monitors that check freshness, range, and agreement with a second source therefore belong in the engine's specification, and a failed check moves the affected classes to the review lane automatically.

A documented case shows how fast a deployment error can act in an automated system. In August 2012 Knight Capital, a securities market maker, deployed new order-routing code to its servers but left obsolete code active on one of them; in about 45 minutes the system sent millions of unintended orders and the firm lost roughly 460 million dollars. The SEC's 2013 order against the firm found inadequate controls on deployment and on monitoring of automated orders. A pricing engine moves far slower, but the pattern of a latent deployment flaw meeting an automated actor with no effective halt is the same.

#### Rollback Plans

Organizational rejection is a failure the math cannot detect. Sales staff who distrust the engine grant discounts beside it; finance staff who cannot reconcile its output rebuild prices in spreadsheets. Each workaround leaves the engine's record looking sound while realized practice departs from stated policy. Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, holds when the resources a price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, when contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them, and when the firm's stated policy and its realized practice agree. Rejection breaks the third condition first and the second soon after. The signal is a widening gap between engine prices and invoiced prices, which the contribution records Governance required will show if anyone compares them.

A rollback plan states in advance how automation shrinks when any of these failures occurs. It names who may trigger it, which classes return to the review lane in what order, which prices freeze at their last approved values, and how customers who received a faulty price are treated. Freezing at last approved values matters, because rolling back to defaults can strand buyers on inconsistent lists that themselves violate stated promises. Each plan is rehearsed, in the sense of Intelligence's simulation, before the expansion it protects.

Gary Klein's pre-mortem (2007), now a common decision practice, asks a team to assume a launch has failed and to list the causes. Applied before each rollout step, it turns the failures above into named conjectures with monitors and owners. Scaling thus closes with the engine as a supervised system whose failure modes are stated before they occur. Enterprise takes up the function that owns it and the regulatory and contractual envelope within which it answers for its prices.

## Chapter 18 — Enterprise

### Introduction
#### Org Form

Chapter 18 of Pricing Tech is Enterprise. Scaling named the limits where human item pricing fails, the throughput design that keeps decisions timely, multi-market rollout, and failure with rollback. Enterprise places that apparatus in an organization outsiders can still hold to account: a dedicated pricing function, regulatory and contractual envelopes, accountability when prices are machine-proposed, and an analytic close of the Strategy–Policy–Engine sequence that does not abolish inherited reasoning or governance. No further parts follow; an epilogue closes the book.

The arc runs Function, Regulation, Accountability, Destination. Function specifies a dedicated team, skills mix, and interfaces to product, sales, and finance so the founding myth of one-head pricing is retired without inventing a black-box priesthood. Regulation treats legal constraints, contractual price terms, disclosure, and jurisdiction variance as binds the engine inherits rather than as afterthoughts. Accountability answers who speaks for a price to customers and regulators, how integrity operates at enterprise scale, and what override culture remains when automation classes widen. Destination states the end-product claim of the book: an auditable engine that maps environment to a real-time price vector under Policy and Strategy inheritance—not autonomy as fashion.

#### Function, Regulation

Function is organizational design for Engine ownership. A PhD-level pricing capability—economics, optimization, data, and product fluency—interfaces with sales deal desks, finance contribution views, and product line owners. Bootstrapping’s inputs and Governance’s Authority map need seats that persist when founders leave. Interfaces matter as much as models: without them, Intelligence’s scenarios stay inside engineering and Audit fails in practice. Retiring the founding myth does not mean erasing Strategy judgment; it means judgment is encoded, owned, and revisable as Policy the function maintains.

Regulation is the external envelope. Legal constraints on discrimination and disclosure, contractual price terms with enterprises and platforms, and jurisdiction variance localize what Optimization called regulatory envelopes and what Markets in Scaling must respect. Contractual commitments are Path Dependence at enterprise scale: promises the engine cannot silently optimize away. Disclosure requirements extend Intelligence’s auditability outward—explainability is sometimes a legal object, not only an internal virtue. Integrity remains efficiency and contribution accountability along the chain, not an ethics sermon.

#### Accountability, Destination

Accountability is answerability when the price was proposed by a machine. Customers, regulators, and boards still need a named function and a reconstructable trail from inputs and conjectures to the vector cell. Override culture from Supervision and Softening must survive scale: automatic classes that cannot be stopped, or that sales always bypass, both fail Accountability. Enterprise therefore binds Engine to Governance permanently: inheritance, not replacement.

Destination names the end product. The end product is the engine described since the book introduction and Part III: environmental inputs, simulated alternatives, conjectures about buyers and rivals, segment and constraint structure, and a real-time price vector plus portfolio-evolution pointers—under human-legible audit. Strategy remains the economic spine; Policy remains the rule spine; Engine is the execution spine under size and speed. Founder judgment and written policy are not optional middle chapters to be discarded at the finish. Black-box slogans about being data-driven are rejected for the same reason they were rejected at Bootstrapping: a price that cannot be traced is not progress.

The sequence Function, Regulation, Accountability, Destination is analytic. Regulation without a function has no owner. Accountability without regulation ignores the external envelope. Closing with Destination returns the reader to the opening map: judgment under thin data, rules under human control, then an auditable engine.

#### Enterprise Scope

Enterprise does not reopen Part I or Part II foundations; it inherits them. It does not reopen Scaling’s operational design. What this chapter owns is the organizational and external close of Part III: function, regulation, accountability, and the engine as end product under inheritance.

### 18.1 Function

#### Pricing Seat

A price structure that runs through an engine needs an owner that outlasts any person. Chandler (1962) traced how the large American industrial firms of the early twentieth century reorganized once their strategies of diversification outgrew a centralized structure, and he summarized the finding as structure following strategy. In The Visible Hand (1977) he described the salaried managerial hierarchy that took over coordination from the market in those firms. Chandler wrote about manufacturing, distribution, and the multidivisional form; this book borrows only his claim that a recurring coordination task acquires its own organizational unit once it is too large and too continuous for occasional attention, noted here because a pricing function is far smaller than the departments he studied.

Pricing reaches that point when the engine Scaling described is running. Founder judgment, encoded as Codification's rules and owned under Governance's authority map, now executes through approval classes and monitors across many items and markets. Someone has to maintain the rules, read the monitors, own the conjectures Intelligence made discussable, and answer when a price is questioned. Spread across product, sales, and finance, those tasks fall into the gaps between them, which is where the exception culture Behavior described grows. A dedicated function closes the gaps by holding the Policy objects as its own work product.

The function's charter follows from what the earlier parts placed on it. It maintains the instrument catalog, segment rules, and competitive stance as versioned objects; it owns the engine's conjectures, falsifiers, and update rules; it runs the experiments Learning required and records their results in memory; it holds the rollback plans Scaling wrote; and it prepares the summaries through which Governance reviews the vector. It does not hold final authority over strategy. Governance placed that authority with named owners, and the function serves them.

The function's placement follows the same logic. Reporting into sales tilts it toward volume and exceptions, reporting into finance tilts it toward margin floors, and reporting into product tilts it toward feature fences. A placement that reports to the executive who owns contribution across all three keeps the trade-offs the earlier parts named inside one budget.

#### Skills Mix

The skills follow the objects. Economic reasoning is needed to state willingness to pay, cross-effects, rival conjectures, and the constraints a price vector must respect, at the level of a doctoral economist or an equivalent practitioner. Optimization skill is needed to formulate the joint problem, read shadow prices, and judge when a solver's answer reflects the model rather than the market. Data skill is needed to build and monitor the inputs Bootstrapping listed and to keep Data Debt ranked. Product fluency is needed to understand what each item delivers and what a fence actually withholds.

No single hire covers all four, and a team built from one of them fails in a predictable direction. Economists without data engineers produce correct reasoning on inputs nobody maintains. Data scientists without economic training produce forecasts that ignore rivals and treat realized prices as if buyers had not chosen them. Optimization specialists without product fluency produce vectors that are feasible on paper and incoherent to buyers. The mix matters more than the headcount. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the scale: a firm with 400 items in 6 markets has 2,400 vector cells, and if the engine routes 3 percent of cells to human review each week, the function reviews 72 cells weekly, a load a team of four can carry with time left for experiments and conjecture maintenance.

The function must also resist becoming a priesthood. A team that alone can read the engine recreates the black box at the level of the organization: prices are traceable in principle and opaque in practice. Intelligence required that scenarios and conjectures be discussable by the people who answer for them. The function's standard of work is therefore that a product owner, a sales leader, or a finance controller can follow the reasoning behind any price in their domain from the summary the function supplies.

#### Interfaces

The interfaces carry the function's value into the rest of the firm. Sales receives prices, response rules, and exception lanes, and returns deal evidence: losses, buyer objections, and rival offers observed in negotiation. The deal desk Governance described becomes a channel inside the function's lanes rather than a parallel pricing authority. Finance receives contribution views by item, segment, and market, and returns cost-to-serve and margin floors; the contribution records Governance required are the shared ledger between them. Product owners receive the evidence on fences, versions, and cannibalization, and return roadmaps that tell the function which items will change before the engine sees the change in demand.

Each interface has a written cadence and a named counterpart, because an interface that runs only when a dispute arises transmits disputes. Legal and compliance staff form a fifth interface, through which the external rules that Regulation states enter the engine as constraints and through which records leave the firm when an authority asks for them. Function thus places the engine in the organization. Regulation states the rules from outside the firm that the function translates into binds on the vector.

### 18.2 Regulation

#### Price Differences

The engine charges different prices for related offers by design. Segmentation, Differentiation, and Portfolio built that variation from fences, versions, and coupled items, and Optimization treated regulatory envelopes as constraints on any single decision. The external rules that set those envelopes differ by jurisdiction and are described here as an economist reads them, as constraints on the feasible set and as signals of which effects authorities attend to, not as legal guidance.

In the United States the Robinson-Patman Act (1936) prohibits discrimination in price between different purchasers of commodities of like grade and quality where the effect may be substantially to lessen competition, tend to create a monopoly, or injure competition with the seller, the favored buyer, or their customers, and it allows defenses for differences justified by cost and for prices set in good faith to meet a competitor's offer. In the statute, price discrimination means only a price difference between purchasers; the difference is unlawful where its effect may be to harm competition in one of those ways and neither defense applies. That is not the economic sense Discrimination in Segmentation took from Pigou, in which prices differ across buyers or quantities with willingness to pay rather than cost, and the two meanings are often confused. Its reach to subscriptions and services is limited, since the Act addresses commodities. In the European Union, Article 102 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union prohibits abuse of a dominant position, and its listed examples include applying dissimilar conditions to equivalent transactions with other trading parties, thereby placing them at a competitive disadvantage. For a firm with a dominant position, segment rules therefore carry a burden of justification that the same rules would not carry for a smaller rival.

Contractual price terms bind the vector from the other side. Legacy Terms in Dynamics described price locks and most-favored-customer clauses as path dependence written into contracts. Platforms add parity terms, under which a seller promises not to offer a lower price on another channel. Article 5(3) of the Digital Markets Act (2022) prohibits designated gatekeepers from preventing business users from offering the same products or services to end users through third-party online intermediation services or their own direct online sales channels at prices or conditions different from those offered through the gatekeeper's online intermediation services. The engine treats each such term as a recorded constraint on the cells it covers, with its source and expiry.

A term that conflicts with a stated segment rule is resolved by Governance before the engine runs, since the engine cannot choose between two commitments the firm has made.

#### Coordination Law

Competition deferred the legal reading of matching clauses to this chapter. Section 1 of the Sherman Act (1890) prohibits contracts, combinations, and conspiracies in restraint of trade, and Article 101 of the Treaty prohibits agreements between undertakings, decisions by associations of undertakings, and concerted practices that may affect trade between Member States and have as their object or effect the prevention, restriction, or distortion of competition. Both turn on agreement or coordination between firms. A unilateral meet-competition or most-favored-customer clause is not an agreement among rivals, which is why authorities have examined such clauses mainly for their effects, case by case, and why Competition asked the firm to record why each term exists. A clause whose recorded purpose is buyer protection, with a band and an owner, reads differently in an inquiry from a clause nobody can explain.

Intelligence raised the harder case of rivals that set prices by software. The research Calvano and coauthors reported shows that learned coordination without communication is possible in simulation; the legal regimes above still turn mainly on agreement, exchanged information, or a common intermediary. In 2024 the United States Department of Justice, with several states, sued RealPage, alleging that its revenue management software let competing landlords share nonpublic data and align rents. In November 2025 the Department filed a proposed settlement under which RealPage would stop using rivals' nonpublic data to set prices. The allegation concerns pooled rival data feeding a shared recommendation, which is a different structure from an engine that reads only public rival prices. That distinction is the one the engine's input record must make visible: which rival prices were read, from what source, and whether any input came from rivals' private data through a vendor.

#### Disclosure Duties

Disclosure rules extend Intelligence's auditability outward. The EU's Omnibus Directive (2019/2161) amended the rules on price indication so that an announced price reduction must show the prior price, defined as the lowest price applied during a period of not less than 30 days before the reduction, and it requires traders to inform consumers when a price has been personalized by automated decision-making. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the effect on a promotion rule. Suppose an item sold at 40 for three weeks, rose to 50 for two weeks, and is now offered at 45 as 10 percent off 50. Under the prior-price rule the reference is 40, the lowest price in the preceding 30 days, so the offer is a rise of 5, or 12.5 percent, and cannot be shown as a discount. An engine that moves list prices and runs promotions independently will produce such cases unless the 30-day minimum is a stored input.

Jurisdiction variance makes these rules local constraints of the kind Markets in Scaling described. Rules on prior-price display, personalization notice, and changes to existing contracts differ by country, and the function keeps them in the catalog as constraints attached to markets and segments, each with its source and the date it was last confirmed, so that a rule change in one country reaches the engine as a dated revision rather than as a surprise in an audit. Regulation thus sets the envelope. Accountability asks who answers within it when the price was proposed by a machine.

### 18.3 Accountability

#### Named Speakers

Every price the engine emits needs a person or office that can speak for it. Governance assigned authority over each Policy object to a named owner, and Bootstrapping kept the first loop under supervision so that each automatic class had someone accountable for its outputs. At enterprise scale the question becomes external: when a customer disputes a renewal, a regulator requests the basis of a price, or a board asks why contribution fell in a segment, the answer must come from an owner who can reconstruct the price and defend the rule behind it.

The answer has three parts, each supplied by an earlier chapter. The input record from Intelligence shows what the engine read. The conjecture and rule record shows which Policy objects and which version produced the proposal, and who approved that version. The approval record from Supervision shows whether the price passed through an automatic class or a review lane, and who signed it if a person did. A price for which any of the three is missing cannot be answered for, whatever its economic merit.

Vendor engines do not change the allocation. A firm that licenses pricing software still owns the prices it charges, and the function keeps the same three records for a licensed engine as for one built in house. Where the vendor cannot supply the conjecture and rule record in a form the function can read, the licensed engine stays in the review lane, on the same terms Supervision set for any class whose outputs cannot be explained.

Some answers are owed by rule. The EU General Data Protection Regulation (2016), in Article 22, gives individuals a right not to be subject to a decision based solely on automated processing that produces legal or similarly significant effects on them, subject to exceptions, with safeguards that include human intervention. Whether a given price falls within that article depends on its circumstances and is left to counsel; the economic point is that human review lanes, which Supervision designed for error control, can also be the safeguard an outside rule requires.

#### Override Survival

Override culture must survive the widening of automatic classes. Softening and Supervision set two requirements that pull in opposite directions at scale. The engine must be stoppable: a kill switch that no one dares use, or an automatic class that no human can halt within a stated time, removes accountability by design. And the engine must not be bypassed: if sales can always grant a lower price beside the engine, stated policy and realized practice part, and the engine's record describes a firm that does not exist.

The function holds both requirements in view with a small set of measures. The override rate shows how often a human changes an engine proposal, by class and segment. The bypass rate shows how often invoiced prices differ from engine prices without an approved override. Each has a band set as Policy. A labeled illustration with invented numbers shows the reading. Suppose the engine proposes 1,200 renewal prices in a week, reviewers override 90 of them, and invoices differ from engine prices on another 60 without approval. The override rate is 90 divided by 1,200, or 7.5 percent, and the bypass rate is 60 divided by 1,200, or 5 percent. An override band of 5 to 10 percent says the review lane is doing work; any bypass rate above zero says the lane is being avoided, and the function traces those 60 prices to their channel before widening any class.

Overrides also feed the double loop Learning described. A cluster of overrides in one segment is evidence that a rule or conjecture is wrong, and the rule revision process treats it as such rather than as a compliance failure by reviewers.

#### Enterprise Integrity

Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, holds when the resources a price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, when contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them, and when the firm's stated policy and its realized practice agree. At enterprise scale each condition acquires an external reader. Customers read the third condition when a published promise meets an invoice. Regulators read it when a stated pricing rationale meets the record. Boards read the first two when contribution by segment meets the resources spent winning it.

The three readers ask for the same objects in different summaries. A customer dispute needs one cell explained; a regulatory request needs a rule and its history; a board review needs contribution and override measures by segment. The function builds all three from the single record rather than writing separate accounts for each audience, because separate accounts drift apart and the drift is itself a departure of practice from stated policy.

The disclosure and coordination rules above give the third condition legal weight in some settings, but the economic case does not depend on them. A firm whose stated policy and practice agree can explain its prices at low cost to anyone who asks; a firm whose practice departs from policy pays for each explanation, and pays most when the request comes from an authority. Enterprise therefore keeps the engine bound to Governance as a permanent condition of operation rather than as a phase the firm passes through. Destination states what that binding produces.

### 18.4 Destination

#### End Product

The book set out to describe how a technology firm's pricing moves from founder judgment to written rules to an engine, and to show what each stage must leave to the next. The end product is now fully specified. An engine reads environmental inputs, the costs, demand signals, rival prices, and constraints Bootstrapping listed and Intelligence traced; it holds conjectures about buyers and rivals, each with a falsifier and an update rule; it simulates alternative moves against those conjectures and stresses them across scenarios; it applies segment rules, fences, and a competitive stance written as Policy objects; and it emits a real-time price vector over a coupled catalog, feasible under the shared limits Portfolio stated and the external rules Regulation described, together with pointers to how the portfolio itself should evolve.

Each element has a chapter that specified it and a chapter that tested it at scale. Inputs came from Bootstrapping and were checked for freshness in Scaling; conjectures came from Intelligence and were exposed to regime shifts in its scenarios; the vector came from Portfolio and was bounded against oscillation by Dynamics and by the joint move bounds in Failure.

The vector is supervised. Approval classes decide which cells move automatically and which go to review; move bounds and damping keep its paths stable; monitors and rollback plans bound its failures; and every cell can be traced to the inputs, conjectures, and rule versions that produced it. The function owns the apparatus, and named owners answer for its outputs. Autonomy in the sense of an engine that sets prices without inherited rules or human authority is not part of the specification, because each removed constraint would remove a property the earlier chapters showed to be needed.

#### Layered System

Each part supplies something the engine cannot produce for itself. Strategy supplies the economic reasoning: what the firm sells, what buyers value, how margins arise, how rivals and time shape the feasible paths, and how a founder prices well under thin data. The engine's conjectures and objective are encoded versions of that reasoning; an engine without it optimizes a proxy. Policy supplies the rules: who may decide, which instruments and segments exist, how promises bind, how competition is handled, and how the firm learns. The engine's constraints and approval classes are encoded versions of those rules; an engine without them acts with no authority behind its prices.

The engine in turn supplies what the earlier stages could not: speed, scale, consistency across thousands of cells, and a complete record. A founder's judgment does not scale past a small catalog, and written policy applied by hand does not keep pace with an environment that moves daily. The three stages are layers of one system, each executing within the bounds of the one before, rather than steps in a sequence where the later stage replaces the earlier. Black-box claims to be data-driven fail on this account, as Bootstrapping argued: a price that cannot be traced to reasoning and rules has lost its inheritance, whatever its accuracy on last quarter's data.

The inheritance also runs backward. When the engine's monitors show that a conjecture fails or a rule produces bypass, the correction is made in Policy, and where the failure is deep enough, in the strategic reasoning Policy encodes. Learning's double loop is the mechanism, and Governance decides who may revise which layer.

#### Open Limits

The account has limits that a reader should carry with the conclusions. Most of the book's illustrations use invented numbers, labeled as such, to show the form of a calculation rather than to estimate magnitudes; the magnitudes belong to each firm's own experiments and records. The external rules described in Regulation change, differ by jurisdiction, and are read here as an economist reads constraints rather than as a lawyer reads obligations. The engine's conjectures about rivals rest on observed conduct, which can shift when ownership, technology, or regulation changes, and Intelligence's scenarios bound that risk without removing it.

Some questions were left outside the account by design. The book does not treat pricing for physical goods with binding capacity in depth, auctions as a primary sales channel, or the internal transfer prices of a multidivisional firm. Each would add its own constraints to the engine without changing the layered structure the book describes, since each enters as further inputs, conjectures, or shared limits under the same supervision.

Within those limits the claim stands as argued. A technology firm can move pricing from one person's judgment to a supervised engine without losing the reasoning that made the early prices good or the authority that made later prices accountable, provided each stage is encoded, owned, and traceable in the next. The conditions are the ones the eighteen chapters stated: costs and value correctly named, rules written and owned, an engine whose every output can be explained by the people who answer for it, and integrity in the sense Value defined and Governance extended.

# Epilogue

### Decision Premises

Herbert Simon (Administrative Behavior, 1947) described an organization as a structure that supplies its members with the premises of their decisions: the goals, facts, and rules each member takes as given when choosing. The three parts of this book can be read as an account of where those premises come from in pricing and how they move as a firm grows. Strategy fixes the value premises. It states what the firm is trying to price, which costs and buyer valuations matter, which rivals and paths are relevant, and why one price structure is preferred to another. Policy converts those premises into rules and assigns authority over them, so that a decision taken by a sales lead in one market rests on the same grounds as a decision taken by a product manager in another. The engine takes the rules as given and executes them at a speed and scale no person matches, while recording which premise produced which output.

Read this way, the sequence is a reallocation of judgment rather than its replacement. Each stage narrows the set of choices the next stage has to make. A founder setting the first list price chooses among almost everything; a written segment rule chooses among the cells the rule allows; an engine chooses among the prices that its constraints, approval classes, and move bounds leave feasible. Kenneth Arrow (The Limits of Organization, 1974) argued that organizations exist in part to economize on information, routing to each decision only what that decision needs. The layered design does exactly that. The engine does not need to know why a fence exists, only that it binds; the policy owner does not need to inspect every cell, only the exceptions and the monitors that summarize the rest.

### Residual Judgment

Narrowing does not reach zero. Three kinds of judgment remain with people at every stage, and the book's architecture is built around keeping them visible rather than absorbing them into the machinery.

The first is the choice of objective. An engine optimizes what it is given, and the choice between margin now and position later, or between one segment's surplus and another's, is a strategic choice the engine inherits. When the objective is left implicit, the engine fills the gap with whatever its training data rewarded, and the firm discovers its effective strategy only by reading the outputs.

The second is the interpretation of surprise. Monitors detect that a conjecture has failed; they do not say whether the failure reflects noise, a rival's deliberate move, or a change in the buyers themselves. That diagnosis decides whether the correction belongs in a parameter, a rule, or the strategic reasoning behind the rule, and it requires the kind of judgment bounded rationality allows rather than the optimization it rules out.

The third is the standard against which the whole apparatus is held. Integrity, in the sense Value defined and Governance extended, holds when the resources a price structure consumes are directed to productive purposes, when contributions to the value it produces can be traced to those who made them, and when the firm's stated policy and its realized practice agree. None of the three conditions is a quantity the engine can maximize. Each is a property that owners assess and defend, and each can fail while every local metric improves.

### Unsettled Ground

Several questions remain open in a sense different from the scope limits already stated at the close of Enterprise. They are not omitted topics but problems the book's own framework does not resolve.

The value of the engine itself is hard to measure. A supervised engine replaces a counterfactual stream of human decisions that no one observes, and comparisons against holdout markets are confounded by the same interactions the engine is meant to manage. Firms adopt engines on the strength of throughput and consistency arguments, which are sound, while the claim that engine prices are better prices in the welfare or margin sense usually rests on thinner evidence than the adoption decision implies.

When many firms in a market run engines built on similar methods and similar public signals, their prices may move together without any agreement among them. The book has treated rivals' conduct as an input to conjectures and the external rules as constraints on the vector, but whether correlated engines change the competitive outcome, and how the rules should treat that, are questions the economics and the law have not settled.

The integrity criterion is a standard, not a test with a known threshold. Traceability can be complete in the record and still opaque to the buyer who pays the price; the agreement between policy and practice can hold for every published rule while the rules themselves become too numerous for anyone outside the firm to read. The book has argued that these conditions belong among the engine's design requirements. It has not shown how much traceability or legibility is enough, and that question will be answered, firm by firm and market by market, by the people who own the apparatus and answer for what the firm charges.
